# Shah Abdul Latif of Bhit (Sorley) — takeaways

*Source: H. T. Sorley, *Shah Abdul Latif of Bhit: His Poetry, Life and Times — A Study of Literary,
Social and Economic Conditions in Eighteenth Century Sind*. Imprint as the book's own pages print it:
Pakistan Branch, Oxford University Press, Lahore / Karachi / Dacca; Oxford University Press, Ely House,
London W.1; "First published 1940 / Reprinted 1966" (p. 3); `ISBN 0 19 636029` on the last leaf (p. 442).
442 pages, transcribed `text_layer` on 14 September 2026, chunked into 24 chunks of ~8000 words; provenance in
out/ocr/sorley_shah_abdul_latif_of_bhit/. Page references are PDF page indices; folio = the printed page
number where the notes print one, and in the body **printed page = PDF page − 9**, an offset that does
not hold in the roman-numbered front matter, which is out of sequence in this scan. **This transcription
is heavily damaged: 6,421 `[text-layer?]` flags across the 24 chunk-notes files, plus 19 `[OCR?]` and 19
`[illegible]`.** Every flag is carried inline below; nothing flagged has been repaired. Consolidated from
the 24 chunk notes on 24 September 2026.
Reviewed: no.*

## What this book is

A one-volume study by a serving British Indian Civil Service officer, part literary criticism, part
economic and social history, part verse translation, published under the imprint its own pages
print: the title page carries "PAKISTAN BRANCH / OXFORD UNIVERSITY PRESS / LAHORE KARACHI DACCA"
(p. 2) and the imprint page "Oxford University Press, Ely House, London W. 1", "First published 1940
/ Reprinted 1966", "The book is copyright under the Berne Convention" and "Printed by· offset 'at
Civil and Military Press, Karachi" (p. 3); the last leaf of the whole volume carries one line and
nothing else, "ISBN 0 19 636029" (p. 442), and there is **no colophon, no printer's imprint beyond
p. 3, no edition statement, no plates list and no list of illustrations** anywhere in the closing
chunk — all **(not stated)**. The book announces itself "In Three Books : I.   History / II.
Literature and Criticism / III.    The Risalo of Shah Abdul Latif" (p. 4), and its own part-titles
and running apparatus then call those divisions both "Book" and "Part": `BOOK .JI`[text-layer?]
(p. 206) against `I NDEX TO PARTS I AND II` in the same division's contents (p. 207), against a
separate `PART I / HISTORICAL` and `PART II / LITERARY, CRITICAL AND MISCELLANEOUS` which are
divisions of the bibliography only (p. 191, folio 182). Sorley states his object in his own words:
"The obj ect of the present work �s [text-layer?] twofold, first to introduce English readers to the
achievement of Shah Abdul Latif, and second, to explain, by reference to the historical and social
environm�nt [text-layer?] of the age in which the poems were composed, something of t:he
[text-layer?] message and meaning they convey" (p. 7, folio vii), restated as reconstructing "the
countryside in which was composed the beautiful mystical poetry" and "what sort of beings were the
people for whom these songs were written and by whom they - were sung" (p. 19, folio 10), and again,
retrospectively, at the head of the poet's own chapter: "The whole of the present work up to this
point has been written solely for the purpose of making clear the kind of people amongst whom he
lived and the historical background of their age" (p. 178, folio 169). What he translates is **not**
the whole Risalo: "I have transl�ted [text-layer?] not the complete Risalo but the abridgement known
as the Muntakhab collected by Kazi Ahmad Shah. This abridgement is- probably the best known and the
most popular collection of Shah Abdul . Latif's verse" (p. 7, folio vii), with permission from
Pokardas & Sons of Shikarpur, who hold the copyright (p. 7, folio vii); the translation is
"Re-arranged" (p. 308) and its concordance is keyed to `POkardas's`[text-layer?] edition of the
Muntakhab (p. 314), whose editor's name is printed four different ways on three consecutive pages —
`Po"kardas's`[text-layer?] (p. 314), `Pokardiis's`[text-layer?] (p. 315, folio 306), `Pokardas's`
(p. 316, folio 307) — with no place, publisher, date or edition number given anywhere
(**not stated**). He claims priority twice: "Except for a few scattered extracts no English
translation of the Risalo has yet been attempted" (p. 7, folio vii), and "I believe that this is the
first occasion on which most of the extracts relating· to the East India C<;>mpany's [text-layer?]
Factory in Sind in the eighteenth century have been published" (p. 8, folio viii). The prose is
signed "H. T. SORLEY / Bombay / March 31st, 1938 / (Revised for publication 1939)" (p. 8, folio
viii) and the verse translation separately signed `H. T. SORLEY` / `Bombay` / `March 3 rst, 1 93 8`
[text-layer?] (p. 306, folio 297) — a 1938–9 book issued in 1940 and reprinted in 1966.

**Method and sources.** The work was done in "Twelve years of leisure time", "partly carried out in
the fascinating land of Sind itself" (p. 7, folio vii), on permission "granted me .by the Government
of Bombay to examine the wealth of ·historical material in the Bombay Record Office" and on "the .
excellent library of the Bombay Branch of the Royal Asiatic Society" (p. 8, folio viii); the record
series are named in the abbreviations list — "S.I.L.B.�Secretariat Inward Letter-Book", "E� .
F.D-English Factories in India", "P.D_.D ..--:.P .. ublie Department Diary" [text-layer?],
"S.P.I),_:_Secr_ et and· Political Department" [text-layer?], "J.R.A .S.-journal of the Royal A
siatic Society" (p. 6) — and decoded again in the bibliography's `G. NOTE` (p. 191, folio 182).
Sorley sorts his evidence into "`five· classes`" — Sind chronicles; general Moghul histories; East
India Company records "(a) 1635-62, (b) 1758-75"; European travellers; and the papers of the British
occupation (p. 24, printed 15) — and condemns the first outright: "The native historians and
annalists taken as a whole must `th�refore`[text-layer?] be classed as thoroughly inadequate and
unsatisfactory" (p. 27, printed 18). **The methodological move a user must weigh before citing any
social fact in this book is stated by the author himself, repeatedly.** Direct evidence for the
poet's own period is absent — "the extreme paudty [text-layer?], indeed the almost total absence, of
reliable contemporary record of any description whatsoever for the actual years of the period
1690-1760" (p. 14, folio 5); "There is no European record for Sind between the years I662
[text-layer?] and 1758" (p. 42); "**We have no accounts of `Kalhorn`[text-layer?] days. The evidence
mostly dates from the time of the `Talpiirs` [text-layer?]**" (p. 123, folio 114) — and his remedy is
interpolation from later and earlier witnesses plus the Sind of his own day: "there is little reason
to doubt that the peoples of Sind in the first half of the eighteenth century lived more or less in
the way in which Sir Charles Napi�r [text-layer?] found them living in 1843… It is thus possible by
means of reasoned deduction and critical judgement to complete the gap that lies between the
seventeenth and the nineteenth century" (p. 19, folio 10). His own appendix on the archive confirms
the size of the hole: in the Bombay Record Office press lists there is "a. complete blank" between
Nicholas Scrivener's letter of 13 October 1657 and Mr Symmons's of 25 November 1744, and no Company
covenanted servant is listed as resident in Sind between 1712 and 1738 (p. 204, folio 195). On
himself as translator he is equally explicit: "I do not pretend that my rendering is free from
error. Indeed in many passages I am doubtful of the meaning which the poet wished to convey"; he
aims at "a literary and not a literal translation, so as to be capable of being read for its own
sake without reference to the Sindhi original" (p. 8, folio viii). And his spellings are a stated
editorial system, not damage: he declines the Royal Asiatic Society transliteration, leaves the "h",
"s", "t", "d" and z classes unmarked, omits "all the final short vowels", accepts Anglicized forms
("Thus I have accepted words like ' Sind ' and ' Sukkur ' when on a principle of formal consistency
I should have written ' Sindh ' and Sakhar '"), and — decisively — "In the numerous
qu�tations[text-layer?] from old records and from writings of past generations which I have ·given I
have almost always retained the spelling found in the originals, even though this spelling is more
often than not erroneous" (p. 203, folio 194). "Shikarpoor", "Kurrachee", "Roree", "Bukkur",
"Scinde", "Tattah", "Chandookah", "Purgunnah", "Shahbunder" are therefore his deliberate retentions.

**Reliability, as this transcription delivers it.** The text was taken by the `text_layer` route from
an OCR layer over a 1940 letterpress monograph of 442 PDF pages, and it is damaged throughout: the
dominant class is **`[text-layer?]`, 6,421 flagged tokens across the twenty-four Pass-1 notes files**
(roughly 268 per chunk), against 19 `[OCR?]` and 19 `[illegible…]`. The damage is not a
total-and-mechanical ligature substitution, so **every flagged token keeps its flag here** and the
class is inventoried once in the Doubts section. Page references in this file are PDF page indices as
the notes recorded them; **the printed page runs PDF page − 9**, an offset that holds unbroken across
the body from PDF p. 12 and is confirmed by the book's own contents pages — twenty-one entries at
p. 320 (folio 311) checked against the pages on which the sections actually open, "with no
exceptions", and both testable entries of the Part II contents at p. 342 (folio 333). **It does not
apply to the roman-numbered front matter, which is out of sequence in this scan**, and no folio is
computed here that the notes did not print. Two further cautions follow from the book's own
apparatus. First, its index prints the book's own page numbers, not PDF indices, and **never turn one
of those locators into a `(p. N)`**. Second, that index shows what the book is not: there is no
headword, under any spelling, for `urs`/`ʿurs`, `mela`, `fair`, `langar`, `sajjada` or `sajjada
nashin`, `mujawir`, `khadim`, `offerings`, `nazrana`, `chadar`, `shrine`, `dargah`, `tomb`, `mazar`,
`ziarat`, `pilgrimage`, `khanqah`, `takya`, `silsila`, `tariqa`, `Chishti`, `Qadiri`, `Naqshbandi` or
`Suhrawardi`; `Wakf` (p. 441) is the only endowment word and `Saints, (Muslim)` (p. 439) the only
saint headword. **This book does not treat shrines as institutions with custodians, revenues and
calendars. It treats saints as a privileged social class and Sufism as a literary and philosophical
tradition** — which is exactly why its cross-cutting yield, below, is fiscal and social rather than
liturgical.

## Shrines in the archive this book informs

Seven archive rows are named site-specifically in the twenty-four chunk notes, and the book is
overwhelmingly about one of them. `archive_place.md` is empty — **not one multi-id bullet exists in
the Pass-1 notes for this book**, because every one of them was written before ruling (a) landed.
The place-level and person-level material was parked in "Other saints, sites and events" and in the
Doubts hedges; it is converted into real bullets in the second half of this section.

### bhit-bhit-shah — Bhit (Bhit Shah)

The book is a monograph about this row's principal figure and nothing else has a claim on it. The
title page prints `SHAH. ABDUL                                LATIF` / `OF            BHIT:` with
the shattered display spacing of the scan (p. 2); the full title is "SHAH ABDUL LATIF OF BHIT: HIS
POETRY, LIFE AND TIMES / A Study of Literary, Social and Economic Conditions in.   - [text-layer?]
Eighteenth Century Sind" (p. 2). **The running head of every body page is the poet's name**, and it
is damaged differently on almost every page — `S H A H A B D U L L A T I F O F B H I T`
(pp. 13, 15–23), `SHAH ABDUL LAT i F OF B R IT`[text-layer?] (p. 39),
`SHAH ABDUL LATI F O F B H IT` (p. 40), `SHAH ABDUL LATI F O F BHIT` (p. 41),
`SHAH ABDUL " LA T I F OF B H IT` (p. 47, with a stray quotation mark),
`SHAH ABDUL . LAT I F OF . B H I T` (p. 49, with stray periods and no page number),
`SHAH ABDUL LATIF OF B R I T`[text-layer?] (p. 54), `S HA H ABDUL LAT I F OF B H I T`[text-layer?]
(p. 55), `SHAH ABDUL LAT l F OF B H IT`[text-layer?] (p. 59),
`S H A H A B D U L L A T iF O F B H I T`[text-layer?] (p. 64),
`SHAH ABD UL LAT I F O F B R IT`[text-layer?] (pp. 67, 71, 72),
`S H A H A B D U L LAT I F O F B H I T` (p. 68), `S H AH . A B D U L L A T I F O F B H I T`
(p. 79), `SHAH ABDUL LATIF OF B H IT` (p. 126, folio 117), `B R I T` (p. 134, folio 125),
`SHAH ABDUL LAT I F OF B H I T` "and fourteen other spacings of the same words" (pp. 110–117,
119–125), `BRIT`[text-layer?] four times in chunk 015 and four times again in chunk 022, and
`SHAH ABDUL LATi F OF B H IT` (chunk 017). The book's names for him inside the text are
"Shah Abdul Latif", "Shah Latif", "Latif", "Latif the Poet", "the Sayid", "the poet", "the Sindhi
poet", "the author of the Risalo", "A bdul[text-layer?] Latif Shah Bhitai" (p. 201, folio 192),
"Abdul Latif Shah" (p. 201, folio 192), "Shah A bdul Latif Bhitai" (p. 192, folio 183), "Abdul
Latif" (p. 356, folio 347) and, as an index headword, `SH.AH ABDUL LATIF`[text-layer?] (p. 439).
**"Bhitai" appears for the first time in chunk 013.**

**Where the material is.** Six of the twenty-four chunks contain nothing whatever about him beyond
the running head, and the notes record this as an explicit negative so no one re-reads the pages:
chunk 004 (pp. 54–66), chunk 005 (pp. 67–79), chunk 007 (pp. 95–109, "folios 86–100 of this book
are economic history with the title saint entirely absent"), chunk 008 (pp. 110–125, "**no
biographical datum about the poet at all**"), chunk 009 (pp. 126–140) and chunk 018 (pp. 276–291,
where "the village of Bhit is not named once in sixteen pages"). Chunk 003 has one sentence
(p. 44), chunk 006 one (p. 90, folio 81), chunk 011 one (p. 169, folio 160). **The biography is
concentrated in chunk 012 at PDF pp. 172–185 (folios 163–176), and the index confirms it: "(life
of) 169-176" in printed pages (p. 439), with "Bhit (Shah) · 1 71:-3[text-layer?],
1 74n[text-layer?]" (p. 433) — three printed pages and one footnote for the place.**

#### Saint

- **The composition date, given before any life-dates appear.** "The poems were composed in-the
  first half of the eighteenth .century." (p. 7, folio vii). At that point in the book "**His birth
  year, death year, lineage, teacher, order and burial are all (not stated)**".
- **His dates, given five times in four manners, and the manner differs.** Flat and parenthetical:
  "the life of Shah Abdul Latif (1689-1752)" (p. 28, printed 19). Hedged twice in the Life chapter:
  "His dates are not established with complete certainty. But they may be taken as extending from
  1689 to 1752" (p. 172); and "The exact dates of the poet's birth and death are not known. But the
  evidence is in favour of his having been born in 1689 and having died in 1752" (p. 179,
  folio 170). Then flat again, as chronology entries with **no hedge at all**: "1689 Birth of
  A bdul[text-layer?] Latif Shah Bhitai, . the author of the ' Risalo ' ." and "1752 Death of
  A bdul[text-layer?] Latif Shah at Bhit." (both p. 201, folio 192). **The years agree; the manner
  does not, and both are kept.** **No day, no month and no hijri date appears anywhere** —
  **(not stated)**.
- **His age at death**: "Shah Abdul Latif died at the age of sixty-three" (p. 180, folio 171).
- **The place of death is given as "Bhit"** (p. 201, folio 192), not "Bhit Shah" and not "Bhit Shah
  village".
- **His age fixed against a traveller instead of a birth year** — "When Captain Hamilton visited
  Laribunder in 1699 Shah Abdul Latif was a boy of about eight or nine years of age and the Kalhora
  were beginning to assert them­selves in Upper Sind" (p. 90, folio 81). **Any birth date derived
  from "eight or nine years of age" would be an inference the book does not make there.**
- **His life dated against four political events, none of them dated in the Life chapter and all
  four dated in Appendix V.** "When Aurangzeb died Shah Abdul Latif was a youth of eighteen years
  of age" (p. 179, folio 170); "He was fifty years old when Nadir Shah sacked Delhi and made Sind
  tributary to Persia"; "He was fifty-eight when Ahmad Shah Durani brought off his coup against the
  dying Delhi Empire, founded modern Afghan­istan and made Sind subject to Kabul"; and he died
  "Five years later, and six years before the second East India Company's factory was
  estab­lished at Tatta" (all p. 180, folio 171). Appendix V supplies the years: "1 707[text-layer?]
  · Death of Aurangzeb and accessfon[text-layer?] of · Bahadur Shah." (p. 201, folio 192) —
  consistent with "eighteen"; "1 739[text-layer?] Sack of Delhi by Na �ir[text-layer?] Shah."
  (p. 201, folio 192) and "1 740[text-layer?] Nadir Shah invades Sind which becomes subject to
  Persia." — consistent with "fifty"; "1748 Ahmad Sha:h[text-layer?] Dm;ani·[text-layer?] begins -
  invasion bf[text-layer?] India." and "1 750[text-layer?] Sind becomes subject to Afghan Kingdom
  of Ahmad Shah Dtirani[text-layer?]." (p. 201, folio 192) — **which does not line up with
  "fifty-eight" on either reading**; and "1 758[text-layer?] English Company's Factory established
  at Tatta." (p. 202, folio 193) — consistent with "six years before".
- **Family and descent.** "He belonged to a notable Alite Sayid family of Matiari. Hence he was the
  scion of the most illustrious religious house of Sind tracing a connexion to Herat" (p. 180,
  folio 171); "It is true that Shah Abdul . Latif came of a .well-known family of Sayids and that
  down the centuries Sayids in Sind have produced a number of men famous for their learning and
  their saintliness" (p. 212, folio 203). The index classifies him under `Sayids` as "(Shah Latif)
  1 7 1[text-layer?]" (p. 439) and prints the family form "Alite[text-layer?] Sayids,·
  f7I[text-layer?]" (p. 432). **"Alite Sayid" is the only affiliation term the book uses of him.
  No silsila, no pir, no bai'at and no spiritual genealogy of any kind is given anywhere in the
  book** — **(not stated)** — and this is recorded independently in chunks 001, 012, 014, 016,
  018 and 024.
- **His ancestor four generations back**: "His ancestor in the fourth generation before him was the
  famous Sayid Abdul Karim {floruit[text-layer?] circa A.D. 1600) whose durgah was at Bulri"
  (pp. 180–181, folios 171–172), restated as "The poet's great-great-grandfather was the Shah Karim
  whose name is still held in reverence in Lower Sind" (p. 212, folio 203). **The two statements of
  the degree agree.** See `shrine-of-shah-abdul-karim-bulri` below.
- **His father, named three different ways in three places.** "the poet's father, Sayid Habib Shah,
  was living at a place called Hala Haweli" (p. 181, folio 172); "his father, Shah Habib, went to
  live in a nearhy[text-layer?] village called Kotri" (p. 181, folio 172); and, in a footnote,
  "His father Habib Khan died in 1 742[text-layer?] and was buried in Bhit a few paces from where
  later in 1 754[text-layer?] Shah Abdul Latif's own tomb was erected" (p. 183, folio 174,
  footnote 1). **The father's burial at Bhit, "a few paces" from the poet's tomb, is stated only in
  that footnote.** The index carries him as "Sayid Habib Shah (Habib Khan) 172, 1 74n[text-layer?]"
  (p. 439), which confirms the two name-forms as the book's own.
- **His marriage and his childlessness.** "A friend of his younger days was one Mirza Moghul Beg,
  who, in 1713, died an untimely death at the hands of robbers and whose daughter he eventually
  married" (p. 182, folio 173) — **1713 is the only firm date in the chunk for an event in his
  adult life**. "He left no heir, and the oral tradition, which in this respect there is no reason
  to disbelieve, records the decorum and dignity of his family life" (p. 181, folio 172). **His
  wife is never named** — **(not stated)** — and is referred to only as "the Shah's wife" (p. 184,
  folio 175).
- **His three residences, in order.** "He spent nearly all his life living in a very restricted
  locality in the northern part of what is now the Hyderabad district. In that locality he resided
  successively in villages called Hala Haweli, Kotri and Bhit Shah" (p. 180, folio 171). Hala
  Haweli was "now in ruins, not far from Matiari" at the time of writing (p. 181, folio 172); Kotri
  "like Hala Haweli, is also in ruins Joday[text-layer?]" (p. 181, folio 172). The index confirms
  the cluster: "Hala Haweli, I7I[text-layer?], I 72[text-layer?]" (p. 435),
  `"Kotri`[text-layer?] "1 7 1[text-layer?], 1 72[text-layer?], 246" (p. 436), "Matiari, 158,
  171-3, 209, 2 16[text-layer?]" (p. 437), "Hala, I 73[text-layer?]. (old Hala) 42" (p. 435),
  "Bulri, 172" (p. 433). **"Hala Haweli" is a separate, ruined village and must not be conflated
  with Hala.**
- **His education, argued at length and then contradicted.** "How far Shah Abdul Latif was an
  educated man has proved a great puzzle to scholars. The popular tradition is that he had no
  regular education but taught himself everything . Popular tradition in this respect is, however,
  unreliable. His poems show clearly an acquaintance with Arabic and Persian far beyond the
  ordinary accomplishments of his time. It is certain that he was familiar with the work of
  Jalaluddin RU.mi[text-layer?]" (p. 183, folio 174), with the conclusion "it is clear that his
  education was neither superficial nor contemptible" (p. 183, folio 174). **Against that, in
  chunk 014**: "It is quite certain that Shah Abdul Latif was not a . lea:rned[text-layer?]
  mari[text-layer?], _ in the narrow scholastic sense. Nor should _ we be justified in ·assuming
  that he had more than a smattering of t'he[text-layer?] higher educ_ation[text-layer?] of his
  day" (p. 224, folio 215) — while in the same passage "he must have picke,d'[text-layer?]
  tip[text-layer?] enough · Arabic and Persian to be 'able to· realize better th�n[text-layer?] his
  teachers the spirit of the Muslim theosophy preached by the great\[text-layer?] vdters[text-layer?]
  of Persia" (p. 224, folio 215). **The two verdicts are kept side by side.**
- **A named teacher, and the name is printed two ways on the same page.** "Very little is known of
  the education of Shah Abdul Latif. One of his preceptors was a Niir Muhammad Bhatti of whose
  scholastic ability we are singularly ignorant" (p. 212, folio 203), with Lilaram Watanmal quoted
  for the gap: "' We do not know ', says Lilaram Watanmal, ' how long our poet studied with Niir
  Mahomad or how much he learnt from him.'" (p. 212, folio 203, footnote 2 citing "Shah Latif,
  p. I I[text-layer?] ."). **"Niir Muhammad Bhatti" and "Niir Mahomad" on one page, and neither is
  to be merged with Nur Muhammad Kalhoro.** **No madressa, no place of study and no dates of study**
  — **(not stated)**.
- **His three constant books, given twice with a changed adverb and once narrowed to one.** "the
  Koran, the Masnawi of Jalaluddin RU.mi[text-layer?], and the Sindhi verses of Shah Karim were
  **always** in the poet's hands" (p. 183, folio 174, footnote 3 citing "Shah Latif by Lilaram
  Watanmal, p. I I[text-layer?]."); "Tradition says that the Koran, the Masnawi of Jalaluddin
  Riimi[text-layer?] and the Sindhi verses of Shah Karim were **constantly** in his hands"
  (pp. 212–213, folios 203–204), to which Sorley adds "The general truth of this assertion need not
  be doubted. But though it may explain some of the characteristic literary and scholarly qualities
  of the Risalo, it does nothing to account for the poetic excellence of the verses" (p. 213,
  folio 204). **Chunk 016 narrows the list to one book and adds a hedge**: "To what extent Shah
  Abdul Latif was acquainted with the great Persian poets we do not know. There is no evidence that
  he had studied any of them except Jalaluddin Riimi[text-layer?], a copy of whose ' Masnawi ' was
  said to be constantly in his hands" (p. 251, folio 242).
- **The Masnawi as a gift, and the political quarrel it settled.** "The independent ways of the
  young ascetic and contemplative were certain to arouse the jealousy of the orthodox ' hierarchy ',
  especially as the fame of the young man with his saintly manner of living was becoming known and
  was attracting to him a number of murids. At this stage of his life, therefore, he found himself
  ranged against the Sayids of Matiari who succeeded for a time in enlisting the support of
  Niir[text-layer?] Muhammad Kalh6ro[text-layer?], then the most powerful man in Sind" (p. 182,
  folio 173); Sorley's explanation, "The Kalh6ra[text-layer?] … were greatly influenced by the
  Sayids and the holy men whorn[text-layer?] they dared not antagonize. This fact must explain the
  early hostility of Niir[text-layer?] Muhammad Kalh6ro[text-layer?] to a young religious leader of
  :tndependent[text-layer?] and unorthodox views" (p. 182, folio 173). The reconciliation:
  "Niir[text-layer?] Muhammad Kalhorn[text-layer?], however, became later convinced of the
  sincerity and worth of this remarkable young man and attached himself to him in friendship"
  (p. 182, folio 173); "it is related that Nur Muhammad Kalhorn[text-layer?], from whom he had
  become estranged, won back the poet's favour by presenting him with a fine copy of the Masnawi"
  (p. 183, folio 174). **This is a documented case of murid recruitment producing a political
  quarrel, and of a manuscript settling it.**
- **His sectarian position, in his own reported words and Sorley's verdict on top of it.** "It is
  stated of Shah Abdul Latif that, when questioned whether he was a Sunni or a Shia, he replied
  that he was between Sunni and Shia" (p. 175, folio 166, footnote 1 citing "Gurbuxani : Shah jo
  Risa..lo[text-layer?], I, p. 50."). Sorley adds immediately: "In reality he was more a Shia than
  a Sunni, because he intended at a later stage of his life to make the pilgrimage not to Mecca but
  to Kerbela" (p. 175, folio 166). **The second sentence is Sorley's inference, not the poet's
  statement, and the two must travel together.**
- **The abandoned Kerbela pilgrimage, vetoed by his own followers.** "It �s[text-layer?] said that
  he had intended to make· the pilgrimage to Kerbela in the last few years of his life, but was
  dissuaded from doing so by the insistence of his followers that he should not desert them.
  Instead of going to Kerbela Shah Abdul Latif is said to have composed the Sur Kedaro which deals
  with the story of Hassan and Husain" (p. 183, folio 174). **The murids' veto on their pir's
  travel is itself an institutional fact.** Mecca and Kerbela are named in the book only as the
  alternative destinations of this journey he did not make (pp. 175, 183; folios 166, 174).
- **His travels, and Sorley's refusal of the fullest account.** "For a person of that age he was
  well travelled. It is true that he did not travel far, but he visited some of the lands adjoining
  Sind and traversed a great part of Sind in his quest for religious truth" (pp. 181–182,
  folios 172–173). **Against that, chunk 016**: "Shah Abdul Latif was a man of Lower Sind. He spent
  most of his life there. But he had travelled wid-ely[text-layer?] for his day and must have been
  familiar with Baliichi[text-layer?] custom and ways of life. Besides, in the Kohistan and Kotri
  localities not far from his own residence there must have been many Mekranis and other
  inhabitants of the southern Baliich[text-layer?] country" (p. 255, folio 246) — **which makes
  Kotri a neighbouring locality, where chunk 012 made it one of his three residences.** Gurbuxani's
  much longer itinerary is quoted and rejected in a footnote: "Gurbuxani says that he visited the
  places of pilgrimage in Sind and Lakhpat, Gimar[text-layer?], J aisalmir[text-layer?], the hills
  on the western borders of Sind, La.hut[text-layer?], Lamakan, Hinglaj, Sabar Sakhi and even went
  to KabuJ[text-layer?] and Kandahar. But I do not know on what authoritv[text-layer?] he holds
  that Shah Abdul Latif went so far. Shah jo Risalo, Gurbuxani's edition, I, p. 2 r[text-layer?]."
  (p. 182, folio 173, footnote 1).
- **His character, as the book sums it up.** "a man of quiet and unassuming disposition"; "a
  gentleness of manner and speech, an innate gentlemanliness, a bent of kindness, compassion and
  generosity"; "He is said to have hated cruelty and to have been unable to bear to beat an animal
  or cause physical pain to man or beast"; "In an age and amongst a population that set little
  store by chastity, he exhibited towards women a self-control that was remarkable" (pp. 180–181,
  folios 171–172).
- **His appearance, from oral tradition** — "the poet in his prime was a well-set-up, handsome,
  bearded man of average height with fine black eyes, an intelligent face, a noble forehead and the
  solemn look of a deep-thinking and penetrating mind overcast with thought" (p. 181, folio 172).
- **His social position at birth** — "The poet was, therefore, born to a position of dignity and
  power as one of the privileged class treated with the superstitious respect described in a
  previous chapter. He was, as an Englishman might say, born with a silver spoon in his mouth"
  (p. 181, folio 172).
- **The career, described as a three-stage ascent ending in sainthood** — "The poet's own life
  displays the successive stages he went through from poet to sage, ending finally in sainthood"
  (p. 214, folio 205). **This is the book's only statement of how he became a saint, and it gives
  no event, no date and no agency** — **(not stated)**.
- **His last years, and his audiences in life.** "The last years of his life were spent in a halo
  of sanctity in which the reverence of his followers for him played an important part. Thousands
  would come to listen to the magic of his poetry and to enjoy the beauty of its message" (p. 183,
  folio 174). **"Thousands" is the only crowd figure the book gives for his lifetime.**
- **His death scene** — "It is recorded that shortly before his death he retired into solitude.
  Coming out again he performed his ablutions, put on a white sheet and ordered the singing of
  songs. When the music stopped,, he fell into a reverie of divine contemplation" (p. 183,
  folio 174, footnote 2 citing "Sharah Latlfi[text-layer?], Part I, p. 7."). **The book does not
  say that he died in that state**, only that he "fell into a reverie of divine contemplation". The
  source is identified in the bibliography as "Mahmud Buksh Wasif. Sharaha[text-layer?]
  Latiji[text-layer?]. 1925." (p. 192, folio 183).
- **Named companions, secretaries and friends.** "The amanuenses whose help was invoked to record
  the poems as delivered were named Tamar and Hashim. A great friend of the poet was called Bilal :
  another was called Inayat, himself a poet, soine[text-layer?] of whose verses are inserted or
  quoted in the Risa.Io[text-layer?] as it exists today" (p. 184, folio 175); Tamar and Hashim are
  elsewhere "the poet's favourite disciples and secretaries" (p. 184, folio 175). Indexed as
  "Hashim, I 75[text-layer?]" and "Tamar, 1 75[text-layer?]" (pp. 435, 441). **On "Inayat" see the
  second section below and the Doubts list — two archive rows carry the name and the book
  identifies neither.**
- **He stood at a dynastic hinge, stated three times.** "He stood at the parting of the ways
  between the rule of the Moghuls and that of the Kalhora. He witnessed himself the transition from
  the Sind of the Delhi Empire to the Sind of the struggling indepen­dence" (p. 172); "His life
  coincided almost exactly with the change from Moghul to Kalh6ro[text-layer?] rule in Sind"
  (p. 179, folio 170); "He is the i;-eal[text-layer?] jewel of the Kalh6ro[text-layer?] age"
  (p. 178, folio 169). Restated as literary history: "Shah Abdul Latif was born at a time when a
  great transition was beginning. This transition continued throughout his lifetime. The supreme
  territorial authority of the Muslim dynasties was breaking up and the settled structure
  o.f[text-layer?] a medieval society was wearing a little thin" (p. 214, folio 205); "Shah Abdul
  Latif is the last of the great medieval poets" (p. 214, folio 205). The index makes the
  relationship explicit under the Kalhora entry — "(contemporaries of Shah Abdul Latif) 170,
  I 7 I[text-layer?], 224" (p. 436).
- **The documentary record for his lifetime is declared empty.** "It is, however, exceedingly
  unfortunate that `tpe`[text-layer?] period covered by the life of Shah Abdul Latif (1689-1752) is
  totally devoid of any reference to Sind in the East India Company's records. The position is that
  there is evidence of the generation before the poet's birth, and there is evidence of the events
  that happened within a decade after his death, but I have been able to trace in the Bombay Record
  Office only one `ref�rence`[text-layer?] to any event in Sind occurring during the life of the
  poet and that event is a trivial one." (pp. 28–29, printed 19–20). The footnote to that single
  reference is printed `S.I.L.B. , No. 4 of 1743-4, p . 1 9 1 .`[text-layer?] (p. 29, printed 20).
  One traveller is said to overlap: of Withington, Manrique, Manucci and "Captain Alexander
  Hamilton in 1699", "The last of these four is the only one who has recorded his impressions of
  Sind during the period of Shah Abdul Latif's lifetime" (p. 29, printed 20), repeated as "He is
  the only European traveller whose narrative is actually contemporary with the life of Shah Abdul
  Latif" (p. 90, folio 81).
- **The book's period of study is built around him but is not his lifespan** — "the period 1690-1760
  with which this book is chiefly concerned" (p. 24, printed 15), "the times of Shah Abdul Latif,
  which cover the period 1690-1760" (p. 24, printed 15), "the Sind of the period
  `r69<?-r760`[text-layer?]" (p. 27, printed 18). One ruler is dated by him: "the Sind of Ghulam
  Shah `Kalh6ro`[text-layer?], the prince who reigned about the end of the lifetime of Shah Abdul
  Latif" (p. 30, printed 21). His grandfather is dated by the first English factory: of the factory
  at Tatta of 1635–62, "This period was contemporaneous with the lifetime of Shah Abdul Latif'�
  [text-layer?] grand-father, and coincided with the epoch of the Moghul's greatest magnificence"
  (p. 44, printed 35) — **the grandfather is not named and his dates are (not stated)**.

#### Shrine / Darbar

- **The founding of Bhit, with the meaning of the name.** "he cut himself off from the old ties and
  founded a village of his own at a place called Bhit (sandhill) , so called from its being built
  on sandhills, in the true ascetic spirit of the religious eremite" (p. 182, folio 173). **No
  foundation year is given** — **(not stated)**. "It was characteristic of the man
  thal[text-layer?] he helped to build the village with his own hands" (p. 182, folio 173).
- **The topography of Bhit, credited to others.** "Bhit itself, though unattractive as a residence,
  was set in the midst of striking scenery. It was close to the Kirar lake which was fed by the Ali
  Ganj or Pahar canal.. There were several small sheets of water near by with trees and a certain
  richness of greenery not common in the arid land of Sind except in the immediate vicinity of the
  river or its large riverain branches" (p. 182, folio 173), prefaced by "Mirza Kalich Beg and
  Gurbuxani give some account of the topography of Bhit as it was in Shah Abdul Latif's time"
  (p. 182, folio 173). The lake is indexed in its own right: "Kirar (lake) 1 73[text-layer?],
  I 75[text-layer?]" (p. 436).
- **The relics kept at Bhit, itemised.** "At Bhit are still preserved several relics of him ; the
  l�mg[text-layer?] stick he used to use when walking, his turban, a piece of cloth he wore, and
  the beggar's bowl of coconut husk from which he ate and drank frugally" (p. 181, folio 172).
  **Four relics; no custodian, no reliquary and no display practice named** — **(not stated)**.
- **The tomb: its patron, its date, its cost and its ornament.** "An imposing tomb was built for his
  body by Ghulam Shah Kalh6ro[text-layer?] in 1754, according to the tradition, in the year · that
  Niir[text-layer?] Muhammad Kalh6ro[text-layer?] died" (p. 183, folio 174), at "the expense of
  Ghulam Shah Kalhorn[text-layer?]" (p. 183, folio 174, footnote 1). Restated: "The fine mausoleum
  built for the poet's body by Ghulam Shah Kalhorn[text-layer?] was ornamented by a well-known
  mason of the time, one Idan from Sukkur." (p. 185, folio 176). **Idan is the only named craftsman
  in the book.** The index records the relationship as such: "Ghulam Shah Kalhoro, … (patron of
  Shah Abdul Latif) I 73[text-layer?]. I 74[text-layer?] and n, I76[text-layer?]" (p. 434), and
  gives **no endowment, no grant and no sum** — **(not stated)**.
- **Two dating problems inside the tomb passage, both kept.** (i) **The building is dated two years
  after the death the book gives the poet (1752 against 1754), and the book does not comment on the
  gap.** (ii) Appendix V dates Ghulam Shah's ascendancy to "1 757-8[text-layer?] Commotion and
  civil war in Sind ending in ascen­dancy of Ghulam Shah Kalhoro." (p. 201, folio 192) and his
  death to "1771 Death of Ghulam Shah Kalhorn[text-layer?]." (p. 202, folio 193) — **so the patron
  is shown paying for the tomb three or four years before he became paramount.** Nur Muhammad's
  1754 death is independently confirmed and given a cause in Appendix V: ". 1 754[text-layer?]
  Ahmad Shah Durani invades Sind : · flight and death of Niir[text-layer?] Muhammad Kalhoro."
  (p. 201, folio 192).
- **His father's grave is beside the tomb** — "buried in Bhit a few paces from where later in
  1 754[text-layer?] Shah Abdul Latif's own tomb was erected" (p. 183, folio 174, footnote 1).
- **The mausoleum became a shrine, and had a courtyard.** "the mausoleum which has become a kind of
  holy shrine which still draws its crowds" (p. 185, folio 176); "the wide courtyard where I kept
  my memorable vigil" (p. 185, folio 176, footnote 2). **These two phrases are the whole of the
  book's physical description of the shrine as a building.**
- **The index's entry for the place is three printed pages and one footnote** — "Bhit (Shah) ·
  1 71:-3[text-layer?], 1 74n[text-layer?]" (p. 433), and **it gives no shrine, no tomb, no ʿurs,
  no custodian and no building date of its own** — **(not stated)**. The colon in
  `1 71:-3`[text-layer?] is damage inside the page range.
- **What is absent, recorded so no one hunts for it.** **No ʿurs at Bhit is mentioned anywhere in
  the book; no sajjada nashin; no langar; no endowment, waqf or grant; no offerings at Bhit; no
  fair or mela at Bhit; no attendance figure after 1882; no custodian of any kind** — all
  **(not stated)**. Chunks 001, 003, 004, 005, 007, 008, 009, 010, 011, 015, 017, 018 and 020 each
  record the absence independently. **The only ʿurs named in the whole book is Shah Abdul Karim's
  at Bulri.**

#### Practices & Events

- **The weekly Friday recitation — the book's central statement about living practice at Bhit.**
  "Since the poet's death the poems have been recited weekly on Fridays in the mausoleum which has
  become a kind of holy shrine which still draws its crowds" (p. 185, folio 176). The book calls
  the occasions "' wakes '" (p. 185, folio 176).
- **An eyewitness account of one wake, dated 1882, with a crowd figure.** "Dayaram Gidumal
  describes his visit in 1882 to one of these ' wakes '. He says, . ' The deepest silence
  occasionally broken by a hearty " Allahu " prevailed in the wide courtyard where I kept my
  memorable vigil with more than a hundred men, women and children. The subdued and tranquil look
  of these people�their[text-layer?] simple habits-their gentle demeanour­their strong faith and
  their erect but humble attitude of mind made a very great impression upon me and inspired me with
  a most profound veneration and admiration for the poet saint who was their recognized spiritual
  teacher and whose burning words and breathing thoughts moved them to tears. '" (p. 185,
  folio 176, footnote 2 citing "ibid. , p. 3."). The source is dated by the bibliography: "Sigma
  (Dayaram Gidumal) . Something abo1tt[text-layer?] Sind. 1882." (p. 192, folio 183) — **the book
  and the visit are contemporaneous**.
- **The poems were composed to be performed, not read, and the practice is stated to continue.**
  "The poems were all originally intended to be recited or sung to a musical accompaniment. In
  essence, therefore,_ they are lyrical." (p. 233, folio 224); "They were originally. composed to
  be recited, . intoned 9r[text-layer?] sung to a musical accompaniment. This close connexion. with
  music they still retain" (p. 226, folio 217) — **three performance modes named, recited, intoned,
  sung**; "Tp.e[text-layer?] music to which the poems were sung was essentially Indian, that
  js[text-layer?] to say� Hindu[text-layer?] in origin, as we know from the names of the tunes to
  which the poems were and still are sung" (p. 246, folio 237) — **but the tune-names themselves
  are not printed** — **(not stated)**; "the Risalo is not a treasure house of graphic detail of
  matters well known to the populace for whom the songs were sung" (p. 265, folio 256). At the
  book's earliest page the same claim is made of the poems' reception: they "have not yet been
  divorced from their origin in spontaneous music and natural recitative" (p. 7, folio vii).
- **The poems reach the illiterate as music, not as doctrine.** "That the refined and idealized
  religion of a man like Shah Abdul Latif was really typical of the populace at large cannot be
  true. The esoteric meaning of mystical poetry does not come to an unlettered multitude. **But
  failure to reach this level of understanding does not mean that the poems as musical compositions
  did not make their appeal or that they failed to illuminate the beliefs of the common man.**"
  (p. 303, folio 294). And from the translator's preface: "He knew throughout that his hearers were
  familiar with its implications and allusions" (p. 306, folio 297) — **"hearers", not readers**;
  "him who listens to the poetry in which Sufism is declared" (p. 297, folio 288).
- **The performance conditions are said to be gone.** "In the days of the Shah jo Risalo this
  demoralization of musical taste ha�[text-layer?] not se�[text-layer?] in. It was still possible
  to write poetry with the assurance' th�t[text-layer?] it would be accompanied musically in the
  ,manner intended by the poet. The minstrel was still a person commanding respect; despite his low
  social position." (p. 228, folio 219). **A dated decline asserted with no date given** —
  **(not stated)**.
- **The Risalo is arranged by musical mode, and the modes are deified Indian forms.** "The poems of
  the Risalo are arranged according to tJ+eir[text-layer?] musical settings, though not all of them
  are named. after the tU:ne·s-[text-layer?] to which they are sung." (p. 229, folio 220); "They
  are collected under the various modes or styles of musical accompaniment to which they are sung."
  (p. 235, folio 226); "The poems of the Risalo are set to rags and raginis of the generic types
  described. These types have of course many local variations. Many of the modal raginis to which
  some of the surs are sung are Sindhi variants of Hindustani generic forms." (p. 231, folio 222);
  "The poems of the Risalo are all set to melodic forms of . this rigid character." (p. 231,
  folio 222). For the subject-named exceptions: "even in these cases the musical accompaniment
  will· be found to fall into some melodic form suitable to the type of subject ·matter, the time of
  the year or the time of day ' when the song should be sung." (p. 232, folio 223) — **performance
  is calendared and clocked even for the poems not named after modes.**
- **The music is Hindu and the thought Islamic, and the book makes that the reason for his reach.**
  "Most of the musicians in Sind have been Hindus and not Mussulmans. Possibly in this circumstance
  may be found another reason why the Islamic poetry of Shah Abdul Latif exercises so strong a
  spell over the non-Muslim inhabitants of the land. While the thought . may be Islamic, the
  musical forms in which the poems are sung are part of the Hindu heritage of India." (p. 231,
  folio 222); "While - th.e poeii;y[text-layer?] of 'Shah _Abdul Latif is typically . Muslim in
  sentiment and e·xpfession,'[text-layer?]. the niusica,l[text-layer?] ,foundation. owes little :
  to Islam" (p. 229, folio 220); and the reason he chose them — "the Sindhi poet, in using the
  musical accompariiment�[text-layer?] to emphasize the .meaning of his verse, employed the musical
  forms of India, which were the most · suitable vehicles for his purpose because' they were
  fodigenous[text-layer?] a;nd[text-layer?] understood · by all classes of the populace." (p. 230,
  folio 221). "Without an understanding of it the full force of the poetry cannot be apprehended."
  (p. 229, folio 220)
- **Music as a defect, in the same book.** "the jerkiness that arises from the Indian musical
  accompaniment with which the poetry is inseparably associated" (p. 259, folio 250); "Indian
  poetry has suffered from its too close connexion with music. There is of course an intimate
  relation between poetry and music, but the field of the two arts is neither co-extensive nor
  conterminous" (p. 260, folio 251).
- **The instruments the book names for village music, and the one it names for the shrine's are
  different.** For village music: "pipes, drums and cymbals, and sometimes … the ektar and sarangi,
  in which many Sindhis are very proficient" (p. 149, folio 140), plus "the drumming with the hand
  on the ' dilo ' (large earthenware `j ar[text-layer?]`) from which expert performers are capable
  of producing a vast variety of rhythmic beats of differing timbre" (p. 149, folio 140).
  **Sorley does not link either list to the poet or to his shrine's music.** Inside the verse the
  instruments named are the fiddle (pp. 338, 353; folios 329, 344, the latter "de.eked[text-layer?]
  . witp.[text-layer?] a. stringing / Of tassels �nd·[text-layer?] rattles"), "his lute and zither
  string" (p. 354, folio 345), "thy-:pipe's tune" (p. 354, folio 345), the kettledrums which the
  poem orders broken up (p. 339, folio 330), and "Violas, fiddles, violins / And drums the happy
  people played" on the occasion of the rain (p. 344, folio 335).
- **The one named composition tied to a calendar festival** — "The beautiful song of Shah Latif's
  called the ' Cotton Spinner ' is written round a background of . id." (p. 145, folio 136), the
  stray full stop before "id" as printed, arriving inside a paragraph about festival spending

  The festivals meant are given in the surrounding sentence: "the chief festivals of the

  Bakri, of the former, and `Hc'>li[text-layer?]` and Diwali of the latter" (p. 145, folio 136).
  **The book does not say which of the two ids the "Cotton Spinner" is set at** — **(not stated)**.

- **Muharram, the one fully specified ritual practice inside the verse.** "Muhurram's holy month is

  rio[text-layer?] . Imams. are · here. / 0 God, . in kindness let ine[text-layer?] be /
  Medinaps[text-layer?] · ruler near."; "· Medina's lords did hie them forth : / But ·they did not

- **Tomb visitation prescribed inside the verse, with an act and an expected result — the single

  folio 354). **The saint whose own tomb is this archive row is printed condemning pir-worship,
  below only idol-worship and beside self-worship. Sorley offers no commentary, no qualification
  and no context** — **(not stated)**. **The frame must travel with it: the passage stands in the
  Jogis section and the surrounding stanzas describe what the Adesis hold; no sur is named for
  either printing** — **(not stated)**.
- **The twelve-day calendar of ascetic practice in `V. THE HOLY MEN (II)` — the most systematic

  place", "Ever Ram 'doth stay / WithD;i[text-layer?] their being's · self. . Fpr[text-layer?]

  Day 9, "vigils Ht[text-layer?] their eyes… These are ascetics' holy signs" (p. 360, folio 351).
  Day 10, **a named rite on a named day** — "Tenth day's anointing came with · holy oil… /

  **Day 11, the single most important line in the translation for this archive** — "There was

  petitions attached, an honour gained, a return, and a guru met at the end. No shrine is named, no
  place, no date, no fair, no offering and no custodian** — all **(not stated)**.

- **Pilgrimage as a figure, and the interior journey set against the physical one.** "Mine eyes
  have made a feast… It is: as if life, body, soul / Had- gone . on pilgrimage… / They saw and

  folio 370); "Why go to Wankar, if not here / Thou searchest thy beloved to see ?" (pp. 295, 380;
  folios 286, 371); "Go with thine heart towards thy love. / Cease, Sasui, wanderings of thy feet.
  / Ask not the sand how lies the path. / **To travel soul-fully is meet.**" (p. 380, folio 371);

  from their mind" (p. 380, folio 371). For travel: "Who go to Windar, let them gird their loins"
  (p. 383, folio 374); "Gird up thy loins, 0 suffering one, / This time, all times. For Kech is
  far." (p. 395, folio 386); and the Lahut journey — "**The holy mendicants pass on their way to
  Lahut over the waste of sand.**" (p. 294, folio 285). **The book does not reconcile them.**
- **Mourning as performance, reproached inside the verse.** "Why do they on mat of mourning for sad
  folk prostrate them so ? / They . are not by wounds affiicte'd[text-layer?] such as touch the
  inmost . heart. / But they make pretence of grieving. With a self-regarding'. art / Not an
  angu�shed[text-layer?] cry they utter for my love." (p. 377, folio 368); "Some wring their hands
  aimlessly : true mourners wail." (p. 395, folio 386). **"mat of mourning" and the prostration on
  it are named as an observable practice, and the poem's charge is that they are being performed
  without feeling.**
- **Burial in the right ground, the clearest statement of burial-place devotion inside the
  Risalo.** "My corse, Lord, convey / To my home that in graveyard of Marus / In fixed peace I may
  stay. / Perchance in my death I'd be living, / Were my corse in Malir." (p. 428, folio 419).
  **A request for the body to be carried home for burial in a named community's graveyard, and a
  claim that burial in the right ground is a kind of continued life. No shrine, tomb, saint or
  observance is attached to it** — **(not stated)**. The couplet that follows immediately is a
  seasonal meeting: "At the times when rain falls I have trystings / With the friends that I love."
  (p. 428, folio 419) — **the book does not call it a fair, a mela or an ʿurs and names no place
  for it** — **(not stated)**.
- **The architecture of approach, inside the verse and attached to no building.** "They stand before
  Beloved' s door / And daily tears of anguish weep" (p. 325, folio 316); "It doth not make thee
  loverwise / To hide behind the screen and peep" and "0 mother ! if thou hast not shut / The
  peeping crannies where folk peer" (p. 326, folio 317); "0 lovers ! sit by loved one's path, / Nor
  weary from Friend's lattice go" (p. 326, folio 317); "Thy body in Beloved's yard / Thou hast not
  mortified nor marred" (p. 326, folio 317); "W�th[text-layer?] a smile he comes to my courtyard
  where I long for him" (p. 335, folio 326); "I cleanse the courtyard for their sake" (p. 373,
  folio 364); "No door like Thine is. I have seen / Of doors my round completely" (pp. 254, 373;
  folios 245, 364) — **a completed round of doors**; "they who search are who behold the courtyard
  of Beloved's home" (p. 395, folio 386). **A door, a screen, a lattice, peeping crannies, a
  courtyard and a path sat beside — and the book attaches none of it to a building** —
  **(not stated)**.
- **The footprint venerated, in a verse image.** "In gracious emergence when walks the Belov'd /
  E'en earth itself sings : / ' In God's name ' : and lo ! on the tracks of his feet / Are the
  r9ad'[text-layer?] s kisses planted. / The houris astonied stand by in respect." (pp. 338–339,
  folios 329–330; also p. 236, folio 227). **The nearest thing in the book to a footprint cult, and
  it is figurative: no qadam, no site and no observance is named** — **(not stated)**.

#### Legends & Miracles (karamat)

Sorley prefaces the whole category with a dismissal and then prints it anyway: "Many stories of the
miraculous order are told of him. But no serious-minded person will attach any value to them."
(p. 180, folio 171). Every attribution below is his.

- **The letter Alif.** "One typical story of this kind is that when he was being taught the alphabet
  he would not go beyond the letter Alif, which is the symbol of God and unity. The moral drawn from
  this is, of course, that from the days of his childhood he was engrossed by a vision of the divine
  unity." (p. 180, folio 171).
- **The Risalo thrown into the Kirar lake and rewritten from memory.** "When Tamar and Hashim, the
  poet's favourite disciples and secretaries, brought him the complete Risa.Io[text-layer?], Shah
  Abdul Latif turned over the pages and remarked of the frequent laments of Sasui that the work was
  simply a safety valve for the peccant humours of the flesh and flung it suddenly into the Kirar
  lake. His secretaries expostulated with him and he allowed them to rewrite the whole from memory."
  (p. 184, folio 175, attributed to "Dayaram GidumaP[text-layer?]" and footnoted to "Dayaram
  Gidumal : Something about Sind by Sigma, p. 41."). Sorley: "it can be no more than a stupid
  magnification of some trivial incident. But it is more likely to be a legend intended to build up
  the tradition of the inspired delivery of the poems."
- **The curse that explains his childlessness.** "' It is said that the Shah's wife while
  enceirite[text-layer?] longed one day for a certain kind of fish and ·her maid sent one of his
  followers to procure it. The Shah happened to enquire about this follower and learning the cause
  of his absence observed : ' When the embryo is such a trouble to my fakirs, what will the full
  blown adult be ? May such a blossom be nipped in the bud ! The child, we are told, in consequence
  was still-born to the great grief of the mother and the Shah left no heir surviving him. '"
  (pp. 184–185, folios 175–176, footnoted to "Dayaram Gidumal : Something about Sind by Sigma,
  p. 42."). Sorley: "Clearly the obj ect of this fairy tale is to account for the poet's
  childlessness." (p. 185, folio 176).
- **The blessing that produced a Kalhora heir.** "Indeed tradition likes to add that the birth of
  Ghulam Shah Kalh6ro[text-layer?] was the result of the blessing of Shah Abdul Latif." (p. 182,
  folio 173). **Attributed to "tradition"; Sorley marks it with "likes to add".** This is the only
  karamat in the book that connects the poet directly to the man who built his tomb.
- **Marvels inside the verse, attributed by the text to the poem and not to a holy person.** Iron
  turned to gold at a touch — "If .Thou .but touch this iron ' me ', gold I should be by reason"
  (p. 338, folio 329); the Prophet bearing the loads of all who ask (p. 339, folio 330); the
  lightning crossing the Prophet's tomb and filling the stream (p. 343, folio 334); the Angel
  within "two bows' distance" of God, attributed only to "the Holy Word" with **no chapter and no
  verse** — **(not stated)** (p. 340, folio 331); the Lord decreeing "An exceHent[text-layer?]
  landing-place" for a fare-less ship (p. 330, folio 321); "' Love came himself, the cable sought /
  And pushed the boat from the land.'" (p. 328, folio 319); the martyrs "garlanded and crowned …
  By maids of Paradis�.·[text-layer?]" (p. 349, folio 340); three orders of creation mourning them
  — "Men in i;heir[text-layer?] homes, · beasts in the wild / And angels .in · the sky" (p. 349,
  folio 340); Hur's blood dyeing beard and teeth "As is pomegranate· flower" and his turban shining
  "As shines full fortnig4t's[text-layer?]. moon" (p. 351, folio 342); the holy men whose
  footprints are in Lahut and who hold a ruby, **neither explained** — **(not stated)** (p. 357,
  folio 348); the ascetics who pass Lahut and reach Ulwahet[text-layer?] and "not one word from
  them ·escapes" (p. 362, folio 353); the mountain that weeps with the sufferer (p. 376, folio 367);
  the death-angels Azrad[text-layer?], Munkar and Nakir met on the road and mistaken for the
  lover's messengers (p. 383, folio 374); the deer that speak after Sasui's death (p. 390,
  folio 381); the beloved's fragrance reaching the hills (p. 387, folio 378); the lover carrying
  her across the passes (p. 397, folio 388).
- **A fakir's prophecy fulfilled, inside `THE KING AND THE MINSTREL`.** "His si�ter[text-layer?] had
  a son named Bijal about whom a· fa.kif[text-layer?] had predicted that he was fated to kill
  Diach" (p. 352, folio 343), and, at the headnote's close, "obtained, as the fakir had foretold,
  the head of the king" (p. 352, folio 343). **The fakir is unnamed** — **(not stated)** — and it
  is the book's only occurrence of the word in the singular inside a story. The king's salvation
  follows: "He found acceptance . .under Allal}'s[text-layer?] door ; / His million-'numbered .
  needs fulfilled, told . o'er." (p. 356, folio 347).
- **The primordial covenant, made the origin of two of the folk romances.** In the Suhini cycle:
  "Time was when God, the One and the Eternal, spake / Unto the souls and thundered : ' Am I not
  your Lord ? ' / Then, even then, to Suhini had there come a love / And longing for the neatherd.
  'Twas of God's own will / That might of waters broke her earthen pot in twain." (p. 406,
  folio 397). In the Marui cycle: "When there fell on mine earsthe[text-layer?] Word, / ' Am I not
  then your Lord ? ' / And with ' Yes ' my heart gave assent, / It was then that my promise I made
  / With the folk in the hedges pent." (p. 422; printed again at p. 290, folio 281 as "When there
  fell on mine ears the Word / " Am I not then your Lord ? "1"). **This is the doctrinal hinge by
  which the folk romances are made religious: the heroine's love is dated to before the world.**
  **The one Koranic phrase the book footnotes inside the poet's own verse is this one**, sourced
  "From the Koran. Rodwell's Translation. Edition Everyman, p. 3 rn[text-layer?]." (p. 290,
  folio 281) — **the page figure is unreadable**. Sorley then cross-reads it against Whinfield's
  introduction to the Masnawi on the pre-eternal contract (p. 291, folio 282, footnote 1 citing
  "The Masnawi-i-Manavi. Translated by Whinfield. Introduction, pp. xxii-xxvii.").
- **Music itself is treated as a supernatural power, and Sorley says so in order to dismiss it.**
  "was sufficient to create in the minds of the listeners a belief t.hat this music was _a kind of
  myste'rious and almost superhuman art ' closed to the bulk of m'arikind[text-layer?]. … Music in
  fact was still a form of mumbo-j'umbo." (p. 228, folio 219). The modes are "deified and are
  represented in religious art in stereotyped ways as gods and goddesses with special powers"
  (p. 231, folio 222), and Sorley calls the whole scheme "a great deal of artificiality
  ind[text-layer?] mythological make-believe" (p. 231, folio 222). **The Sur Sorath's
  head-sacrifice is the book's only narrative miracle attached to the poet's own work**: "the
  singer, the ' master of music ', is depicted · as charming the king so overwhelmingly as to
  induce him to yield up his head to the singer in loving sacrifice which gains him communion with
  God" (p. 250, folio 241), and "it makes clear the relation believed to exist between music and
  immortal things" (pp. 228–229, folios 219–220). **In chunk 016 neither the king nor the singer is
  named; the index later supplies "Diach the King hath yielded up his head   347" (p. 310,
  folio 301) and the glossary supplies both, "Bijal  Name of the singer" and "Diich[text-layer?]
  Name of the King" (p. 430, folio 421). The book never states that the untitled `THE KING AND THE
  MINSTREL` sections are the Sur Sorath** — **(not stated)**.

#### Significance

- **What the work is, stated on the book's first page of text and repeated.** "THE collection of
  mystical poems known as the RisaJ.o[text-layer?] of Shah Abdul Latif of Bhit is the only classic
  which the language of Sind has yet produced in the realm of deeply imaginative literature."
  (p. 7, folio vii); "the beautiful mystical poetry of Shah Abdul Latif of Bhit-the only classic
  work in the Sindhi language" (p. 19, folio 10); "Nor is there in Sindhi literature either before
  or after Shah Abdul Latif's time anything fit to be compared with'.[text-layer?] the Risalo"
  (p. 212, folio 203); the Risalo is "the only gem cast up from the sea of Sind's vernacular
  literature" (p. 216, folio 207).
- **His religious character, as Sorley states it at the outset.** "They are the wor�[text-layer?] of
  a natural poet of Siifi[text-layer?] leanings." (p. 7, folio vii); the thought "is also everywhere
  permeated with the deep subtleties of Sufi philosophy" (p. 8, folio viii). **No silsila is named
  there or anywhere.** The dedication poem calls him "your lowly hamlet bard" and "your humble
  minstrel" whose "melodies / Enthralled a rust!c [text-layer?] people holds" (p. 9).
- **The standing of the poems in Sind, as Sorley reports it.** "In Sind his poems are · held in such
  universal and popular esteem as is accorded only to poetry which has successfally [text-layer?]
  interpreted the most intimate thoughts and the sincerest feelings of a people. In some
  fori:n [text-layer?] or other_ the verses are. known to all classes." (p. 7, folio vii).
- **The poet as the true measure of Sindhi Islam — the hinge of Sorley's argument about Sindhi
  religion, and his single most quotable sentence for this archive.** "The real form and meaning of
  Islam in Sind is not to be found in these ebullitions of popular and incredible fanaticism, but
  is to be read in the beautiful spiritual poems of Shah Abdul Latif. It will be most unfair to
  judge Sind by the former and forget the latter." (p. 169, folio 160). **The "ebullitions" are the
  dancing of darwishes at Tatta and, in the pages immediately before, the mendicant extortion and
  shrine scenes at Sehwan.** The surrounding judgement about the century: "It was the Kalhora's
  practice of deferring to the priestly class that led to the extraordinary reverence for pirs,
  Sayids and holy men, which may be said to have been the predominant characteristic of eighteenth
  century Sind." (p. 163, folio 154).
- **His audience crosses the Muslim–Hindu line, asserted five times and given three different

- **He is the first literary user of Sindhi, and the language is defended in its own right.** "Shah
  Abdul Latif was the first great exponent of the imaginative use of the Sindhi language" (p. 215,
  folio 206); "He is a poet using for the first time with supreme skill the language of the country
  folk" (p. 217, folio 208). The language passage the book does not connect to him: "A language is
  usually a key to a people. The Sindhi language is a beautiful instrument of precision admirably
  adapted to the civilization for which it was intended-a peace-loving agricultural and pastoral
  culture elaborated by an industrious and non-martial people. But it is naturally deficient in
  words suited to the kind of civilization for which Burton stood" (p. 120, folio 111) — **offered
  as a defence of Sind against Richard Burton and flagged in the notes as an unlinked passage.**
- **The poetry is unrepeatable, argued three ways.** "It is extremely doubtful whether poetry like
  Shah Abdul Latif's can ever again be composed in Sind. The verses can of course be imitated and
  have been imitated" (p. 214, folio 205); "the probabilities are all against Sind's producing a
  second Shah Abdul Latif" (p. 215, folio 206); "No one will ever produce again this particular
  kind of poetry amidst the same local environment and in the same halo-content of thought, belief
  and feeling" (p. 215, folio 206).
- **Its present hold, and Sorley's qualification of it.** "the poems of Shah Abdul. Latif still bind
  Sind with a powerful spell of love and admiration" (p. 215, folio 206); but "Modern delight in
  the poems is of a different genre entirely… Though the ideas in the poems are still believed they
  are not believed in quite the same way or for quite the same reason" (p. 215, folio 206). Had
  British education not intervened, the poems "w�uld [text-layer?] have remained a rather vague
  body of sung and recited verse handed down from one singer to another and familiar, as songs; to
  the Sindhis who delight in Hste:r;iing[text-layer?] to this form of literary· composition"
  (pp. 233–234, folios 224–225); "without . the stimulus of English education and · the interest in
  literature produced by it the poems of Shah Abdul Latif would still have been today in much the
  same condition as that in which the 'J3riti?h[text-layer?] found them in 1843" (p. 234,
  folio 225).
- **His genius is unexplained by his background — the governing claim of the Birth of a Classic
  chapter.** "In Shah Abdul Latif's case the enquirer finds small help towards knowing how this
  outstanding poet came to the fulfilment of his·genius. Shah Abdul Latif's life in fact would seem
  to prove the truth of the old adag�[text-layer?] ' poeta nascitur, non fit '" (p. 212, folio 203),
  restated as the chapter's closing line, "They p�ove[text-layer?] inqeed[text-layer?] _ the truth
  of the saying' �hat[text-layer?] the poet is born and. not made" (p. 226, folio 217).
- **His originality is limited in the same breath as it is asserted.** "Shah Abdul Latif cannot be
  called conspicuous for any great originality of thought. But he expresses supremely well a
  species of religious philosophy current amongst the better educated men of his time" (p. 217,
  folio 208); "Shah Abdul Latif is no mere imitator of Jalaluddin Rumi,. of Jami, or Hafiz or Al
  Bistami" (p. 216, folio 207); "The fact that Sind was the home of several skilled exponents of
  Sufi thought during the middle ages accounts fully for the mastery of Sufi expression exhibited
  by Shah Abdul Latif" (p. 252, folio 243) — **no order, no silsila, no khanqah and no named
  exponent is given** — **(not stated)**.
- **Sorley's demotion of him against the Persians, recorded in full because the archive will want
  the author's own limits on his subject.** "In comparison with the achievements o{[text-layer?]
  the great Persian masters Shah Abdul Latif's poetry is as the miniature of the plaque artists
  when set beside the great canvases of Michael Angelo or Rafael ·;[text-layer?] or as when the
  minor elegances of the light operas of Sullivan or Offenbach are put in
  j uxtaposition[text-layer?] with the magnificent music of great operatic composers like Wagner or
  Verdi. The work is fine but it is on an infinitely lower plane of achievement. Excellent in a
  humble way though it be, it is not in the same class" (pp. 252–253, folios 243–244); "the Sindhi
  poet is cast in a much more homely an.cl[text-layer?] simple mould" (p. 253, folio 244); "Such
  sublimity of expression will be looked for in vain in the verses of the Sindhi :poet[text-layer?]"
  (p. 253, folio 244). **Set against chunk 012's verdict "He is incompar­ably the greatest man whom
  Sind has yet produced in the realm of imaginative art" (p. 178, folio 169) — the two are not
  contradictory (one is about Sind, one about Persia) but they belong together.** The counterweight:
  "Yet when ,Shah[text-layer?] Abdul Latif is drawing a moral from some little everyday occurrence
  or stating the simple conviction of the Oneness of things, the delights of union with God, or the
  pangs of separation, he can be fully as effective as any of the great Persian poets. Many of his
  shorter passages would not disgrace them" (pp. 253–254, folios 244–245). And the final
  characterisation: "the genius of the Sindhi poet is essentially lyrical. It is neither
  philosophic nor discursive" (p. 254, folio 245); "Shah Abdul Latif is primarily a poet and not a
  philosopher" (p. 293, folio 284); "Shah Abdul Latif was not a c�nscious[text-layer?] and
  consistent philosopher" (p. 262, folio 253).
- **He is placed at the top of Sind's Sufi poets, and dated.** "Of the Sufi poets of Sind about whom
  an immense modern literature is now poured forth, none are superior to Shah Abdul Latif and most,
  if not all, of them are later in date." (p. 299, folio 290); "Between the thirteenth century when
  Lal Shahbaz flourished and **the eighteenth century when Shah Abdul Latif lived** there is very
  little that can be. said to be securely established in the . way of verified historical fact."
  (p. 299, folio 290) — **a direct statement by the author that the archive's Sindhi material
  between the 1200s and the 1700s is not securely evidenced.** The Preface adds only "this
  eighteenth-century Sindhi poet" (p. 306, folio 297). His literary descent: "an expression of the
  later Sufism in India, especially as influenced by the Muhammadan domination of Upper India from
  the fifteenth century onwards" (p. 284, folio 275); "who represents an Indian Muslim development
  of the philosophy of Jala.Iuddin[text-layer?] Riimi[text-layer?]" (p. 290, folio 281); "how true
  Shah Abdul Latif is to the spirit of Siifi[text-layer?] belief" (p. 265, folio 256); "how Shah
  Abdul Latif is in the authentic line of Sufi poets" (p. 295, folio 286).
- **THE WARNING A SHRINE ARCHIVE MUST CARRY: the Risalo is nearly useless as topography.** "There
  is surprisingly little of Sind topography in the Risalo. Places are indeed mentioned : mountains
  are named and features of Sind scenery receive passing remark. But there is nothing individually
  distinctive of place, mountain or scenery : nothing of the peoples of the land, and nothing
  whatever of the stirring political events of his own day. Where a mountain is named it is more to
  emphasize the danger and difficulty of crossing it than with any idea that its location and
  character have any human interest. **His place names might be any place names for all the value
  they possess as guides to definite localities**" (p. 266, folio 257). The method that follows:
  "the knowledge of Sind and its rustic civilization to be culled from the Risalo is gained from
  the study of internal evidence and not otherwise" (p. 266, folio 257). **This should travel with
  any entry that cites the poems for a place**, and it is reinforced by the story-migration
  argument at p. 270 (folio 261): "The story wanders from Catch[text-layer?] over Lower Sind to
  Kech Mekran… so that names nearly similar have been given as belonging to places in the land of
  the Bahichis[text-layer?]."
- **The silence on politics, stated twice.** "He produced a very considerable quantity of verse. But
  it contains little or no reference to the current political events which he had witnessed. He was
  interested not in the transient phenomena of his age but in eternal verities, which formed a much
  better subject for immortal verse th.an the petty wars and intrigues of the KalhOra[text-layer?]"
  (p. 180, folio 171); "nothing whatever of the stirring political events of his own day" (p. 266,
  folio 257). **The two agree.**
- **The audience the poems were composed for, in the book's own summary of rural Sind — and it puts
  shrines into that landscape.** "It was a land of cultivators and herdsmen, keepers of camels,
  buffaloes, sheep, goats and oxen, **of tombs and Sayids**, of dust and heat, of the annual rise
  and fall of the waters of the Indus but inefficiently controlled by a crude system of irrigation.
  For the common folk living in such a country Shah Abdul Latif composed his verses" (p. 265,
  folio 256). The poems' own subject is the same world: "They are concerned with the open-air life
  of a popula­tion[text-layer?] that works and lives largely in the fields, turning the river
  waters on to the land, and gathering the grain from the threshing floor into the large earthenware
  j ars[text-layer?] that hold a home food-supply sufficient to last till the floods of the Indus
  inundate the lands once again in the due fulfilment of the seasons" (p. 256, folio 247).
- **The text is at two removes from him, and the manuscripts disagree.** "The . poems, as we know
  them today� have; however, suffered from the . fact that they were not written down as composed.
  They have also been subj ected _t o[text-layer?] careless compilation :by persons unfamiliar with
  literary tradition _and . ill-equipped with scholarly taste"; "Thus the poems as we know them
  today are not identical with the verses that came from the lips of Shah Abdul Latif himself as he
  recited them. They are instead the verses as subse­quently written down by others, altered by
  them, and edited by them." (both p. 233, folio 224); "In the form in which they now exist there
  is much extrari~ous[text-layer?] ·matter. It is . often difficult to say with any accuracy ·
  whether a doubtful passage is the work of Shah Abdul Latif or not.· The manuscripts of the poems
  are. very discrepant in this respect." (p. 233, folio 224). **No manuscript count, no shelfmarks
  and no dates for any manuscript** — **(not stated)**; the index carries the topic as "Risalo
  (Shah jo) … (MSS. and editions) 1 70[text-layer?], 1 75[text-layer?] and n, 224, 225" (p. 439),
  **which is the exact page range the archive needs for it**. The transmission account: "Tradition
  says that he did not write his poems at all but that they were collected and transcribed by
  amanuenses. This passes the bounds of credibility�[text-layer?] The poems were certainly later
  collated by scribes and formed into a regular collection which exists in several manuscript
  forms" (pp. 183–184, folios 174–175). The explanation of the form: "Doubtless the poetry of
  Orientals owes th�s[text-layer?] untidiness to its origin in half spontaneous lyrical
  composition, a kind of semi­improvisation which permits thoughts to be uttered exactly as they
  are conceived" (p. 259, folio 250).
- **Two named signs of corruption, and three interpolating poets.** "Among the more obvious signs of
  careless compilation and lack of literary taste are the frequent incorporation in the poems of
  explanatory asides like ' As Shah Latif says ' and the insertion of short verses by other poets,
  as for instance Inayat, Lakhmir and Jiinej o[text-layer?]." (p. 233, folio 224). **The glossary
  confirms all three as poets** — "Iniyat[text-layer?]  Name of a Sindhi poet.";
  "Jiinejo[text-layer?]  Name of a Sindhi poet."; "Lakhmir  Name o'f[text-layer?] a Sindhi poet."

  1 75[text-layer?], 224" (p. 435); "J iinejo[text-layer?], 224" (p. 436); "Lakhmir (in Risalo)
  224" (p. 436). All three appear in the signature position inside the verse: "Inayat says : ' The

  carry the name "Inayat"; see the Doubts list.**

- **The text Sorley translated is not the Risalo but a named abridgement.** "The poems which I have
  transla,ted[text-layer?] do not form the complete mass of verse attributed to Shah Abdul Latif.
  They are instead tl;ie[text-layer?] poems contained in the abridged compilation called· the '

  unsatis­factory[text-layer?]) 226 ; 23 I[text-layer?], 259, 262" (p. 438). **The canon was

- **Sorley re-ordered the poems, and printed his three categories.** "in tr;;mslating[text-layer?]
  the poems I have declined to follow the order of the poems given in the Muntakhab. Instead, while
  keeping · the individual poems entire, · l[text-layer?] have rearranged them according to their

  (2) names the habits of fakirs and holy men as a subject class of the poetry — the nearest the
  book comes to putting shrine content inside the verse.** The Muntakhab's own three classes are
  given separately: "first, the mystery of divine love, second, · the folklore of Sind in its
  love-stories, and third, some prominent ·features of the common experience of the Sind rustic
  countryside" (pp. 234–235, folios 225–226). **In the printed translation the poems carry no sur
  titles at all** — every heading is an English thematic title, and "Not one of the sur names …
  appears as a heading anywhere in these pages"; the sur names survive there only in a concordance

- **The verse unit is the `bait`, named once.** "One great difficulty in dealing with the mass of
  poems of the Muntakhab lies in the irregular length of the baits… The baits are usually
  self-con­tained[text-layer?]. The thought often changes · abruptly[text-layer?] from one to
  another." and "Personally I regard the irregularity in length as some­thing[text-layer?] worth
  preserving because it is a sign of the spontaneity which is a supreme merit of the original."
  (both p. 307, folio 298).

  - and· of S�hini[text-layer?] and Mehar, and the much lighter semi-comedies of
  J;..ilari:and[text-layer?] Chanesar, Momul and Ra.no[text-layer?], and Marui and Umar" (both

  origins as Sorley assigns them: the Sorath story "comes from Cutch or Kathia·war_[text-layer?]
  and mrist[text-layer?] go back to the twelfth century A.D . at least… The story is plainly a
  Hindu tale ill'[text-layer?] ·an essentlals[text-layer?]" (p. 271, folio 262); the Momul and Rano
  story "is a Lower Sind story connected with the Sodha who live in the Thar country near Umarkot"
  and "manifestly relates to a period when this portion of Sind had not been converted to Islam"
  (p. 270, folio 261); the Marui story "belongs to the same locality and seems to go back to the
  days when the Siimra[text-layer?] were the chief political power in the country" (p. 270,
  folio 261); the Suhini and Mehar tale "must have come into Sind through the immigration from the
  Punjab of those Hindu races which form an important element in the population, as has been made

  folio 261). **The Sasui–Punhun cycle is "the longest collection of germane poems in the Risalo"
  (p. 257, folio 248) and the Arrangement table gives it seven surs and forty-six numbered items
  (I–XLVI, pp. 315–316).** **The glossary identifies its heroine as "a Brahman girl from Bhambhor"
  (p. 431, folio 422), and the verse states her caste as a disability — "Mother, my caste is a slur
  / In the minds of the men of Kech" (p. 393, folio 384).** **She dies on the road and the book
  records NO grave, NO tomb, NO shrine, NO burial place and NO subsequent veneration for her**
  (p. 370; pp. 390–397, folios 381–388) — **all (not stated)** — **which is worth recording
  precisely because a shrine archive will expect a Sasui tomb here.**

- **The book's own plan for Part II, printed in its contents** (p. 207): `INTRODUCTION. Principles
  of Poetry and Poetical Criticism 199`; `CHA PTER I. The Birth of a Classic 203`; `CHAPTER II. The
  Poems of the Risalo 224` with four lettered sections; `CHAPTER III. A Web of many Strands` with
