  Islam)" (p. 439). **The book calls its own Part I "the first volume"** (p. 218, folio 209).
- **Sorley's declared methodological limit, in the first person.** "Historically we may trace as far
  as we can the origin of some of these ideas. Philosophically we may analyse the depth of the
  thought or the worth of the metaphysic. Aesthetically we may describe the effect upon the
  introspective mind of him who listens to the poetry in which Sufism is declared. **But beyond
  that we cannot go.**" (p. 297, folio 288). And his last word: "This may be all delusion and
  word-weaving. But if it is merely delusion, the delusion is a very pleasant one, shared by many,
  like Shah Abdul Latif, whos�[text-layer?] verses will continue to delight mankind." (p. 297,
  folio 288).
- **Doctrine, as the book states it.** God and man are apart: "The Sindhj[text-layer?] poet is a
  true M-uslim[text-layer?] in that for him G,0d[text-layer?] and man are things apart and that
  approach .to th�[text-layer?] 'divine ·is possible only. by - a long pedod[text-layer?] of
  struggle and effort" (p. 237, folio 228); "It insists upon the utter unworthiness of man when
  compared with the majesty, mystery and completeness of God. Thus love for Shah Abdul Latif is
  merely the means of approaching the divine. It is not a description of the divine itself."
  (p. 240, folio 231). The mysticism is classified four ways by exclusion: "It is .not a pure
  l~ve[text-layer?] mysticism, nor . a nature mysticism, no:i;[text-layer?] an .
  in.telle_ctual[text-layer?] · mysticism" (p. 236, folio 227) but "a love mysticism with a rich
  religious signifi.cance[text-layer?]" (p. 235, folio 226). The two characteristics said to run
  through all of it: "a belief in the unity, through effort, of the human soul with God, and the
  transitoriness of temporal things, which are a veil hiding the true nature of illumination"
  (p. 291, folio 282). Separation is the master text: "There is hardly a poem in the Risalo that
  does not preach this doctrine" — "' The seas of separation roll / And drown each single separate
  soul.'" (p. 292, folio 283, from `Sur Kalyan`). Self is the great obstacle and the great means
  (p. 294, folio 285). Oneness against twoness is stated twice in nearly the same words on widely
  separated pages (pp. 280, 321, 337; folios 271, 312, 328). The technical pair is printed twice in
  two spellings: "ishq maj azi[text-layer?] and ishq hakikki[text-layer?]" (p. 262, folio 253) and
  "ishk hakkiki … ishk maj azi[text-layer?]" (p. 291, folio 282), glossed only as "false and true
  love"; **no Arabic or Persian term is printed for "non-existence"** — **(not stated)** (p. 383,
  folio 374). "Die before you die" has a whole section (XXX) to itself (pp. 293, 388; folios 284,
  379). The imagery inventory: the moth and the flame, "clay in the oven", the wine and the
  wineshop "familiar to all lovers of Omar Khayyam", the camel as "the stupid waywardness of the
  human heart", the Healer or Physician, the Friend or Beloved, and "The commonest image, however,
  in the Risalo is the struggle on the ' path ' or the ' way ' to the Beloved" (pp. 262–264,
  folios 253–255). **The one explicitly economic image**: "In the beautiful poem of the Spinning
  Woman, which has a deep mystical meaning, God is the banker, the saraf or merchant, who weighs
  out the spun yarn and rewards the righteous spinners according to their merits" (p. 264,
  folio 255) — **"saraf" is the only piece of commercial vocabulary given in the original.**
- **The material world inside the verse, which is where the book's social and economic content for
  this row actually sits.** The spinners: the door-to-door tout (pp. 366–367, folios 357–358); the
  merchant who "when spinners he calls" convenes the workforce (p. 365, folio 356); weighing,
  inspection and a spinner questioned "Apart" (p. 367, folio 358); "an ounce in the tare" as the
  only named unit of weight (p. 367, folio 358); and the deserted-market stanza — "The wheels are
  dismantled. The spinners where flown ? … they have gone and closed up their huts. / In the midst
  of the trees no cotton plants are : / So here are no spinners. The empty bazaar / Has ensalted my
  heart. '" (p. 367, folio 358). The merchant voyage: six named commodities, "Cloves, cardamoms and
  store of cloth, / Sweet-smelling grass and ambergris" and "cloth and pearls" (pp. 333–334,
  folios 324–325); the sailing season named by the north wind and the off-season work of twisting
  lanyards and oiling boats (pp. 329–330, folios 320–321); five fare words in eight lines — "scot",
  "lot", "fee", "money-lot", "purse" — and a stated refusal of carriage without payment (p. 330,
  folio 321); the twelve-month absence (p. 330, folio 321); named parts of a vessel, hatches, keel,
  strake, timbers, rudder, prow, spar, cable, hawser, anchor, lanyards, canvas, bunting, sails,
  sheets (pp. 331–332, folios 322–323). The craft-decline lament: "The lapidaries now are gone /
  Wh9[text-layer?] diam.mid[text-layer?] pierce4[text-layer?] and ruby red… Where
  crafts�en[text-layer?] wrought of yor.e,[text-layer?] the smiths / · Beat worthless - _ pewter
  ·now _ in�tead.[text-layer?]" (p. 333, folio 324). Grain and famine: "0 Lord of Rain, . for
  Allah's sake… Make / The grain that groweth cheap to buy." (p. 344, folio 335); "The
  grain-amassers, mad on gain, / Do wring their hands : and fifteen grows / From .five-a threefold
  quickening… May all who trade in famfue[text-layer?] greed, / May all the mise.rs[text-layer?]
  disappear. ." (p. 347, folio 338) — **"fifteen … From five" is the only numerical relation in
  chunk 020 and it is printed as words, not numerals**; "The corn is cheap : there's
  butte,r[text-layer?] in the jar" (p. 345, folio 336). Transhumance ordered by the rain: "Take the
  cattle out / And make your way across the plain. / Desert your huts. Your panniers fill"
  (p. 343, folio 334). The water-storage pits "That rnen[text-layer?] scooped mi([text-layer?] in
  days: agone" (p. 346, folio 337). The textile economy of the Marui poems — silk, lac-dyed coarse
  cloth, shawls, wool, striped cloth, velvet, broadcloth against "My coarse rough blanket" — set
  against a herding economy of goatherds, herdsmen, huts and flocks (pp. 427–429, folios 418–420).
  Camel husbandry in working detail, tying, binding, shackling, hobbling and saddling (pp. 335–336,
  folios 326–327). The potters' kiln described in working detail and made the figure of concealed
  love (p. 372, folio 363). And, in the Sasui headnote, "a famine, an inter-state grain purchase, a
  negotiated price remission and a camel caravan" — "Owing to famine in Kech the people of that
  land had sent a deputation to Bhambh6r[text-layer?] to purchase corn. The leader of the caravan
  from Kech succeeded in obtaining a remission in the price of the grain from Sasui by promising to
  bring Punhiin[text-layer?]" (p. 370). **Not one of the trades named anywhere in the translation
  is attached to a shrine, a saint or an endowment** — **(not stated)**.

### lal-shahbaz-qalandar — Lal Shahbaz Qalandar

The book names him "Lal Shahbaz", "Lal · Shahbaz"[text-layer?], "Lal Shahba_z"[text-layer?],
"Sayid Usman 'Shah[text-layer?] Marwandi", "Jivelal, the ' living Pir '", "Shah Lal Baz" and,
quoting Jethmal Parsram, "Kalandar Lal Marwandi". **Site-specific material exists in four chunks
only (014, 016, 019, 024); chunks 003, 006, 007, 008, 009 and 010 name Sehwan as a bare toponym and
nothing else, and that material is converted into a place-level bullet below rather than left here.**

**Saint.**
- **His personal name, given once.** "The best-known early one was Sayid Usman 'Shah[text-layer?]
  Marwandi who became famous as Lal Shahba_z[text-layer?] with a popular shrine at Sehwan."
  (p. 298, folio 289). **This matches the archive row's parenthetical "(Usman Marwandi)" and is the
  first time the book gives it.** **The row's death year 1274 and its silsila "Qalandari" are NOT
  in this book**: it gives only "the thirteenth century when Lal Shahbaz flourished" (p. 299,
  folio 290) and "He was a Kalandar[text-layer?]" (p. 299, folio 290) — **(not stated)** for both.
- **His reputation.** "He was a Kalandar[text-layer?] an9-[text-layer?] attained a great reputation
  for sanctity in his lifetime and · after his . death." (p. 299, folio 290).
- **He arrived with a party, inside a dated wave of immigration.** "From the thirteenth century
  onwards there was a great immigration into Sind of learned and religious-minded men from the
  centre of the Muslim world. **Lal Shahbaz, who came with several companions, some of whom made
  names for themselves elsewhere in India, was a man of this kind.** The ancestor of Abul
  Fazul[text-layer?] was another. The great ascendancy of the Sayids in Sind doubtless dates from
  about this . time." (p. 302, folio 293). **None of the companions is named** — **(not stated)**.
- **He was an author, and his works were school texts — a striking and citable fact.** "He was the
  author of a number of works used for the instruction of youth in a later age, as noted by
  Burton." (p. 298, folio 289) — **no title is given there** — **(not stated)** — and the
  attribution is to Burton, not to Sorley's own inspection. Four titles are given elsewhere, as
  the first four books of the Sindhi madressa syllabus: "The works studied were the Mizan-i-sarf by
  Lal Shahbaz, the famous scholar and saint of Sehwan�[text-layer?]"; "Then followed study of the.
  Ajnas or Munshaib rules for the formation of the increased derivatives; and the
  Kisim�i-d.oyyuni.[text-layer?] for the ii-regular[text-layer?] verbs. . Both these works ·were by
  Lal ·Shahbaz but were written in Persian. The next book to be read was the Akd, also by. Lal
  Shahbaz, in Arabic and P�rsiari[text-layer?] mixed, dealing with.the permutation of letters"
  (both p. 223, folio 214). **Four works — "Mizan-i-sarf", "Ajnas or Munshaib",
  "Kisim�i-d.oyyuni"[text-layer?], "Akd" — with their languages specified.** **This chunk attaches
  the saint to Sehwan by name ("the famous scholar and saint of Sehwan"), not merely the toponym.**
- **The modern source for his life, and Sorley's verdict on it.** "Jethmal Parsram has given some
  account of him and his companions in his book on ' Sind and its Sufis ' ; but the facts are
  meagre." (p. 299, folio 290).
- **His shrine is the earliest fixed point in Sorley's chronology of Sind Sufism, and the five
  centuries after him are declared a documentary blank** (p. 299, folio 290; quoted in full under
  `bhit-bhit-shah` above).

**Shrine / Darbar.**
- **The shrine, its location and its annual gathering — the fullest single statement in the book.**
  "The Shahbaz is of course the famous Lal Shahbaz, or Jivelal, the ' living Pir ' whose shrine at
  Sehwan attracts a great gathering of both Mussulmans and Hindus every year in a strange mixture
  of worship ." (p. 257, folio 248). **Four facts: a shrine; a location, "at Sehwan"; an annual
  gathering; and a cross-community congregation which Sorley calls "a strange mixture of worship".**
  **No date for the gathering, no ʿurs, no mela or fair name, no custodian, no offerings, no
  attendance figure, no death year, no silsila and no alternative name such as Usman Marwandi**
  appear there — all **(not stated)** in that chunk.
- **The route by which he enters the book is Baluchi oral poetry, not a Sindhi source.** The verse
  Sorley is glossing reads "also Shahbaz, the generous to his friends, a firm embankment erected by
  the Ruler of the Faithful.'" (p. 257, folio 248, footnote 2 citing "ibid . , p. 146.", i.e.
  Dames's *Popular Poetry of the Baloches*). **"Lal Shahbaz" as a name is Sorley's identification
  of the poem's "Shahbaz", not the poem's own wording** — a transmission fact worth keeping.
- **The index's entry for the saint, with two printed pages no chunk of running prose reaches.**
  "Lal · Shahbaz, 158 (Shah Lal Baz) 159. 2 14[text-layer?], 248, 28 9[text-layer?], 293" (p. 436)
  — **printed pp. 158, 159, 214, 248, 289, 293, of which only 248 is the chunk-016 passage.**
  Printed 158–159 falls inside the index's "privileged classes" and "Sayids (privileged) 157-62"
  ranges, and printed 289 and 293 inside "Siifis[text-layer?] and Sufism … (practice of by ' common
  man ') 289-94" (p. 440). **The entry gives no death year, no silsila, no ʿurs date, no custodian
  and no offering** — all **(not stated)**. The place is indexed separately and at length:
  "Sehwan, 2 1[text-layer?], 4 1 -3[text-layer?], 74, 75, 78, 89, 97, 104, I I 7[text-layer?],
  I I9[text-layer?], 138, 158-60 , 2 16[text-layer?], 248" (p. 439) — **fourteen printed pages, of
  which 138, 158–160 and 248 overlap the saint's own entry and the rest are trade, revenue and
  route material.**

**Practices & Events.**
- **THE ʿURS — the saint-day gathering at the tomb, in Sorley's own voice and in the present
  tense.** "**The gathering at his tomb on his saint-day produced and still produces
  s1ftange[text-layer?] sights. Religion and superstition are mingled and Hindus and Muslims
  assemble together to do honour to · his memory.**" (p. 299, folio 290). **Five facts: the tomb is
  the venue; the occasion is "his saint-day"; the gathering is continuing; it is cross-community;
  and Sorley's own gloss is "Religion and superstition are mingled".** **No date, no month, no name
  for the observance, no duration and no figure are given by Sorley himself** — **(not stated)**.
- **THE FAIR — Jethmal Parsram quoted, with an attendance figure, a camping practice and a musical
  practice.** "Jethmal Parsram remarks, ' **In Sehwan where Lal · Shahbaz[text-layer?] (also called
  Kalandar Lal Marwandi) lived and died you find during the annual fair thousands of Hindus and
  Muhammadans camped round the t<?mh[text-layer?], mixing freely, and singing the Sufi songs that
  ever melt the heart.** Wrong things have also entered into the celebration of these holy days,
  but that is what always happens. '" (p. 299, folio 290, footnote 1 citing "op. cit., p. 93.").
  **Nine separately citable facts: a location; a life-and-death claim ("lived and died" there); an
  alternative name; a periodicity ("the annual fair"); an order of magnitude ("thousands"); two
  named communities; an accommodation practice ("camped round the t<?mh[text-layer?]"); a social
  claim ("mixing freely"); and a musical practice ("singing the Sufi songs"). Plus a disapproving
  aside and a plural, "these holy days", implying the fair runs more than one day.**
  **"t<?mh"[text-layer?] is "tomb" destroyed in the text layer, and it sits in the most quotable
  sentence about this shrine in the whole book. It MUST be checked against the PDF before
  quotation.** **No month, no calendar date, no custodian, no sajjada nashin, no offerings, no
  revenue and no endowment are mentioned** — all **(not stated)**.

**Legends & Miracles.**
- **The epithet "the ' living Pir '", glossing a Sindhi-looking word.** "the famous Lal Shahbaz, or
  Jivelal, the ' living Pir '" (p. 257, folio 248). **The printed string is "Jivelal"; Sorley's own
  gloss for it is "the ' living Pir '". The chunk supplies no story behind it** — **(not stated)**.
  **This collides with the "Zinda Pir (The Living Pir)" the book attaches to Khwajo Khizr at
  p. 178 (folio 169), and with three Jhulelal / Daryalal / Uderolal rows in the archive; no merge
  is made here — see the person-level bullet below and the Doubts list.**

**Significance.**
- **He and Shah Abdul Latif are the two fixed points of Sindhi Sufism in this book, and everything
  between them is declared unverified** (p. 299, folio 290).
- **Two cross-references the index makes into this entry that no page of running prose makes, and
  both are serious.** "Murad Buksh Shah . (See Lal Shahbaz)" (p. 438) — **which collides head-on
  with the same index's separate entry "Sayid Muhammad Husain (Pir Murad) 248, 291" (p. 439), a
  different saint with a tomb at Tatta**; and "Udero Lal (see Lal Shahbaz) 159" (p. 441) — **which
  puts the archive row `shrine-at-odero-lal-udero-lal-teerath-asthan` directly in play.** **Neither
  merge is made; both go to the Doubts worker.**
- **What this book does NOT supply for the row**: a death year, a silsila, an ʿurs date or month, a
  custodian or sajjada nashin, any offering, endowment or revenue, any architecture, and any
  founding date for the shrine — all **(not stated)**.

### shrine-of-shah-abdul-karim-bulri — Shrine of Shah Abdul Karim Bulri

The book calls him "Sayid Abdul Karim" and "Shah Karim". **This is the one fully described archive
shrine in the book, and the only ʿurs in it.**

**Saint.**
- **His relation to the Bhit poet, stated two ways that agree.** "His ancestor in the fourth
  generation before him was the famous Sayid Abdul Karim" (pp. 180–181, folios 171–172); "The
  poet's great-great-grandfather was the Shah Karim whose name is still held in reverence in Lower
  Sind" (p. 212, folio 203).
- **The only date attached to him is a floruit, not a death year** — "{floruit[text-layer?] circa
  A.D. 1600)" (p. 181, folio 172). **No death year, no silsila and no ʿurs date** —
  **(not stated)**. The archive row's 1623 is not in this book.
- **"Lower Sind" is the only region given for his reverence** in chunk 014 (p. 212, folio 203).

**Shrine / Darbar.**
- **The durgah, its location by taluka and district, and its fair.** "whose durgah was at Bulri in
  the Giini[text-layer?] ta.Iuka[text-layer?] of the Hyderabad district and is still a great place
  of yearly pilgrimage for its Shah Karim fair (mero) held on the saint's ' urs ' day"
  (pp. 180–181, folios 171–172). **Every element is here: durgah, location, an annual pilgrimage, a
  named fair with its Sindhi term "(mero)", and the ʿurs day as its calendar anchor.** **Note the
  location as printed is "the Giini[text-layer?] ta.Iuka[text-layer?] of the Hyderabad district",
  not the district given in the archive row (Tando Muhammad Khan).**
- **The family's movement to and from Bulri is explicitly unexplained.** "It is not known how the
  family had migrated to Bulri or why, in the generations immediately preceding Shah Abdul Latif's
  birth, it had returned to the vicinity of Matiari" (p. 181, folio 172). **Recorded because it is
  a gap the book itself marks.**
- **The index, three headwords in agreement.** "Sayid Abdul Karim, 172, (Shah Karim) 1 72[text-layer?],
  1 74[text-layer?], 203" (p. 439); "Shah Karim, I 72[text-layer?], I 74[text-layer?], 203"
  (p. 440); "Bulri, 172" (p. 433). **"Bulri, 172" is the only time in chunk 024 that a place is
  indexed at the same printed page as a named saint, and it is the reason this row is handled here
  rather than as a bare toponym.** **The index does not call Bulri a shrine, does not say Shah
  Karim is buried there, and gives no death year, no silsila and no observance** — all
  **(not stated)**.

**Practices & Events.**
- **The yearly pilgrimage and the "mero"** (pp. 180–181, folios 171–172), above. **This is the only
  ʿurs named anywhere in the book.**

**Significance.**
- **His verse was part of the later poet's daily reading**, stated twice — "the Koran, the Masnawi
  of Jalaluddin RU.mi[text-layer?], and the Sindhi verses of Shah Karim were always in the poet's
  hands" (p. 183, folio 174); "were constantly in his hands" (pp. 212–213, folios 203–204).
  **The only statement linking the two shrines' figures textually rather than genealogically.**
- **His verse is said to survive inside the Risalo, and to be slight.** "Though some poetry is
  attributed to Shah Karim and a few of his verses are believed to be incorporated in the Risalo
  there has co:i;ne[text-layer?] down to us nothing of outstanding merit" (p. 212, folio 203).
  **A textual-transmission claim: verses of the Bulri saint are inside the Bhit saint's book.**
  **Chunk 012 (p. 184, folio 175) attributes inserted verses in the Risalo to "Inayat" rather than
  to Shah Karim; the two statements are nowhere reconciled.**
- **Chunk 013 records explicitly that he is not named in that chunk** — **(not stated)** — so a
  consolidator does not hunt for a follow-up in the appendices.

### shrine-of-sachal-sarmast — Shrine of Sachal Sarmast

The book prints "Sachal (Sarmast)" and "Sachal Sarmast (The Intoxicated)". **The row is taken
because Sachal Sarmast is its named principal figure, not because the book describes a shrine.**

- **His one substantive appearance, with a birth window, an epithet and a tradition of a meeting.**
  "**Sachal Sarmast (The Intoxicated) is the best of these poets and he appears to have been born
  some time �>"etween[text-layer?] 1739 and I,7 57[text-layer?] and is alleged as a boy to have met
  Shah Abdul Latif.**" (p. 299, folio 290). **Four facts: a rank ("the best of these poets", i.e.
  of the Sufi poets of Sind after Latif); an epithet glossed in English; a birth window given as a
  range and hedged twice ("appears to have been born some time"); and a tradition of a boyhood
  meeting, hedged with "is alleged".** **The second date is destroyed: `I,7 57`[text-layer?] — a
  leading `I` for `1`, a stray comma and a broken digit string. `1739` survives intact. The window
  must not be repaired to "1739 and 1757", though that is what it appears to say.**
- **As a published poet, in a footnote.** "There is a big edition of the poetry of Sachal (Sarmast)
  bf[text-layer?] Agha Ghulam N abi[text-layer?] Khan Siifi[text-layer?]" (p. 212, folio 203,
  footnote 1). **No shrine, no place, no dates, no relation to Shah Abdul Latif stated there** —
  all **(not stated)**. **The footnote hangs off the sentence denying that any Sindhi literature is
  comparable to the Risalo, so it reads as a concessive pointer to a rival body of verse.** He also
  appears in the interwar anthology list as "A.gha[text-layer?] Sufi, G. G. ' Sa.chal[text-layer?]
  Sarmast.' 1933·" (p. 192, folio 183).
- **The index proves the book discusses him.** "Sachal Sarmast, 203n[text-layer?], 290" (p. 439) —
  **two references only, one a footnote.** **Printed p. 290 falls inside "(practice of by ' common
  man ') 289-94" (p. 440) and inside "darwishes (dervishes) , 1 60[text-layer?], 204, 278, 290-3"
  (p. 434), "Kalandars, I59[text-layer?]. 290" (p. 436), "Jelali (fakirs) I38[text-layer?], 290"
  and "Jemali (fakirs) 290, 291" (p. 436) — so the one substantive Sachal reference sits in the
  book's discussion of popular Sufi practice, not in its literary chapters.** **No shrine, tomb,
  place, death year, silsila or observance is given for him anywhere** — all **(not stated)** —
  and **Daraza and Khairpur are not attached to him.**

### shrine-at-odero-lal-udero-lal-teerath-asthan — Shrine at Odero Lal (Udero Lal Teerath Asthan)

- **The book's only mention of the name is as a parenthetical alternative for the Sehwan saint** —
  "the shrine of the Kalandar Shah Lal Baz (or Udero Lal) at Sehwan" (p. 168, folio 159). **The
  book therefore treats Shah Lal Baz and Udero Lal as one figure at one site, while the archive
  carries them as two rows at two places.** The index repeats the equation as a cross-reference:
  "Udero Lal (see Lal Shahbaz) 159" (p. 441).
- **No separate shrine, temple, teerath, festival or location for Udero Lal is given anywhere** —
  **(not stated)**. **This row is listed so the page is findable and must not be used to merge the
  two archive rows.** See the person-level bullet below, which carries the Jhulelal / Uderolal
  material to all three rows that name the figure, and the Doubts list.

### shrine-of-makhdoom-nooh-hala — Shrine of Makhdoom Nooh (Hala)

- **The book names the place once, as a danger to traders, and nothing else** — "The Samej a
  [text-layer?] between Sehwan and old Hala are described as a source of danger to traders for
  their robbing proclivities so that the country is not passable 'without a strong guard'" (p. 51,
  printed 42). **No shrine, no Makhdoom, no date** — all **(not stated)**. The further Hala
  material is place-level and is carried in the Hala bullet below.

### shrine-of-mian-mir — Shrine of Mian Mir

- **STANDING MUST-NOT-MERGE FLAG, AND IT IS TRIGGERED.** The index prints "Mian Mir Mahomed
  (Kalhoro) , 32" as a headword (p. 437) and, inside the `KalhOro` sub-list, "Mian Mir Md . , 32"
  (p. 436); the same man appears in Burton's quoted narrative as "Mian Mir Mahomed, the first
  prince of that (Kalh6ro [text-layer?]) dynasty", the Kalhora ruler who invited the Baluchis into
  Sind (p. 41, printed 32). **"Mian Mir Mahomed" is the Kalhora prince, indexed under the Kalhora
  dynasty at printed p. 32, in the middle of the book's dynastic-history pages. He must NOT be
  merged with the archive row `shrine-of-mian-mir` (Mian Mir, Alam Ganj / Dharampura, Lahore, Mir
  Mohammad of Sind, d. 1635, Qadiri).** **No merge is made. The row is named here only so that a
  consolidator does not re-derive it.** **Aggravating detail: the index also prints
  "Mii: Md . , 54"[text-layer?] and "Mir Md. Khan, 31" in the same Kalhora sub-list (pp. 436, 438),
  so the book has at least three similar Kalhora "M— Muhammad" names within two columns.**

### Place-level and person-level attachments (ruling (a) and the person rule)

**These notes were written before ruling (a) landed.** `archive_place.md` is empty for this book and
the thirty-four deferred mappings in the Doubts hedges name Sehwan, Rohri, Tatta, Bakhar, Thatta,
Hala, Sukkur, Shikarpur, Hyderabad, Karachi, Multan and others as rows they "would take". They are
converted here. **Every attachment below is place-level (or person-level) rather than
site-specific**: the book names the place or the person and attaches no shrine, tomb, saint, ʿurs,
fair or custodian to it, except where the bullet says otherwise. A toponym with no matching row is
in the second section. The join is `shrine_index.tsv`'s `location_short` via `toponym_map.txt`.

- **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**, **shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar** (the book's "Sehwan", "Sewistan", "Seahwaun", "Sewwan", "Sevensteere (Sehwan)", "Seuestan"): **place-level attachment for Sehwan, the most heavily named archive place in the book after Bhit.** The material is economic, military and administrative throughout.
  - **Territory, revenue post and battlefield.** The Indus narrows "in uncultivated country in the territory of Sehwan", "a spot notorious for pirates, as we know from Withington and from the East India Company letters of the seventeenth century factory" (p. 83, folio 74); Manrique's boat reached Sehwan two days after the pirate attack, "where the custom house was passed `�i�hout` `trq.uble[text-layer?]`" (p. 84, folio 75); "Below. Sehwan the `_country` was once more `cultivat�d[text-layer?]` and inhabited" (p. 84, folio 75); Aurangzeb's divisions nearly cut Dara Shikoh off "in a river fight near Sehwan" (p. 87, folio 78) and "the Imperial army and fort at Sehwan" is where he "managed successfully to nm [text-layer?] the gauntlet" (p. 22, folio 13). The chronology entry is "River -War. at· Sehwan." (p. 200, folio 191). Ahmad Shah Durani encamped at "Sewistan" in his threat of invasion (p. 39, printed 30) and Ghulam Shah fought "the Khosas of Sewistan" (footnote, pp. 39–40).
  - **Indigo, cotton and weaving.** "Sehwan: was noted for being the centre of the indigo growing district, but it w.p.s [text-layer?] also noted as a weaving centre" (p. 50, printed 41); "Indigo growing was concentrated mostly in Middle Sind-the territory of Sehwan, Bubak and Sann, and Sehwan itself were the chief areas of production" (p. 106, folio 97); a cotton place on or near the Indus (p. 98, folio 89); one of the factors' "chief centres of production", "`Nasarpiir[text-layer?]`, Sehwan, Kandiaro, Darbelo, Rohri or Bakhar" (p. 113, folio 104). "The Tarikh-i-Masiimi mentions the fertility of the Sibi-Sehwan area where cotton plants grow as large as trees so that men could pick the cotton mounted" (p. 128, folio 119).
  - **Population, houses, canal and wine.** "ro,ooo[text-layer?] in Sehwan and Mirpiir" (p. 126, folio 117); "From Karachi Bunder to Sehwan on the western bank of the river all the dwellings except those in the immediate vicinity of the Indus are of this construction" (p. 125, folio 116); the Arul canal "takes a westerly and southerly course round the country to Sehwan" (p. 139, folio 130, footnote 2); grape wine "' `angiiri` `[text-layer?]`' made of grapes at Hyderabad, Sehwan and `Shikarpiir[text-layer?]`" (p. 147, folio 138); "the Nahmurdi or `Niimria[text-layer?]` near Sehwan who mustered 300 horsemen and 7,000 foot" (p. 151, folio 142).
  - **A madressa cluster, which is the nearest this place-level material comes to an institution.** Of the six Talpur-era madressas, three are at or near Sehwan: "at Sehwan,- Trippat near Sehwan, }\hohra[text-layer?] north of . Sel!wan[text-layer?], Matiar�[text-layer?], Mohar · or- Walhari near Umarkot and Chothiyari on the Nara river" (p. 225, folio 216). **The place names there are bare and no shrine, tomb, saint or observance is attached to any of them.**
  - **The Sehwan mujawars.** Named in the same clause as the two places where the descendants of Ali refugees settled — "Lakhiari (Lak aliavi) and Matiari (Mat aliavi)" (p. 167, folio 158, quoting Elliot). **This is the one occurrence of shrine servants at Sehwan outside the Lal Shahbaz material above.**
  - **The shrine scenes Sorley condemns.** "the mendicant extortion and shrine scenes at Sehwan" stand immediately before the sentence that makes Shah Abdul Latif the true measure of Sindhi Islam (p. 169, folio 160). **The chunk-011 notes record them as part of "these ebullitions of popular and incredible fanaticism"; no saint and no site at Sehwan is named in them.**
  - **Note on "Sewistan".** The book uses it as a sarkar and district name — "the divisions in Sind" are "Tattah, Hajkan, Sewistan, `Nasarpiir[text-layer?]` and Chakar Hala" (p. 150, folio not printed, footnote 1); "the districts of Bakhar and Sewistan were granted him as fiefs" (p. 151, folio 142); "the summer heats are moderate except in Sewistan" (p. 119, folio 110); "Rel, a pleasant village of Sewistan" (p. 173, folio 164); indexed "Sewistan, 29, 30 and n, l 10[text-layer?], 141n[text-layer?], 142, 164, 2 10[text-layer?]" (p. 439). **The book nowhere states that Sewistan is Sehwan** — **(not stated)** — **and the equation is not made here.** See the Doubts list.
- **gurdwara-dash-mesh-pita**, **gurdwara-pehli-patshahi-jind-pir-sukkur**, **kalka-cave-temple-asthan-of-kalka-devi**, **sadh-belo-sadh-belo-island-temple**, **sant-baba-asudaram-darbar-panno-aqil** (the book's "Sukkur", "Old Sukkur", "S. district"): **place-level attachment for Sukkur, and it is the one place-level bullet in this book that carries a described shrine, a dated inscription and an annual river observance.**
  - **Khwajo Khizr / Zinda Pir and his shrine on an island in the Indus at Sukkur.** "Khizr, or Elias, was supposed to have drunk of the fountain of the waters of life and believed not to die till the last trump. Khwajo Khizr was for this reason called in Sind Zinda Pir (The Living Pir) . **He had a shrine on an island in the Indus at Sukkur.** He was the patron saint of waters and rivers and hence had come to be regarded as the god of the river Indus" (p. 178, folio 169). Sorley lists "the strange cult of Khwajo Khizr" among Crooke's categories and singles it out: it "attained a peculiar religious signifi­cance of its own, linked up with the worship of the river Indus and supported by Muslim and Hindu alike" (p. 177, folio 168). Fuller in chunk 019: "**Amongst the chief of these is Al-Khadir, who drank the waters of immortality. In Sind under the name of Khwajo Khizr this saint has always exercised a great power and has in popular superstition become somehow identified with the spirit of the waters and, by an easy transference of thought, with the deity of the river Indus.**" (p. 301, folio 292); "In Baliich[text-layer?] poetry the Khwajo is identified with the Indus and is represented as an old man dressed in green" (p. 301, folio 292; the green clothing also at p. 256, folio 247); "**The Darya­panthis[text-layer?] of Sind are believers in the sanctity -of[text-layer?] the River God or the God of Waters, and the belief affects Hindu and Muslim alike.**" (pp. 301–302, folios 292–293). "Burton quotes a hymn in honour of the Indus where the river is addressed as Khwajo Khizr (History of Sindh, p. 327)." (p. 302, folio 293). The index agrees across three headwords: "Khwajo Khizr, 168, 169, 247, 292, 293" (p. 436); "Zinda Pir (Khwajo Khizr) 169, 292" (p. 441); "(Khwajo Khizr) I68[text-layer?], I69[text-layer?], 247, 292" inside `Indus` (p. 435).
  - **A dated building and a chronogram — the only dated structure in the book apart from the poet's tomb.** "In the mosque of Khwajo Khizr at Sukkurthere[text-layer?] is an inscribed stone dating from A.D. 952-3 which reads : ' Know, that when this fabric was raised / Khwajo's waters encompassed it about. / This pleasing hemistich Khizr wrote : " In the court of God the date is found." '" (p. 178, folio 169, footnotes citing "Raverty : Mihran of Sind, op . cit . , p. 49 1[text-layer?]." and "ibid., p . 492 .").
  - **An annual raft-floating to the river god, with a named locality.** "' The Mazaris untied a boat from the ferry and let it float in the Khwaj a's[text-layer?] waves.'2 As it stands this might be a description of the annual floating of the rafts to the river god that takes place on the Indus at Sukkur near Din Belo." (p. 256, folio 247, footnote 2 citing "ibid . , p. 73."). **"Din Belo"[text-layer?] has its own index headword, "Din Belo, 247" (p. 434), so the spelling is the book's settled form and not a one-off.** A longer printing of the same Baluchi song appears at p. 301 (folio 292): "' The Mazaris untied a boat from the ferry and let it fl.oat[text-layer?] into the Khwaja's waves. Round featherless arr.ows[text-layer?] and four-feathered arrows were all mixed together : the Khwaja himself will remember that battle.'" — **"in" at p. 256 against "into" at p. 301, from the same page of the same source, and the second printing drops the Sukkur raft gloss entirely.**
  - **The mason of the Bhit mausoleum came from Sukkur.** "ornamented by a well-known mason of the time, one Idan from Sukkur" (p. 185, folio 176).
  - **Town, trade and war.** The imperial army learned of Dara's flight on reaching Sukkur (p. 86, folio 77); the Bakhar garrison "bombarded the towns of Sukkur and Rohri" (p. 88, folio 79); Sorley argues Manrique's garden-and-grove description "is truer `o` Rohri than `o` Bakhar" and that "what Manrique calls Bakhar may really have been ' Old Sukkur '" (p. 83, folio 74, footnote 1); "Sukkur was beginning to show signs of progress" by the annexation (p. 100, folio 91); one of the towns with "large communities of weavers" and, in Salbank, "' consisteth most of weavers and diers which serve the country round about '" (p. 102, folio 93); "Von Orlich in 1842 found that it contained no more than 6 ,ooo[text-layer?] people as against 8 ,ooo[text-layer?] in Rohri" (p. 127, folio 118); one of three districts of Baluchi settlement (p. 255, folio 246); "the old Shikarpiir Collectorate (in Sukkur district)" and "the portion named Moghuli, comprising Suk�ur[text-layer?], Bakhar · and Shikarpfir[text-layer?] and its dependencies" (p. 164, folio 155, footnote 3). **At p. 203 (folio 194) "Sukkur" occurs only as an example of an Anglicized spelling, not as a reference to the place** — **weaker than a bare toponym, and flagged as such.** Indexed "Sukkur, I I[text-layer?] and n, 2 1[text-layer?], 2 7-9[text-layer?], 74n, 77, 79, 91, 93, I I B[text-layer?], 1 69[text-layer?], 247, (Old Sukkur) 74n, (S. district) I35n[text-layer?], 246" (p. 440).
  - **The Zinda Pir collision, unresolved.** The chunk-012 notes name two rows for this saint — `gurdwara-pehli-patshahi-jind-pir-sukkur` ("Jind Pir", located in the index "Near the Sukkur–Rohri (Lansdowne/Ayub) railway bridge", i.e. the island site) and `darbar-ghamkol-sharif-zinda-pir` (Kohat, Khwaja Muhammad Qasim, d. 1999) — and refuse both: "**The chunk's Zinda Pir is a river saint at Sukkur with a tenth-century mosque, which cannot be the Kohat shrine on any reading of the text; but the chunk never uses the word 'Jind Pir', never mentions a gurdwara, Guru Nanak or Nanakpanthi custodianship, and calls the site a 'mosque'.**" **The material is attached here place-level, to all five Sukkur rows, and NOT merged with either named row as a site identification.** The Kohat row is untouched. See the Doubts list.
- **gurdwara-pehli-patshahi-jind-pir-sukkur**, **sadh-belo-sadh-belo-island-temple** (the book's "Rohri", "Roree", "Roori"): **place-level attachment for Rohri.**
  - A cotton place (p. 98, folio 89); Salbank's 1609 report, "' `Roree[text-layer?]` is a town of husbandmen and painful people who deal also in merchandise, as cotton cloth, indico and other commodities and are a peaceable people to deal with all '" (p. 102, folio 93), and again in 1605 "said Rohriwas[text-layer?] full of ' painful people '" (p. 134, folio 125).
  - Bombarded by the Bakhar garrison (p. 88, folio 79); "8 ,ooo[text-layer?]" people in 1842 against Sukkur's 6,000 (p. 127, folio 118); "Rohri was considered the garden of Sind ' and the gardens do actually extend w�thout intertnissi.on[text-layer?] '" (p. 129, folio 120) and "Rohri was surrounded with gardens" (p. 137, folio 128); Von Orlich's giant jowari seen "iri[text-layer?] Upper Sind north of Rohri" (p. 129, folio 120); one of the production centres the factors toured (p. 113, folio 104).
  - **The one religious datum.** "The chief centres of learning were Tatta, Matiari and Rohri, places famous for tlw[text-layer?] residence of Sayids and holy . men attached to tombs, mosques and shrines" (p. 218, folio 209). **The attachment there is generic — "tombs, mosques and shrines" — with no named shrine at any of the three.** And "Memoirs of Shikarpoor : the Syeds · of Roree· and · Bukkur" (p. 190, folio 181) treats a Sayyid lineage as an administrative subject alongside states, tribes and ports; **no shrine is named** — **(not stated)**. Indexed "Rohri, nn[text-layer?], 2 1[text-layer?], 74n, 79, 89, 93, 104, 1 18[text-layer?], 128, 209" (p. 439).
- **gurdwara-sach-khand-sahib**, **khatwari-darbar-shikarpur**, **sant-bhagat-kanwar-ram-temple-chak** (the book's "Shikarpur", "Shikarpiir", "Shikarpfir", "Shikarpoor", "Shikarpore", "Siccarpore", "Sicc.a Poer"): **place-level attachment for Shikarpur.**
  - **Foundation and conquest.** Founded 1616 by "the `Daiidpotras[text-layer?]`, a weaver tribe who had founded it after a conflict with the numerous tribe of Mahars then powerful in Upper Sind and still represented in the ruling house of `Bahawalpiir`"[text-layer?] (p. 38, printed 29); "1605 Rise of the Daiidpota[text-layer?] in Shikarpiir[text-layer?] and Upper Sind." (p. 200, folio 191); `Yar Muhammad Kalh6ro`[text-layer?] "took Shikarpur from the Daudpotras about 1701" (p. 38, printed 29) and "Defeat of the Daiidpota[text-layer?] in Upper Sind" is entered under 1701 (p. 201, folio 192). **The two foundation dates, 1605 and 1616, are given in different chunks of the same book and are not reconciled.**
  - **Trade and banking.** An annual merchant traffic to Tatta, extinguished by revenue-farming oppression (p. 62, folio 53); "the Patans had slipped by the Marattas into `Sicc.a`[text-layer?] Poer near Scindy", 16 December 1759 (p. 66, folio 57); "Karachi, Hyderabad and `Shikarpfir[text-layer?]` had all become important towns" by the annexation (p. 100, folio 91); "the great business of `Shikarpiir[text-layer?]` with Khorasan had not developed" under the Mughals and Kalhoras (p. 101, folio 92), the Khorasan trade being "financed by the `Shikarpiiri[text-layer?]` Hindus" (p. 101, folio 92); by 1842 it "was the centre of an indigenous banking organization that financed much trade in Sind, `Balfichistan[text-layer?]`, Afghanistan and Central Asia" (pp. 103–104, folios 94–95). Publishers: "Messrs. Pokardas & Sons, the well.:.known · publishers of Shikarpiir [text-layer?], Sind" hold the copyright in the text Sorley uses (p. 7, folio vii).
  - **Town and population.** Masson in 1826: "' The houses of the principal Hindus are distinguished by their loftiness and extent,'" but the town "' suffering from the usual inconvenience of narrow and confined streets ; nor is too much attention paid to cleanliness . . . The bazaar is extensive and well supplied . . . The town is surrounded by numerous gardens, flourishing mangoes, mulberries, bananas, melons and other fruits'" (p. 124, folio 115, footnote 2) — **the only religious datum there is the wealth of "the principal Hindus"; no temple, darbar or saint is named**; 25,000 inhabitants in Burnes's 1828 estimate (p. 126, folio 117); one of the three towns to which "the chief concentration" of population shifted from Tatta "between 1740 and 1 800[text-layer?]" (p. 127, folio 118); "to the north and north-west of Shikarpur there is a large tract of country which at one season is entirely under water" (p. 137, folio 128); a wine town (p. 147, folio 138). Administrative records: "Memoirs of Shikarpoor" (p. 190, folio 181), "Report by Maj or Goldney on Shikarpfir Collectorate" (p. 164, folio 155), the 1853 "Reports on the districts lately resumed from Meer Alli Moorad in Sind … in the Shikarpore Collectorate" (p. 190, folio 181). Indexed "Sliikarpiir[text-layer?], II[text-layer?] and n, 21, (Sicca Poer) 53, 5 7[text-layer?], 91 , 92, 94, 1 1 5 n[text-layer?], u7[text-layer?], u8[text-layer?], 1 38[text-layer?], 155n" (p. 440).
- **bhagnari-mandir**, **darya-lal-mandir-darya-lal-sankat-mochan-mandir**, **jhollay-lal-mandir**, **shree-ratneshwar-mahadev-temple-karachi**, **shri-laxmi-narayan-mandir-native-jetty-bridge**, **shri-panchmukhi-hanuman-mandir-karachi**, **shri-swaminarayan-mandir-karachi**, **shri-varun-dev-mandir**, **shrine-of-abdullah-shah-ghazi**, **shrine-of-pir-mangho**, **shrine-of-qalandar-baba-auliya** (the book's "Karachi", "Carachee", "Carrachee", "Crotchy", "Kurrachee", "Kurachee", "Karachi Bunder"): **place-level attachment for Karachi, eleven rows.**
  - **Why the port rose.** "It is to this desire on the part of the Afghans that Karachi owes its first importance as a port and centre of trade" (p. 58, folio 49); the residue of the Shikarpur trade "now centers at Crotchy where there `rrout`[text-layer?] to and from is pretty clear of the Princes dominions" (p. 62, folio 53); "By this time Karachi was an important port" (p. 103, folio 94).
  - **Shipping and fisheries.** The Imam of Muscat's agent lay there "with two large vessels buying unrefined saltpetre" (pp. 67, 68); the loading point for patchuck shipped to Bombay (p. 70, folio 61); "The centre of the sea-fishing industry was Karachi, and the coastal area" (p. 109, folio 100); "The modern sea fisheries of Karachi and· the Sind coast" (p. 109, folio 100).
  - **Population, and a communal composition.** "15 ,000 in Khairpiir and Karachi" in 1828 (p. 126, folio 117); "The population of Karachi in 1813 by actual enumeration … was about 13,000, which was more than one-half greater than it had been in 1809, and **of these the majority were Hindus**" (p. 127, folio 118).
  - **Revenue — the book's most heavily documented tax site.** Thirteen fishing cesses (p. 158, folio 149); the Kedywaree River[text-layer?] incident (p. 158, folio 149); Pottinger's figures for the ijareh customs farm in 1809 and 1816 (p. 159, folio 150); "in Karachi several Baliich tribes were exempt from taxation" (p. 170, folio 161); with Hyderabad, a district with "no fewer than fifty-six sets of rules governing the payment of clearance charges" (p. 141, folio 132). The Collector of Customs, Karachi, in 1847 is John Macleod (p. 158, folio 149, footnote 3).
  - **Schooling.** Captain Hart's 1840 account of Karachi schooling is quoted in the book, cited "Government of Bombay Records. Selections. New Series, XVII, Part I, p. 2 16[text-layer?]. ." (p. 225, folio 216, footnote 1).
  - **Housing.** Named once as the southern end of Pottinger's *wandh* zone, "From Karachi Bunder to Sehwan on the western bank of the river" (p. 125, folio 116) — **given there as a port, not as a town**. Indexed "Karachi (rise of) II[text-layer?] and n, 2 1[text-layer?], 91, 94, 1 1 7[text-layer?], 1 1 8[text-layer?] ; 49, (Crotchy) 53, 55, 60, (sea-fishing) 100 ; 149, I50[text-layer?], 2 I 6[text-layer?]" (p. 436). **No shrine, temple, tomb, saint, ʿurs or custodian is attached to Karachi anywhere in this book** — **(not stated)**.
- **prahladpuri-temple**, **shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya**, **shrine-of-hafiz-muhammad-jamal-multani**, **shrine-of-hazrat-shah-ali-akbar-shah-ali-akbar-shamsi**, **shrine-of-shah-rukn-e-alam**, **shrine-of-shah-shams-ud-din-sabzwari**, **shrine-of-shah-yusaf-gardez**, **shrine-of-syed-musa-pak** (the book's "Multan"): **place-level attachment for Multan, eight rows — and this is the one place in the book where tombs are explicitly attached to the city without a single one being named.**
  - **The proverb, which is the bullet's reason for existing.** "There was a Persian proverb about Multan that applied -equally to Sind that it consisted of **heat, dust, beggars and tombs**." (p. 166, folio 157). **The proverb distinguishes none of the eight rows; under ruling (a) it attaches to all of them, place-level.**
  - **The governorship.** Aurangzeb was "himself a Governor of Multan during 1648-50, had resided in Sind for part of the time 9f [text-layer?] his Governorship" (p. 22, folio 13); "Governor of Multan until 1652" (p. 54, folio 45); "in `1648-5�[text-layer?]`" (p. 98, folio 89); "became Governor of Multan in 1648" and "During Aurangzeb's residence in Sind as Governor of Multan (1648-52)" (pp. 151, 143; folios 142, 134); "the days of Aurangzeb's Governorship of Multan (1648-52)" (p. 169, folio 160, footnote 2); the governorship gave him "authority over the territory of Tatta then supplying saltpetre to the East India Company at Laribunder" (p. 105, folio 96); "A.urangzeb[text-layer?] Governor of Multan and Sind" in the chronology (p. 200, folio 191). **The end-date is printed as 1650, 1652 and "1648-5�[text-layer?]" in different places and is not reconciled.**
  - **Multan as the province Sind belonged to.** "Sind consisted of two sarkars (divisions) of the `siibah[text-layer?]` (province) of Multan" (p. 150, folio not printed); "Upper Sind at the period of the sack of Delhi by Nadir Shah formed part of the sfibah[text-layer?] of Multan" (p. 164, folio 155, footnote 3).
  - **The Governor of Multan executed the Kalhora ancestor.** Of Adam Shah Kalhoro, the Governor of Multan "had him put to death. The result was to canonize him and make his name more potent than ever." (p. 37, printed 28); his son Nasir Muhammad "went some years later to Multan, pleaded for forgiveness and obtained the royal mercy and an amnesty… about the very end of the seventeenth century" (p. 38, printed 29). **This is a canonisation by execution at the hands of Multan's governor, and Sorley narrates it in his own voice and attributes it to no source.**
  - **Trade, banking and manufacture.** A trade point (pp. 50, 53); "its easy connexion with Lahore and Multan, two very flourishing towns at this period of India's history" (p. 97, folio 88); "silk manufactured goods from `Bahawalpiir[text-layer?]` and Multan" among imports (p. 101, folio 92); a weaving centre (p. 102, folio 93); "he must have been one of the merchants called Multanis, who have given their name to an efficient banking business, men whose names are still current in the indigenous banking and commercial transactions of this portion of Asia" (p. 113, folio 104); "conveyed them by way of Multan to Turkistan and Khurasan" (p. 248, folio 239).
  - **Politics and religion.** Dara's "flight down the river from Multan to Bakhar" (p. 87, folio 78); "Ranjit Singh master of Multan" (p. 202, folio 193); "the early dynasties of Multan and Mansura" (p. 247, folio 238); "the country that lies in the Indus valley south of Multan" (p. 256, folio 247); and, on the Ismaili century, "' In the beginning of the eleventh century Mahmud of Ghazni found both Multan and Mansiirah[text-layer?] in the occupation of this Ismaili sect '" (p. 174, folio 165, quoting Ray) — **chunk 016 gives the same event with Mansura alone (p. 248, folio 239) and the two printings differ.** Indexed at length under "Multan" (p. 438).

Rauf confirmed this is meant to be heavy. The book names Lahore only as a trade node, a political capital and a standard of urbanity, and attaches no shrine, tomb, saint or observance to it anywhere.**
  - **Trade and route.** A trade point (p. 51); the upstream terminus of the Indus route (p. 97, folio 88); "its easy connexion with Lahore and Multan, two very flourishing towns at this period of India's history" (p. 97, folio 88); a weaving centre (p. 102, folio 93); the export channel for the higher-priced indigo, "i e_ produced at Bayana but exp.orted through Lahore" (p. 106, folio 97, footnote 3); the terminus of Multani banking, Navaldas being "a reliable man of business capable of financing commerce between Sind, Multan and Lahore" (p. 113, folio 104).
  - **Urbanity and fabric.** Among "such cities as Ahmedabad, Lahore, Agra, Delhi, Tatta and Surat" whose "glowing accounts … did not apply to the villages" (p. 95, folio 86); "Only Tatta could then offer comparison with towns like Lahore and Agra" (p. 122, folio 113); "Lahore in the beginning of the seven­teenth century seems to have been built largely of brick" (p. 123, folio 114).
  - **War and politics.** Manrique's point of departure downriver (p. 82, folio 73) and the site of his grenade demonstration "in a garden at Lahore where they created a great sensation" (p. 83, folio 74, footnote 4); Dara's cannon foundry (p. 88, folio 79); the place Khalilullah Khan "returned in disgust to" and where the eunuch Primavera "was killed shortly afterwards" (p. 89, folio 80); Dara pursued "from Lahore into Sind" (p. 87, folio 78); "Sikhs become masters of Lahore" in the chronology (p. 202, folio 193).   - **Its foundation is dated twice and the two do not agree.** "the foundation of Hyderabad ( 1 764) [text-layer?]" among Ghulam Shah's achievements (pp. 39–40) against "1 768[text-layer?] Foundation of Hyderabad." in Appendix V (p. 202, folio 193). **Both are kept.**

- **swami-dharmdas-darbar-larkana-kennedy-market** (the book's "Larkana", "La:rkana", "Llrkana"[text-layer?], "Chandookah"): **place-level attachment for Larkana, and it carries the book's best-documented non-archive shrine.**
  - **The tomb of Shahul Muhammad Kalhoro, six miles from Larkana.** "Lieutenant ]ames[text-layer?] in his account of the Chandookah. Describing the tomb of Shahul Muhammad KalhOro[text-layer?], who became a saint on his death at a battle fought at Fatehpiir[text-layer?] six miles from Larkana" (p. 168, folio 159). **Sainthood follows from death in battle; no miracle is claimed.** The offerings are hung by "those who consider that their prayers for any particular blessing have been heard through the mediation of the saint" (p. 168, folio 159) — **an intercession rationale stated in the pilgrims' own terms, which the book reports without endorsing and for which it gives no instance of a granted prayer.** **The place name collides with the archive row `dargah-fateh-pur-sharif` (Gandawah, Jhal Magsi District, Balochistan) and must NOT be merged with it.**
  - **The sacred millstones of Dato Khuhawar and the unnamed pir's tomb, also from James's account of the Chandookah.** "' Near Shahdadpiir[text-layer?] there is a pair of large millstones in a garden about four feet in diameter. It is on the banks of the Dato canal, so called from Dato Khuhawar, a man renowned for his wealth and the canals dug by him, as for his un­bounded liberality. . He was Chandookah' s Hatim of Tayyi. It is related of him that no one passed his door unfed and the above mill­stones are now considered sacred … **They are approached with bare feet and the precincts are kept in cleanliness and good order. There is a tomb over the remains of a celebrated pir on poles, round which are seen the heelropes of horses …**'" (p. 177, folio 168, footnote 2). With it, two attached beliefs: **the inexhaustible flour** — "we are told that God was so pleased with his piety and liberality that even if a handful of grain was thrown in the supply of flour was equal to all demands" (p. 177, folio 168) — and **the tree taboo** — "If a person breaks a branch of the tree which overshadows the tomb he is supposed to be doomed to meet with some mishap" (p. 177, folio 168). **The pir is not named and the site is located only as "Near Shahdadpiir[text-layer?]", which is not Larkana; the attachment here is through James's Chandookah account and is place-level.** Dato Khuhawar is indexed on his own, "Dato Khuhawar, 168" (p. 434), at a page the index's own ranges put inside "(Hindu practices intermingled) 168, I69[text-layer?], 290" (p. 438). The canal that gives the site its name is measured in the Chandookah canal table — "Datah . . . . 4:2;000[text-layer?] 2I[text-layer?] . IO[text-layer?] 9 4" (p. 198, folio 189) — **but the book does not make that identification.**
  - **The district.** "In his account of the Chandookah (the present Larkana area) Lieutenant James noted that the villages were of good size and nearly all possessed a bazaar" and "Chandookah contained no fewer than three hundred and ninety-two of such villages" (p. 125, folio 116); "These areas were chiefly in the Larkana district of Upper Sind, called Chandookah in the old records" (p. 136, folio 127); "The Chandookah and the Lar were the two great rice producing areas just as they are today" (pp. 127–128, folios 118–119); "Shah Baharo, a minister of Nur Muhammad Kalhoro was an enlightened man who excavated several canals in the Chandookah" (p. 138, folio 129, footnote 1) and "had the management of the Chandookah and commanded a division of `ro,ooo[text-layer?]` men, according to James" (p. 151, folio 142, footnote 1) — **"Shah" there is part of a minister's name as the book gives it and no shrine, tomb or saintly role is attributed to him**; "The grass is very plentiful and luxuriant, particularly towards Larkana" (p. 124, folio 115, footnote 2); Yar Muhammad "had overrun Kandiaro and Larkana by `l7II`[text-layer?]" (p. 38, printed 29); a district of Baluchi settlement (p. 255, folio 246); paper manufacturers and "the `llrkana`[text-layer?] river" (pp. 158, 164, 168); indexed "Larkana, 27, 29, 1 16[text-layer?], 1 27[text-layer?], 13on[text-layer?], 149n, 246" (p. 437).

- **kalka-cave-temple-asthan-of-kalka-devi** (the book's "Alor", "Alar"): **place-level attachment for Aror.** "The ruins of Mohanjodaro and Chanhudaro, **the dead sites of Alor** and Brahmanabad, an occasional stupa, the ruckle of decay that is Tatta" (p. 20, folio 11, footnote 1); "their territory included.Alar[text-layer?] iri[text-layer?] the north" of the Sumra, per the Tarikh-i-Tahiri (p. 247, folio 238); indexed "Alor, I m[text-layer?], 238" (p. 432). **The chunk-001 hedge named this row explicitly — "nothing of `kalka-cave-temple-asthan-of-kalka-devi`, whose archive location is Aror" — and it is converted here, place-level.** **No temple, shrine or deity at Alor** — **(not stated)**.
- **kalat-kali-temple** (the book's "Kelat", "Kelit"[text-layer?], "Kelatwards"[text-layer?]): **place-level attachment for Kalat.** Din Muhammad `Kalh6ro`[text-layer?]'s tribe "fled to Kelat" (p. 37, printed 28); "the Brahui Mir Abdullah Khan, chief of Kelat, killed attacking the Kalhora" (p. 38, printed 29); "Kelat" as a political actor (p. 39); and, inside the verse, "the h91y[text-layer?] men · who , tru�ged[text-layer?] · Kelatwards[text-layer?]" (p. 358, folio 349); glossed "Kelit[text-layer?]  A State in Baluchistan bordering on Sind ." (p. 431, folio 422); indexed at pp. 431 and 436. **The chunk-021 hedge flagged this as marginal because the text "does not merely name the place, it has holy men travelling to it", which is closer to an observance than a bare mention — but nothing at Kelat is named: no temple, no shrine, no tomb, no saint, no deity, no fair and no custodian** — **(not stated)**. **Also note the printed form is "Kelat", not "Kalat".**
- **shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar** (the book's "Lakhi"): **place-level attachment for Laki / Lakhi, and it is the weakest attachment in this section.** "the `Kalmani.[text-layer?]` `Baliichis[text-layer?]` near Lakhi who mustered 20,000 horsemen" (p. 151, folio 142) — **named only as the locality of a Baluch sept, with no shrine, saint or tomb** — **(not stated)**. **The chunk-010 hedge is explicit that "even as a toponym the chunk's 'Lakhi' may be the Lakhi hills or range rather than the settlement" and said "Do not attach this page to that row"; the attachment is made here because ruling (a) is in force, and the doubt is carried with it rather than resolved.** **"Lakhi" also occurs as a named branch of the Indus in Del Hoste's list of branches (p. 137, folio 128); that occurrence is a river, not a place, and is NOT attached.**
- **shaktipeeth-shri-hinglaj-mata-mandir** (the book's "Hinglaj"): **site-name attachment on one occurrence, inside a footnote Sorley rejects.** The place appears only in Gurbuxani's itinerary of the poet's travels: "Gurbuxani says that he visited the places of pilgrimage in Sind and Lakhpat, Gimar[text-layer?], J aisalmir[text-layer?], the hills on the western borders of Sind, La.hut[text-layer?], Lamakan, **Hinglaj**, Sabar Sakhi and even went to KabuJ[text-layer?] and Kandahar. But I do not know on what authoritv[text-layer?] he holds that Shah Abdul Latif went so far." (p. 182, folio 173, footnote 1). **Sorley's own text stops far short of the list — "It is true that he did not travel far" (p. 181, folio 172) — and the claim that the poet went there is Gurbuxani's, explicitly disbelieved.** **The join here is on the archive row's own name, "Shaktipeeth Shri Hinglaj Mata Mandir", not through `toponym_map.txt` and not through `location_short`; the book says nothing about a temple, a goddess, a festival or a custodian at Hinglaj** — **(not stated)**. Flagged for the Doubts worker.
- **amb-temples-amb-sharif**, **bhagnari-mandir**, **chandragup-baba-chandragup**, **katas-raj-temples**, **shahwala-teja-singh-mandir**, **shiv-mandir-chiti-ghati**, **shree-ratneshwar-mahadev-temple-karachi**, **umarkot-amarkot-shiv-mandir** (the book's "Shiv"): **person-level attachment under the many-row person rule — eight rows of which Shiva (Mahadev) is the principal figure.** The god is named once in the whole book, inside the poet's own verse, as an object of ascetic devotion: "they who �orship[text-layer?] Shiv / Wash. not their faces�[text-layer?] save it be with dust. / By dawri[text-layer?] they're settled by the road-way's side. / To .non.e[text-layer?] this �peech[text-layer?] they utter : ' We Adesis are.'" (p. 363, folio 354). **The chunk-021 notes recorded the deferred mapping in as many words — "several Shiva and Rama rows exist in the archive and none is triggered, because no site is named" — and the person rule now converts it.** **No temple, image, festival, place or custodian is attached** — **(not stated)** — and the attachment is person-level, not site-specific.
- **ram-mandir-saidpur-ram-kund-mandir** (the book's "Rama", "Ram"): **person-level attachment — the one archive row of which Rama is the principal figure.** Named twice, inside the poet's verse and in the same twelve-day ascetic calendar: "To strands / Of Rama somewise secretly the bands / Of their own lives they bound" (p. 359, folio 350), and "Ever Ram 'doth stay / WithD;i[text-layer?] their being's · self. . Fpr[text-layer?] holy_' pay[text-layer?] / · They roam midst people quietly." (p. 359, folio 350). **A Muslim poet naming Rama inside his own verse, with no temple, image, festival, place or custodian attached** — **(not stated)**.
- **darya-lal-mandir-darya-lal-sankat-mochan-mandir**, **jhollay-lal-mandir**, **shrine-at-odero-lal-udero-lal-teerath-asthan** (the book's "Udero Lal"): **person-level attachment under the many-row person rule — the three archive rows whose principal figure is Jhulelal / Uderolal / Daryalal.** The book names him twice, both times as an alternative name for the Sehwan saint: "the shrine of the Kalandar Shah Lal Baz (or Udero Lal) at Sehwan" (p. 168, folio 159), and the index cross-reference "Udero Lal (see Lal Shahbaz) 159" (p. 441). **The book therefore treats Shah Lal Baz and Udero Lal as one figure at one site, while the archive carries them as four rows at four places. No separate shrine, temple, teerath, festival, location or date for Udero Lal is given anywhere** — **(not stated)**. **This is a person-level attachment and must NOT be used to merge `lal-shahbaz-qalandar` with any of the three rows above.** The river-god cult in the Sukkur bullet above is a different figure in the book's own handling (Khwajo Khizr), though **the book attaches "the Living Pir" to both** — see the Doubts list.

## Cross-cutting material

### Fairs on saints' days, and the pilgrimage calendar

- **The book's one general statement about shrine fairs treats them as a commercial institution, and
  defers the list of places to another book.** "The oriental system of markets and fairs on saints'
  days at saints' tombs was well established and the `Gazetteer3 of 'Sind`[text-layer?] gives a long
  list of places where such fairs were held on regular days every year: In fact in this respect
  Sind's commerce was · on the old medieval system which had prevailed in England itself up to the
  time of the rise of the trading towns and the growth of the merchant gilds" (p. 98, folio 89), the
  whole citation being "See Gazetteer of Sind by Hughes." (p. 98, folio 89, footnote 3). **The system
  is "well established"; the fairs fall on saints' days at saints' tombs; they recur "on regular days
  every year". No shrine, saint, district, date or number is given, and the words *ʿurs*, *mela* and
  *langar* are not used** — all **(not stated)**. The framing is Sorley's own and it is an economic
  one: shrine fairs are a *stage of commercial development*, not a devotional practice.
- **The annual fair at Sehwan, cross-communal, with camping and Sufi singing.** The practice terms the
  book supplies are "saint-day", "the annual fair", "these holy days", "camped round the
  t<?mh[text-layer?]" and "singing the Sufi songs" (p. 299, folio 290); and separately, "whose shrine
  at Sehwan attracts a great gathering of both Mussulmans and Hindus every year in a strange mixture
  of worship ." (p. 257, folio 248). **Annual, both communities, "a great gathering" — no attendance
  figure, no month and no name for the event** — **(not stated)**. Place-level: **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**, **shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar** (the book's "Sehwan").
- **A monthly, not annual, pilgrimage cycle at Tatta, named in Sindhi and twofold.** From the
  *Tarikh-i-Tahiri*: "' Each month … has several ids for them, the first Friday after the new moon
  they call in the Sindh language ' ' `Mah-pahra JU.ma`[text-layer?] ''" — the crowd fills the Makali
  mountain "that there is scarce room to stand" — and "' the similar festival of Mah-pahra
  `Siimar`[text-layer?], or the first Monday in each month, a great day for making pilgrimages '"
  (pp. 145–146, folios 136–137). What pilgrims did: "' The pleasure of visiting each other induces
  them to go in large parties taking with them abundance of sweet river water and food such as they
  can afford. The day is spent in amusements and visits to the shrines.'" (p. 146, folio 137)
  **Provisioning is self-supplied; the book names no langar, no distribution and no custodian** — all
  **(not stated)**.
- **A rain-triggered, cross-communal gathering at a tank on the same hill.** "When rain fell on the
  Kira-tal, a sweet-water tank on the Makali hill, men and women of all classes, Hindus and
  Mussalmans, crowded there from morning till night, cooked their meals and feasted." (p. 146,
  folio 137) **Triggered by weather rather than by calendar, and explicitly cross-communal.**
- **Saints' days as the pir's own audience day.** He "sat, as on a throne, upon his holy seat, himself
  the gadinishin before whom on saints' days the multitude came to offer obeisance" (p. 176,
  folio 167). **This is the only calendar anchor the book attaches to the pir's office.**
- **Tombs on saints' days as one of four occasions of contact with the outside world.** Sindhi society
  knew "little of the outside world except what could be seen of it at markets and fairs, in the
  gatherings of strangers at tombs on saints' days or in the company of fellow pilgrims" (p. 214,
  folio 205); "There was the same sort of rriovirig[text-layer?] about to faiis[text-layer?] · and on
  pilgrimages" (p. 213, folio 204).
- **The annual raft-floating on the Indus, given twice with different particulars and not reconciled
  by the book.** At Sukkur: "the annual floating of the rafts to the river god that takes place on the
  Indus at Sukkur near Din Belo[text-layer?]." (p. 256, folio 247). In the Khwajo Khizr passage:
  "Muslims offered him oblations of lamps and flowers. These were launched upon the river on
  Thurs­day evenings in the fifth month of the solar year. At this time there was held the festival of
  the Bera (raft) when a raft was launched upon the river in honour of him." (p. 178, folio 169)
  **A weekday, a month of the solar year, a festival name and two offering types from one passage; a
  locality from the other.** Place-level: **gurdwara-dash-mesh-pita**, **gurdwara-pehli-patshahi-jind-pir-sukkur**, **kalka-cave-temple-asthan-of-kalka-devi**, **sadh-belo-sadh-belo-island-temple**, **sant-baba-asudaram-darbar-panno-aqil** (the book's "Sukkur").
- **The four named festivals of the Sind year, and their economic effect.** "the two `fds`[text-layer?],
  Ramzan and Bakri" for Muslims and "`Hc'>li`[text-layer?] and Diwali" for Hindus (p. 145, folio 136),
  kept with "abandon and merriment … when money was spent freely and much display was made with `·new`
  clothes" (p. 145, folio 136). Elsewhere the teacher's three gift-days are named differently — "the
  three great ids of Bakr, Fitr and Ba.rat[text-layer?]" (p. 225, folio 216). **"Bakri" / "Bakr",
  "Ramzan" / "Fitr" and a third, "Ba.rat"[text-layer?], with no cross-reference by the book itself.**
- **Holi as a stoppage and as a cash transaction, twice.** In the factory diary: "13th March . 1764.
  This being the Hooly Festival of the Gentoos occasions a delay of five or six days, no business of
  any kind being attended to during this time of riot, madness and confusion" (p. 75, folio 66); and
  Hamilton in 1699 "(which he misspells Wooly !)": the crowd "dance through the streets to pipe, drum
  and cymbals. The women with baskets of sweetmeats on their heads distributing to everybody they
  meet…and if they get into houses of estimation they make foul work with their oil… but on giving a
  present of rose water or some silver coin they are civil enough to keep out of doors" (p. 145,
  folio 136). **A documented payment made to end a religious procession's attentions.** Sorley reads
  the factory clerk's hostility as corroboration: "That Holi (a Hindu festival) was celebrated with
  gusto in Tatta is proved effectively from the Company's records" (p. 75, folio 66) — the reading is
  his; the record says only that business stopped.
- **The Diwali present as a standing institution of the export trade.** Ovington, quoted: "' that the
  banyans, once a year which is their great festival season called the Diwally time, have a custom,
  much like that of our New Year gifts, of presenting the President and Council, the Minister, Surgeon
  and all the factors and writers with something valuable either in `j ewels`[text-layer?] or plate,
  atlasses or other `silk.s`[text-layer?] according to the respect which they owe to every man's
  station…'" (p. 112, folio 103); Sorley then makes it evidence of shared profit (p. 113, folio 104).
- **Id observed inside the factory as a payment to servants.** "'I gave the sepoys,
  hamrnals[text-layer?] etc. , factory servants their usual enams'" (letter of 19 February 1764,
  p. 74); "At Id the company rewarded its servants with special buksheesh and the whole place went on
  merry holiday" (p. 146, folio 137). **"*Enam*" is used here for a customary festival gratuity to
  servants, not for a land grant.**
- **Long-distance Hindu pilgrimage out of Sind, with a crowd figure.** "more than 2,000 persons
  including men, women and children who came `irom`[text-layer?] the direction of Tatta with their
  idol which they carried in a rich palanquin on their way to visit the great idol of the pagoda at
  Tirupati" (p. 146, folio 137, footnote 2). Sorley reads the ability to make the journey as a welfare
  index: "From this it may be inferred that conditions under the Moghuls were not really so bad as a
  casual reader might infer from the diatribes of foreign observers" (p. 146, folio 137, footnote 2).
- **Pilgrimage inside the Risalo is calendared as a twelve-day ascetic round, not as an ʿurs.** Day
  eleven: "There was fulfilment on th' eleventh .day / Of all Bairagis practised. Holy shrines / The
  Jogis visited and where there shines / The seat of worship" (p. 360, folio 351); day twelve: "All on
  · the pilgrimage had wishes set. / The Jogis won full honour, these who met / Their
  giirii-master[text-layer?] and are back again" (p. 360, folio 351). **"had wishes set" is the
  transactional element; the guru is met on the return, not at the shrine. The book does not say what
  the twelve days are and gives them no name** — **(not stated)**.
- **Pilgrimage in the mystical chapters is a metaphor, not a practice.** "there. is ,in[text-layer?]
  ·�im[text-layer?] the urge fo[text-layer?] be. a . pilgrim or warider�r[text-layer?] 'in -
  seq.rch[text-layer?] of the lost home" (p. 275, folio 266). **Keep this beside the Sehwan fair and
  the Makali hill: the same book uses the word in two registers.**

### Offerings, votive deposit and the distribution of shrine income

- **The fullest description of shrine practice in the book, and its only itemisation of offerings**,
  quoted by Sorley from Lieutenant James on the tomb of Shahul Muhammad Kalhoro: "' The doorway of
  this court and of the mausoleum is hung with native offerings of those who consider that their
  prayers for any particular blessing have been heard through the mediation of the saint. These
  consist chiefly of iron bells and strings of shells. The tomb itself is covered with rich' silk and
  brocade, the offerings of the wealthier visitors. . . . On descending from the edifice a party of
  miserable mendicants, whose duty it is to keep the courts in a state of cleanli­ness, clamorously
  demand a fee, a portion of which is retained by them and the remainder distributed to the poor
  surviving descendants of the family. '" (p. 168, folio 159) **Four institutional facts in one
  passage: votive offerings for answered prayer, graded by the giver's wealth; a silk-and-brocade
  covering over the tomb; a body of sweepers with a customary fee; and a fixed split of that fee
  between the sweepers and the saint's descendants. No ʿurs, mela, langar, number or date is attached
  to this shrine** — all **(not stated)**.
- **The pir's income, stated as the first of his four working functions.** "The pir received the
  offerings of his murids, settled their disputes often very satis­factorily, and led the prayers on
  solemn occasions." (p. 176, folio 167) **An income, a jurisdiction and a liturgy in one sentence.**
- **A votive deposit still in place at a Kalhora tomb.** "Yar Mahomed `Kalh6ro`[text-layer?] directed a
  number of clubs to be suspended in front of his tomb as a memorial to his having conquered the
  country with clubs alone. The clubs remain suspended to this day in front of the tomb at Khudabad."
  (p. 37, printed 28, footnote 1, quoting "Report by Captain Preedy. Bombay Government Records,
  No. XVII, New Series, p. `67r`"[text-layer?])
- **A conditional vow with a livestock-health clientele and a physical ex-voto, near Shahdadpur.**
  "There is a tomb over the remains of a celebrated pir on poles, round which are seen the heelropes
  of horses. For whenever an animal in the neighbourhood is afflicted with disease the owner prays at
  the tomb and on the recovery of the animal offers the heelropes in gratitude for the saint's
  assistance." (p. 177, folio 168) With the access rule at the same site: "They are approached with
  bare feet and the precincts are kept in cleanliness and good order." (p. 177, folio 168) **The book
  does not say who maintains them** — **(not stated)**.
- **Cross-community votive offering, raised by Sorley as a problem and left general.** "How are we to
  explain the extraordinary phenomen.on[text-layer?] of Hindus visiting Mussulman shrines and making
  votive offerings at them, and the general easy-going character of Sind orthodoxy ?" (p. 175,
  folio 166) **No shrine, place or figure is given** — **(not stated)**.
- **Tribute paid at Sehwan by both communities.** "where Hindus as well as Muslims paid tribute to the
  demands" (p. 168, folio 159), in a passage whose subject is importunate Sayids and mendicants rather
  than a shrine treasury. Place-level: **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**, **shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar** (the book's "Sehwan").
- **Prayer at a saint's tomb as maritime insurance, from inside the Risalo.** "A prayer is said at the
  tomb of t;tie[text-layer?] saint for the saf�ty[text-layer?] of the ship at sea" (p. 267,
  folio 258) — **the tomb is unnamed.** In the translated verse the same transaction is addressed to
  the Prophet: "They clomb aboard and to the Lo�d[text-layer?] / The Prophet vows they vowed well"
  (p. 334, folio 325); and "For thee, 0 Master of mine, my mouth / Thousands of vows sent forth."
  (p. 328, folio 319).
- **A prescribed recitation at a tomb with a stated benefit.** "Repeat at Lord Muhammad's tomb / The
  holy words of God and doom, / That succour come to thee ( anon.[text-layer?]" (p. 335, folio 326).
  **The words themselves are not given** — **(not stated)**.
- **Intercession bought in public, without a shrine.** "The Sayi:d[text-layer?] riding in state on his
  noble horse has his stirrup leather seiz_ed[text-layer?] in 'entreaty by persons craving for his
  help or intercession" (p. 267, folio 258); the same gesture on the pir's tour, "The devout pressed
  forward for the honour of touching his stirrup leathers" (p. 176, folio 167). **Two goods named,
  "help" and "intercession".**
- **The one offering word in the poems is domestic and fails.** "The cowries which you offered me /
  Have, mother, useless proved to be." (p. 399, folio 390) — **"cowries" is the only named currency in
  that chunk, the offerer is the mother, and no shrine is in view.**
- **Charitable feeding as the road to sanctity — the nearest thing in this book to a langar.** Of Dato
  Khuhawar, "a man renowned for his wealth and the canals dug by him, as for his un­bounded
  liberality. . He was Chandookah' s Hatim of Tayyi. It is related of him that no one passed his door
  unfed" (p. 177, folio 168). **Canal-building and an open table produce the sacred millstones; the
  book never uses the word *langar*.** The other shelter institution is horticultural, not shrine-run:
  garden grants "in the hands of all classes, from infiuential[text-layer?] Baluch jaghirdar or Afghan
  puttadar down to the poorest faquir or gosain… Al1[text-layer?] were, however, more or less public
  benefi.ts[text-layer?] created by private cost and labour. It was owing to them that the wayside
  traveller did not lack shelter, shade, refresh­ment and repose. '" (p. 165)
- **Alms as an institution the poems regulate.** "They beg in alms what five poor Jtngers[text-layer?]
  seize" (p. 359, folio 350); "The will of. God their order is : th.ey[text-layer?] b eg[text-layer?]
  / F�>r[text-layer?] not one single mor�el[text-layer?] else" (p. 362, folio 353); "Those·wlio[text-layer?]
  find alms in hunger are they / Who take for their incense the dust" (p. 364, folio 355). **Against
  the counter-rule, stated twice: "Men who are slaves to food / Are Jogis false in name" (p. 362,
  folio 353) and "Thds�[text-layer?] Swamis . who first thing at morn, / �aith[text-layer?] the Sayid,
  made question for food, / Such Swamis are shameless" (p. 364, folio 355). Begging is licit; asking
  for food is not.**
- **The singer's takings as a religious transaction.** "go beg there on your way / And- gifts that are
  of value win."; "So, d�ily,[text-layer?] earnest effort make before the Giver's door."; "No
  othe�[text-layer?] .business has a singing stroller. but to -sing" (all p. 338, folio 329) — **daily
  attendance at a door, gifts as income, and the door is God's.**
- **Alms as an arm of the penal system, not of charity.** "A particularly revolting part of this
  punishment of m·�le­factors[text-layer?] was the custom of making prisoners beg for their
  maintenance and any surplus over. bare maintenance that the prisoners succeeded in getting in alms
  became the perqui�ite[text-layer?] of the kotwal." (p. 160, folio 151)

### The pir as an office: murshid, murid, gadinishin

- **The office described feature by feature.** "The Sayid who was also a pir exercised as a murshid
  over his murids an almost regal power. The pir was usually connected with some shrine. He was
  invested with the pag (turban) . He sat, as on a throne, upon his holy seat, himself the gadinishin
  before whom on saints' days the multitude came to offer obeisance." (p. 176, folio 167) **Four
  institutional terms in the book's own spelling — pir, murshid, murid, gadinishin — plus the
  investiture object, "the pag (turban)", and the calendar anchor, "saints' days".**
- **The tour of the murids as a fiscal and ceremonial circuit, with an armed retinue.** "When he went
  on tour to visit his murids his progress was one of almost regal magnificence. He rode a gaily
  caparisoned camel attended by bands of followers usually dressed in some kind of uniform and armed
  with an assortment of arms." (p. 176, folio 167) Sorley's name for the resulting political geography
  is his own: "The countryside became in this way the home of an immense number of these petty
  imperia" (p. 176, folio 167), and his mechanism is medieval-European: "The pir had thus a spiritual
  power which was always tending to take temporal forms, exactly as happened in the most influential
  days of the papacy in medieval Europe." (p. 176, folio 167)
- **The murshid–murid tie as the hinge of Sindhi dynastic politics.** "The Kalh6ra[text-layer?] were
  the murshids of the Talpiirs[text-layer?]. It was because of the hold they thus possessed over the
  Baliich[text-layer?] shepherd tribe that the Kalh6ra[text-layer?] were able to use the
  Talpiirs[text-layer?] for their own political aggrandizement. When the Kalh6ra[text-layer?]
  commanded as murshids, the Talp.iirs[text-layer?] as murids had to obey." (p. 176, folio 167) And
  the limit: "Actually, it was an outrageous act by a Kalhorn[text-layer?] which shocked the public
  opinion of the day and led to the displacement of the Kalh6ra[text-layer?], the murshids, by the
  Talpiirs[text-layer?], their murids. The abuse of veneration could not, therefore, be carried beyond
