  the limits which the public conscience would tolerate." (p. 176, folio 167) **The book never says
  what the outrageous act was** — **(not stated)**. **Set beside the other account of the same
  relationship, where the Baluchis are simply hired: "Their political importance, however, dates from
  the time when they were called in by the Kalh6ra[text-layer?] in the eighteenth century to stiffen
  the military forces at the disposal of the Prince of Sind." (p. 255, folio 246) Two mechanisms, one
  relationship, and the book does not connect them.**
- **Discipleship as recruitment of state power, at the origin.** The Baluchi migration is described as
  a prince summoning his own disciples: "two of his mountaineer Murids, Mirs Aliido [text-layer?] and
  Masiido [text-layer?], to emigrate from their barren hills and settle in the low country" (p. 41,
  quoting Burton).
- **The ruling house's own sanctity as an asset.** "the tendency to pamper holy men became more
  pronounced. under the Kalhora who were typical Sindhis and themselves invested with a halo of
  sanctity as murshids, in view of their descent from the sainted Adam Shah." (p. 169, folio 160)
  Burton's sceptical version of the same descent: the Kalhora "were originally Channo Sindhis and
  therefore converted Hindus… their `Shajar6`[text-layer?] or genealogical tree was pronounced by the
  learned to be a complete failure. Upon this they sent a messenger to copy the documents in the
  possession of the holy men of `Sehrah Khatibah`… and then became undoubted descendants from Abbas
  and Murshids." (p. 34, printed 25, footnote 2, quoting Burton, *History of Sindh*, p. 410) And a
  British officer's phrase, "the sacred tribe of KalhOra[text-layer?]" (p. 169, folio 160, footnote 3).
- **The murids as a constraint on their pir.** The poet "had intended to make· the pilgrimage to
  Kerbela in the last few years of his life, but was dissuaded from doing so by the insistence of his
  followers that he should not desert them" (p. 183, folio 174). **Followers as a claim on the saint,
  not only as a constituency** — **bhit-bhit-shah**.
- **Murid recruitment as a competitive threat between shrine families.** The poet's "fame … was
  becoming known and was attracting to him a number of murids", and this is what "arouse[d] the
  jealousy of the orthodox ' hierarchy '" and set "the Sayids of Matiari" against him (p. 182,
  folio 173). **A documented instance of one family's growth being resisted by another** —
  **bhit-bhit-shah**; place-level for the opposing family: **shrine-at-odero-lal-udero-lal-teerath-asthan**, **shrine-of-makhdoom-nooh-hala** (the book's "Matiari").
- **The initiation vocabulary, from the doctrinal side, and it does not match the pir/murid pair.**
  "the Talib, or seeker, attaches himself to a murshid or teacher and becomes a Salik, or traveller,
  passing through periods of service, love, seclusion, knowledge, .ecstasy[text-layer?], to truth,
  union with God (wasl) and fana (extinction of individuality in the divine)" (p. 293, folio 284,
  footnote 1 citing "The Faith of Islam, pp. 92 -4[text-layer?] ."). **Five technical terms glossed
  and a six-stage progression; the book does not reconcile this vocabulary with the pir–murid one.**
- **And the four stages of the path, with Sorley's own hedge attached.** "there are four, **nasut or
  humanity, where there is obedience to the tenets of orthodox religion : tarikat, or the way in which
  the forms of religion give place to spiritual adoration : aruf or knowledge, where inspiration
  begins to take the place of plain belief : and hakikat or truth, where the Siifi[text-layer?] attains
  union with the divine.**" — prefaced by "The stages on this progress are very differently described
  by different writers" (pp. 292–293, folios 283–284).
- **The doctrine that makes tomb devotion intelligible — the nearest thing in this book to a theory of
  why shrines exist.** "Then finally there prevails throughout Islam the belief that **the saints are
  not dead, but merely sleeping. They are within their tombs. Their tombs have become their houses
  which they can leave and whence make journeys. They are accessible to prayer and grant
  "f,;lvours[text-layer?]. This is the solid foundation of the extraordinary devotion paid in Sind to
  the tombs of the deceased saints, pirs and holy men. This devotion is bound up with the system of
  personal religion that exists in the relatfon[text-layer?] of pir to murid. The disciple must obey
  his spiritual director. From this have risen many darwish orders commonly called after the name of
  the saints who founded them. Thus present practice and continued tradition combine to weld the whole
  into a coherent system, the roots of which go deep into the past.**" (p. 302, folio 293) **The
  damaged word sits on the load-bearing term, "favours".**
- **Tomb veneration named as a distinct phenomenon.** "Another form that popular belief took was to
  carry to extremes what is little less than worship of the dead." (p. 175, folio 166)
- **And the counter-doctrine, printed in the book's own translated verse and never reconciled with
  any of the above.** "There.'s none of pir or. prophet . They do sin / Wh9[text-layer?] worship pirs,
  and Worse than these are they / Who worship idols", with "Of none bu�[text-layer?] God is· any,
  worship meet" (p. 363, folio 354) — **two pages after the same section has the Jogis visiting holy
  shrines (p. 360, folio 351). Both are kept.**

### The religious classes as a privileged order

- **The list, and the claim that they were the real power.** "The Sayids, the Pirs, the Pirzadas, the
  Kalandars, the Siifis[text-layer?] and a host of others were the real power in the land. No ruler
  could afford to neglect them and all were afraid of them. The reason lay in the peculiar psychology
  of the Sindhi Muslim peasantry." (p. 166, folio 157) **Five categories distinguished, with
  "Kalandars" and "Siifis"[text-layer?] as classes separate from pirs and pirzadas.**
- **They are classed with the landholders as one of the privileged orders.** "The privileged classes
  under Moghul and Kalhoro were (omitting the Government officials) the jaghirdars, the zamindars, the
  Sayids, fakirs, pirs and holy mendicants, and the · most powerful Baliich[text-layer?] tribes which
  could not be antagonized." (p. 163, folio 154); Sorley's own transition, "There remained the much
  more formidable privileged class of the religious ' hierarchy '." (p. 166, folio 157) Pottinger's
  three-class version puts them first: "the first was the privileged class consisting of Amirs,
  Chiefs, Baliichis, Sayids and religious mendicants" (p. 170, folio 161).
- **The quantitative estimate — the single most citable economic figure in the book on religion.**
  "MacMurdo estimated the expense of the ecclesiastical establishments at one-third of the gross
  revenue enjoyed by the state." (p. 167, folio 158) **No date, no source page and no method is given
  for MacMurdo's estimate** — **(not stated)**.
- **A count of shrines, reported at second hand.** Dr Burnes: "' There is no country in Asia or rather
  on earth that is so perfectly priestridden. It is sai4[text-layer?] to possess no fewer than one
  hundred thousand tombs of saints '" (p. 167, folio 158). **The figure is spelled out in words, so it
  survives the text layer; but it is Burnes reporting hearsay, "It is said to possess".**
- **Sayid privilege described as social immunity, and as a pull factor for immigration.** Dr Burnes:
  "' The meanest wretch who can boast his origin from the holy stock enjoys a place in society higher
  than temporary rank can bestow. Among the crowds who came to me for medicine all readily gave place
  to the Sayids. No person under any provoca­tion would dare to abuse or strike one unless at the risk
  of being torn to pieces by the populace, and, in consequence of the privil�ges[text-layer?] and
  immunities they enjoy, they flock from all the neighbouring centres into Sind where they are a
  constant tax on the poorest of the inhabitants. '" (p. 167, folio 158)
- **Recruitment of the saintly class from outside Sind, as a continuous eight-century process.**
  "Throughout the Muslim domination of Sind for eight centuries there was a continual recruitment of
  the saintly families from outside." "They entered Sind at various times from the Mussulman lands
  that lie between Arabia and India. They were a heterogeneous mixture of many races." (p. 173,
  folio 164) Sorley makes this one of two causes of their power (pp. 173–174, folios 164–165). Restated
  for the thirteenth century: "From the thirteenth century onwards there was a great immigration into
  Sind of learned and religious-minded men from the centre of the Muslim world." (p. 302, folio 293)
  And Arnold, quoted, ties immigration to the shrines themselves: "the saints whose tombs are still
  venerated throughout all ·parts of Muslim India were for the most part of foreign extraction"
  (p. 219, folio 210, footnote 1 citing "' Islam in India ' in Hastings' Encyclopaedia of Religion and
  Ethics, Vol. V, pp. 895-7.").
- **Sayid endogamy and the inflation of the status.** "They would not allow their daughters to marry
  Muslims of less dignified stock than their own. If either of the parents was a Sayid, the children
  must be called Sayids too. There was in this way a considerable watering-down of the original Arab,
  Persian, Turk and other Asiatic strains in them." (p. 173, folio 164)
- **Sayids as a military category as well as a religious one.** "The best soldiers under the Moghul
  system were foreigners, Pathans, Sayids, Moghuls and Rajpiits." (p. 150, folio not printed) And as
  *tribes* holding jaghirs: the jaghirdar list ends "Kh6sas[text-layer?], Kh6khars[text-layer?],
  Abras, Katians, Sayids, Pirs and Pathans" (p. 165).
- **Mendicancy as organised tribute-taking, with a tariff and a method.** Sir Alexander Burnes: "' The
  mendicants in Sind are more numerous than in any other country in Asia. They can scarcely be called
  beggars, for they levy tribute in crowds and by threats of great arrogance. One of the most common
  is to sit all day on the house top and repeat the sacred name " Allah " as many thousand times as
  the tongue can utter it.'" (p. 168, folio 159) Captain Del Hoste: they "went about wearing sword and
  shield, and riding horses, declaring that they would not move till given sums of money varying from
  two or three to one hundred and fifty rupees. They enforced attention by blowing horns, ringing
  bells, beating drums and shout­ing at th� pitch[text-layer?] of their voices." (p. 168, folio 159)
  The set piece: "' O Feringhee Elchi, give me a hundred rupees or I will stay here all day ; and, if
  you do not, I will stay here all day ; and; if you do not, I will tell Mir Murad Ali Khan not to
  comply with anything you purpose, whereas if you make me your friend, you shall get whatever you
  want.'" (p. 168, folio 159; footnote: "Quoted by Burnes : op. cit., p. 76 from Del Hoste's MS.
  Journal.") **The clause "I will stay here all day" is printed twice.** Where the demand was pressed:
  "The streets of the towns were infested by such persons whose importunity and insolence made heavy
  demands upon the superstitious poor under thr.eat of all kinds of penalties." (p. 168, folio 159)
- **The itinerant's circuit and his takings.** "the Kalandars and so-called Sufi beggars who wandered
  far and wide over Sind taking their toll of the superstitious country people" (p. 300, folio 291).
  **A class, a territory, a payer and an extraction verb; no sum, rate or occasion** — **(not
  stated)**. **Note that "Kalandars" here is a class of wandering beggar while "a Kalandar"[text-layer?]
  one page earlier is Lal Shahbaz's own designation (p. 299, folio 290), and the book does not
  distinguish them.**
- **A market in religious authority, stated outright.** Burton, quoted: "' Tasuwwuf[text-layer?] ',
  says Burton, ' under the native governments was as formidable a political engine as most of the
  secret confrater­nities[text-layer?] recorded in history . . . Even among the mild Sindhis a noted
  Pir (religious ·s�perior[text-layer?]) formerly might safely order one of his Murids or disciples to
  murder an enemy. **Yet the native princes encouraged it partly from superstition and partly because
  the price of every Pir was well known to them** ' (History of Sindh, pp. 203-4)." (pp. 299–300,
  folios 290–291), with Sorley's own gloss, "The Pirs held · positions of great personal authority."
  (p. 300, folio 291) **Four facts: tasawwuf as a political engine; a pir's power to order a killing;
  state encouragement; and a known price per pir. No sum, no prince, no transaction** — **(not
  stated)**.
- **The learned and the devout are separated from the mob, twice.** "Many of these fanatical
  exhibitions by an ignorant and unlettered populace met with the disapproval of learned and cultured
  Muslims, who, however, were powerless to interfere and knew better than to try to do so." (p. 168,
  folio 159) And, on the popular side: "**Popular Sufism in fact degenerated into something that the
  mullas viewed with distaste.**" (p. 298, folio 289)
- **Against the Sayids, the observed piety of ordinary Muslims, recorded in the same pages.** Sir
  Alexander Burnes: "' Nothing more arrests the notice of a stranger on entering Sind than the severe
  attention of the people to the forms of religion as enjoined by the prophet of Arabia. I have
  observed a ·boatman quit the laborious duty pf[text-layer?] dragging the vessel against the stream
  and retire wet and covered with mud to perform his genuflexions. In the smallest villages the sound
  of the Muazzin, or crier, summoning true believers to prayers may be heard…'" (p. 167, folio 158);
  Sorley's comment: "All this is admirable and shows a deep and profound respect for the tenets of
  faith. But the practical results were not so encouraging." (p. 167, folio 158)
- **What the money went on, in a hostile contemporary summary.** Crowe, writing shortly after the
  Kalhora fell: "' There is no zeal but for the propaga­tion of the faith, no spirit but in celebrating
  the Id, no liberty but in feeding Sayids and no taste but in ornamenting old tombs.'" (p. 167,
  folio 158) **"feeding Sayids" and "ornamenting old tombs" are named as the two forms of religious
  expenditure; this is the only ʿid named in that chunk.**
- **A proverb that attaches tombs to a city.** "a Persian proverb about Multan that applied -equally
  to Sind that it consisted of heat, dust, beggars and tombs" (p. 166, folio 157). Place-level: **prahladpuri-temple**, **shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya**, **shrine-of-hafiz-muhammad-jamal-multani**, **shrine-of-hazrat-shah-ali-akbar-shah-ali-akbar-shamsi**, **shrine-of-shah-rukn-e-alam**, **shrine-of-shah-shams-ud-din-sabzwari**, **shrine-of-shah-yusaf-gardez**, **shrine-of-syed-musa-pak** (the book's "Multan"). **The proverb distinguishes none of them.**
- **Sorley's verdict on the whole arrangement, in his own voice and with his own distaste.** "The
  extent to which holy men or reputed holy men were reverenced and pampered in Sind is almost outside
  the bounds of credibility. This was the least satisfactory feature of the Sind social structure."
  "The facts admit of no dispute and are extremely unedifying." (p. 166, folio 157) **The hedge "or
  reputed holy men" is his, and it carries the genuine/spurious distinction on which his defence of
  the poet later turns.**

### Endowment: wakf, inam, jaghir and grants to mosques and holy men

- **Wakf named as a category of tenure the British formally recognised at annexation.** "On the
  annexation of Sind the British recognized three kinds of these alienations, first, jaghirs which had
  been held from antiquity, and were aUowed[text-layer?] to continue hereditarily, second, puttadari
  holdings of old standing, and third, **grants of a religious or charitable character (wakf)**."
  (p. 164, folio 155) **Who held them:** "The religious and charitable grants were at the disposal of
  mosques, Sayids, pirs, fakirs and holy men of all descriptions." (p. 164, folio 155)
- **How old the practice is said to be, and when it swelled.** Elliot, quoted: "' Much · of the
  conquered land was, during the whole course of the Arab occupation; liberally bestowed upon sacred
  edifices and institutions as wakf or mortmain, of which some remnant dating from the earlier period
  is found even to this day. '" (p. 167, folio 158) Sorley's own periodisation: "The great granting of
  privilege to reiigious[text-layer?] persons began to be pronounced under the Kalhora, as distinct
  from the ordinary Muslim system of wakf which had existed from the original Arab conquest." (p. 170,
  folio 161) And the earlier Arab form of the same instrument: "' The land was held by _Arab[text-layer?]
  garrisons supported by grants of land. '" (pp. 247–248, folios 238–239, quoting Ray)
- **Inam in perpetuity to the Sayids under the Kalhora — the book's one explicit statement of a
  religious class holding endowed land for ever.** Burton, quoted: "' Many are learned men, much
  respected by the commonalty in spite of discrepancy of belief. Under the Kalhorn[text-layer?]
  dynasty they became possessed of large landed estates granted as inam in perpetuum.'" (p. 173,
  folio 164)
- **Non-land endowment: a fishing right granted to a mosque, and the verb Sorley chooses.** "At
  Moghulbhin the fishing rights had to be granted to a mosque and there were many other instances of
  valuable alienations in favour of the priestly caste." (p. 169, folio 160) **"had to be granted" —
  the endowment is presented as extracted, not donated.**
- **A religious establishment holding a customs farm outright.** Pottinger: "' they have great
  establishments at Sehwan, Halakhandi and other places where they have the absolute right of
  collecting customs and transit dues. '" (p. 169, folio 160) Place-level: **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**, **shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar** (the book's "Sehwan").
- **The measured scale of alienated land, though wakf is not separated out.** "The area of land held
  in jaghir and inam was estimated in 1847 by the Commissioner in Sind at one-tenth of the total area
  of the Government land." (p. 164, folio 155; footnote 1: "Bombay Government Records. Revenue
  Arrangement Sindh, Vol. 203.") **The figure covers jaghir and inam together; the wakf share is
  (not stated).**
- **Jaghir defined, and distinguished from inam by the sanad — the mechanism that matters for any pir
  family's holding.** "the j aghirdar held his land either entirely free or on payment of a small rent
  and was entitled to collect for himself the land assessment. Jaghirs were granted principally to
  chiefs who provided armed retainers, to officials who discharged public duties, to menials who
  performed domestic services and to courtiers who had won the favour of the court." (pp. 163–164,
  folios 154–155) "A j aghir, however, differed from a plain gift (inam) in that it involved
  obligations which terminated with the death of the j aghirdar and the sanad was called in and had to
  be regranted to a successor at the will of the ruler. An inam was a free irreclaimable gift."
  (p. 164, folio 155)
- **Puttadari, and how old the grants were.** "The new cultivators wishing to secure a light
  assessment petitioned their sovereign . . . and obtained puttas (leases)…"; "The puttadaris, in
  Upper Sind especially, are held mostly by Afghan settlers dating from the time of Nadir Shah, and
  even earlier .from the days of the Arghiins[text-layer?] and .Tarkhans." (p. 164, folio 155) Talpur
  practice on inherited grants, from Mr Ellis: "' the Talpiirs, though liberal in their grants to their
  own immediate retainers and dependants, were not remarkable for generosity in confirming grants · of
  their predecessors.'" (p. 164, folio 155) And Sorley's own first-person testimony as a serving
  official: "But I am myself familiar with at least one case of a claim of puttadari by a Hindu
  descendant of an Amil in the service of the . Government." (p. 164, folio 155)
- **Endowment as the funding of religious education.** "The chief rrianner[text-layer?] in. which
  education was encouraged was by .the_ 'bestowal[text-layer?] in wa:kf[text-layer?] ofinam�[text-layer?],
  j aghirs[text-layer?] and grants to mosques and religious foundations. · But' rn;my[text-layer?] of
  these exhibitions of favour were the resuit[text-layer?] of 'the religiosity of local rulers and not
  of the enlightenment of the D�lhi[text-layer?] · throne" (p. 221, folio 212); and, more compactly,
  "The chief support of the literary groups came from revenues granted in the form of, inams to
  mosques and to ' maktabs ; attac�ed[text-layer?] to",m9sques[text-layer?]" (p. 222, folio 213).
  **Three instruments named, and the initiative located with local rulers.**
- **The Mughal state's own religious-grant department, named, with Sorley's disclaimer attached.**
  "the proport�on[text-layer?] of the state revenues actively devot€'.d[text-layer?] to
  edU<;a:tion[text-layer?] through ·t\le[text-layer?] 'Department of Alms, administered .by the
  Imp�rl_al[text-layer?] _-A_lnioner[text-layer?], the . Sadr-us-Sadiir[text-layer?], was very small
  indeed" (p. 221, folio 212) — against which, in the administrative chapter, "We do not know if there
  was such a special officer as the Sadr-us-Sadiir, the Muntasib or the Beyutat" in Sind (p. 150, folio
  not printed). **The office that in Mughal practice handled religious grants is named twice and its
  presence in Sind is explicitly unknown** — **(not stated)**. And Sorley's flat negative: "There is
  certairily[text-layer?] no evidence that I have seen that would warrant the belief .that a single
  educational institution in Sind owed its origin to any act of an Emperor of Delhi" (p. 221,
  folio 212).
- **Land seizure by the colonial state, named as "resumption".** "Report on the District of Sahita-
  lately resumed from His Highness Ali Murad" (1853) and "Reports on the districts lately resumed from
  Meer Alli Moorad in Sind" (1 853[text-layer?]) (p. 190, folio 181). **The verb the records use is
  "resumed" and the party dispossessed is named.**

### Tax exemption and the fiscal privilege of the religious classes

- **The exemption list, the single most useful institutional document the book quotes.** Sorley's
  framing: "The length to which privilege werit[text-layer?] can be realized from the statement of
  Winchester in his topographical report on the city of Tatta and its environs. in 1839, where he says
  that the following tribes were exempted from taxes unless engaged in trade, in which case only half
  the ordinary duty and custom was exacted." The list as printed: "Sayids, Rajwi or Mahadvi, Sikhlai,
  Sufi, Meerukhi, Amirkhani, Shirazi, Bokhari, L6cli[text-layer?], Muila[text-layer?], Abbasi, Kazi,
  Moofti, Akhund, Jh6kia[text-layer?], Bakhali, Buttearah, Halalkh6r[text-layer?], Junkra, Juti
  {fishmongers)[text-layer?] and Brahmins, Sara.swat[text-layer?], Pokarna and Hussaini, and all
  mendicants and priests." Citation: "Bombay Government Records, Selections XVII, New Series, Part II,
  p. 2 73[text-layer?]." (p. 163, folio 154, footnote 1) **Twenty-three named categories plus a
  residual clause covering "all mendicants and priests", with a half-rate rule for those in trade.
  "Sufi", "Kazi", "Moofti" and "Akhund" stand as tribal or status names beside Brahmins and
  Saraswats.**
- **The general principle behind it.** The two defining characteristics of the revenue system are
  "first, that the state share of the agricultural assessment was taken in kind (ghalabuksh in the
  Moghul · records) and second, that taxation as a whole was extraordinarily comprehensive and
  `tOuched`[text-layer?] in some form or other all classes of the population except those exempted as
  . privileged." (p. 154, folio 145) **The exemption clause is stated as a general rule and the exempt
  are not named there.**
- **The religious classes appear on both sides of the fiscal relation.** The poll-tax collectors
  included "public officers, military men, religious personages like kazis, pirs, pirzadas and Sayids.
  Large zamindars had often the right to collect the sar shumari." (p. 156, folio 147) **Pirs and
  pirzadas as tax *collectors*, not merely as exempt persons.**
- **The base rate of that poll tax, and its confessional asymmetry.** "Men, women and
  chHdren[text-layer?] were. all taxed and the head of the family was res­ponsible for the amount
  which was one rupee per head per annum. Hindus paid no regular poll tax but were more than adequately
  taxed in other ways." (p. 156, folio 147) The exemption list from the poll tax itself is **severed by
  a chunk boundary**: "The exceptions for sar shumari were Baliichis, zamindars," (p. 155, folio 146,
  footnote continuing) — **two legible categories, and whether sayids, pirs, fakirs or shrine servants
  followed is (not stated).**
- **The Talpur extension of privilege, and the sentence that compresses the whole mechanism.** "The
  Talpiirs could do nothing to check this tendency. They added indeed to the number of the privileged
  classes by granting farms and showing extraordinary preference to the Baliichi tribes whom they were
  afraid to oppose." (p. 169, folio 160) And: "Sayids arid fakirs began to be treated with great
  respect, **which the taxation system acknowledged**, while the ordinary cultivating and pastoral
  classes, the true Sindhis, the Jats and camelmen, the fishermen and hunters, were fully
  e_xploited[text-layer?]." (p. 171, folio 162)
- **Sorley's structural verdict on the arrangement.** "the social condition of the country was due to
  the gradual disintegration of the Moghul · system influenced by the psychological attitude of
  com­plaisance shown by the Muslim populace in respect · of autocratic government and its reverence
  for holy men. In such circumstances the trading and commercial elements were crushed as .between ·
  the upper and nether millstones. **A quasi-theocratic state, dependent on an unsound division of
  labour of this kind, could have taken rto[text-layer?] other form.**" (p. 171, folio 162) **"A
  quasi-theocratic state" is his own term for eighteenth-century Sind**; the same word recurs at p. 19
  (folio 10), "a peculiar brand of Islamic quasi-theocracy", and at p. 23 (folio 14), "the government
  had a strong theocratic basis with much virtual power in the hands of the priestly class of Sayids".
- **And his causal claim, which reverses a devotional account.** "The Kalhorn[text-layer?], weak
  followers of Moghul models, with less strength behind them and controlled by the fanaticism of an
  ignorant populace, were even more dependent on the existence of privileged classes. **It was the
  Kalhora's practice of deferring to the priestly class that led to the extraordinary reverence for
  pirs, Sayids and holy men, which may be said to have been the predominant characteristic of
  eighteenth century Sind.**" (p. 163, folio 154) **State weakness is the cause and popular veneration
  the effect.** The chain closes on itself at p. 166 (folio 157): peasant psychology → pir power →
  ruler deference → more pir power.
- **The one countervailing statement, and it is about the Talpur peace, not about religion.** "Under
  the `Moghnls`[text-layer?] and the KalhOra Sind was never at peace. Under the `Tfilpiirs`[text-layer?]
  it was. But the price of peace was the tyranny of a Baliich minority which the Moghuls had always
  endeavoured to suppress and the Kalh6ra unsuccessfully to conciliate." (pp. 151–152, folios 142–143)

### Architecture, building dates and the fabric of tombs and mosques

- **Mosques and tombs are the only substantial old buildings in Sind — and they are not old.** "Even
  the buildings of burnt brick (and these are nearly all mosques and tombs) have suffered from the
  assault of the river, the salinity of the soil and the rigour of a climate which for six months in
  the year is as hot as anything to be found in India and is cursed with dust storms" (p. 20,
  folio 11); "Few buildings exist in sufficient preservation to display the· civilization- of any
  century previous to the fifteenth and, with rare exceptions, the buildings that are now accounted
  old are generally not earlier than the seventeenth century and most are even late�[text-layer?] in
  date." (p. 20, folio 11)
- **Brick and stone were reserved for saints' tombs and the largest towns.** "Building was unimportant
  as most of the houses and habitations were made of mud, sticks, grass or branches of trees. **Only
  in the largest towns like Tatta and for the tombs of holy men was the use of brick and stone much ·
  indulged in.** The climate was so dry that mud houses lasted for a 'generation, and the poorer
  classes, just as they do today, lived in shacks and huts built of the cheapest material available.
  Sind is deficient in `hu,ilding`[text-layer?] · stone. It is, therefore, useless `loo.king`[text-layer?]
  in it for masterpieces of the mason's art." (p. 101, folio 92)
- **Glazed tile as the skilled trade that decorated them.** "The making of glazed tiles was, however,
  highly skilful and the products were used to ornament tombs and mosques." (p. 101, folio 92) **It
  stands inside a paragraph whose whole burden is that Sind crafts were primitive — flour milling, oil
  pressing and pottery all unchanged since Mughal times — so the tile trade is the named exception and
  its market is religious architecture.**
- **Public money went on tombs rather than on works of utility.** "But public money in those ·.days
  was used for the erection of costly tombs, mausoleums and palaces. `It.`[text-layer?] was not spent
  on what we now call public works of utility." (p. 122, folio 113) **Sorley says this of the Mughals,
  in the context of neglected irrigation; he names no tomb, no site and no sum** — **(not stated)**.
- **Mosques abound in the villages and are built of the same frail stuff as the huts.** Burnes: "' The
  villages of Sind on this side of the Indus are much inferior in `appearanca`[text-layer?] to those
  of Cutch and are entirely destitute of stone buildings and tiled roofs… They are for the most part
  collections of low huts comprised entirely of clay and thatch · **while even the mosques with which
  they abound are generally of the same frail materials.**'" (p. 123, folio 114, footnote 2, "p. 26")
  **"with which they abound" is the book's only statement about the density of religious buildings in
  Sindhi villages.**
- **The mosque at the centre of the village plan.** "The Sindhi village must in fact have reminded one
  strongly of the villages of early medieval Europe. **The village clustered round the mosque, the
  bazaar and `th.e`[text-layer?] head man's house**, and encircling these were the
  `landhis`[text-layer?] (or sheds) of the Sindhis…" (p. 125, folio 116)
- **Tatta's mosques in 1842, counted only as "a few".** Von Orlich: "' There are only a few mosques in
  the place which are built of stone and · painted white, but they have the same dirty and ruinous
  appearance as the town ; and there are no traces of the extensive trade which formerly was carried
  on . . . . Poverty and dirt everywhere prevail.'" (p. 103, folio 94) **No mosque is named.**
- **Two dated structures, and they are the book's only building dates for religious fabric.** "an
  inscribed stone dating from A.D. 952-3" in "the mosque of Khwajo Khizr at Sukkurthere[text-layer?]"
  (p. 178, folio 169) — place-level: **gurdwara-dash-mesh-pita**, **gurdwara-pehli-patshahi-jind-pir-sukkur**, **kalka-cave-temple-asthan-of-kalka-devi**, **sadh-belo-sadh-belo-island-temple**, **sant-baba-asudaram-darbar-panno-aqil** (the book's "Sukkur") — and the poet's own mausoleum, "An imposing tomb was built for his body by Ghulam Shah Kalh6ro[text-layer?] in 1754" (p. 183, folio 174), "ornamented by a well-known mason of the time, one Idan from Sukkur" (p. 185, folio 176), "erected at the expense of Ghulam Shah Kalhorn[text-layer?]" (p. 183, folio 174, footnote 1) — **bhit-bhit-shah**. **A ruler pays; a named mason decorates.** The earliest dated burial on the site precedes it by twelve years: "His father Habib Khan died in 1 742[text-layer?] and was buried in Bhit a few paces from where later in 1 754[text-layer?] Shah Abdul Latif's own tomb was erected" (p. 183, folio 174, footnote 1) — **bhit-bhit-shah**.
- **A shrine village founded ex nihilo by its saint, with its water works named.** Bhit is "a village
  of his own", built on sandhills, "in the true ascetic spirit of the religious eremite", and he
  "helped to build the village with his own hands", "close to the Kirar lake which was fed by the Ali
  Ganj or Pahar canal.." (p. 182, folio 173) — **bhit-bhit-shah**.
- **A tomb still standing on a rock as an acropolis.** Of Adam Shah Kalhoro: "His tomb today, perched
  on its rocky hill at Sukkur, is still a place of veneration and respect, and stands out prominently
  as an acropolis above the surrounding alluvial plain." (p. 37, printed 28) **No building date, no
  custodian, no ʿurs and no visitor figure** — **(not stated)**. Place-level: **gurdwara-dash-mesh-pita**, **gurdwara-pehli-patshahi-jind-pir-sukkur**, **kalka-cave-temple-asthan-of-kalka-devi**, **sadh-belo-sadh-belo-island-temple**, **sant-baba-asudaram-darbar-panno-aqil** (the book's "Sukkur").
- **No relics of learning survive at all.** "There are no relics of old seats of learning, no old
  libraries and no collections of ancient2 documents. The men of learning who in earlier centuries
  lived in Sind have disappeared , leaving behind them only trifling remains for the use of modem
  [text-layer?] scholarship." (p. 20, folio 11) Restated for Tatta: "if . such relics do
  stili.[text-layer?] exist, they must be· .bu�ied[text-layer?] in manuscripts which are in the hands
  of private persons ·and which have· not been published" (p. 218, folio 209). **A standing pointer to
  unpublished private manuscript holdings in Sind.**
- **Sorley's cause for architectural decline, offered as a likelihood.** "It seems likely that the
  `Baliich`[text-layer?] influence `wa�`[text-layer?] partly at least responsible for the decline in
  the standard of building" (p. 124, folio 115). **His hedge; carry it.** The best late-Kalhora
  building he can point to is a technique, not a monument — Pottinger's hollow-wall houses at Tatta,
  "three or four storeys high with `fiat`[text-layer?] heavy roofs" (p. 124, folio 115), which Sorley
  calls the standard of "the best buildings in Sind … towards the end of the `Kalh6ro`[text-layer?]
  rule."
- **Brick as salvage, and the inference Sorley draws.** "' The present houses of `Tattah`[text-layer?]
  are generally of mud but advantage has been taken of the quantities of bricks remaining, which are
  strewed about in every direction…'"; "it is probable that during `Kalh6ro`[text-layer?] days Tatta
  and the larger centres of population contained many pretentious buildings of brick which have long
  since disintegrated in the saline soil" (p. 124, folio 115).

### Mendicancy, the darwish orders and the material culture of the fakir

- **The two great orders and the four silsilas — the book's first and only list of orders.** "The Sufi
  fakirs of Sind belonged to the two great orders of Jelali and Jemali darwishes. They underwent
  courses of initiation which followed more or less the discipline of the Muslim darwishes about whom
  much recondite information can be gleaned from Brown's great work on ' The Dervishes '." (p. 299,
  folio 290) And: "**The Sind Jemali fakirs belonged to foµr[text-layer?] orde:rs[text-layer?], the
  Kadiri, the Nakshbandi, the Suhra­wardi[text-layer?] and the Chishti.** The shughls and
  huziirs[text-layer?] of some of these orders are described by Burton." (p. 300, folio 291) **A
  two-level taxonomy, "courses of initiation" as an institution, and two named exercise-types,
  "shughls" and "huziirs"[text-layer?]. The Jelali branch is given no sub-orders** — **(not stated)**.
  **The printed forms are "Kadiri", "Nakshbandi", "Suhra­wardi"[text-layer?] and "Chishti" and no
  normalisation has been made.** Note against this the book's earlier refusal to enumerate: Sufism
  "can be clearly distinguished from the many kinds of asceticism and other-worldliness… the latter
  chiefly in the orders of darwishes, eremites, and fakirs in a variety too compFcated[text-layer?] to
  receive attention here" (p. 284, folio 275) — **no silsila is named anywhere in that chapter.**
- **The Jelali fakir's dress and kit — the fullest description of a religious occupation's material
  culture in the book.** "The Jelali fakirs of Sind wore **a felt hat like a fool's cap called ' taj
  '**, **a coat of black wool with white threads like a shroud without sleeves called ' kafni '** and
  **a blanket of wool called ' godri '**. **Such persons were in reality professional beggars and part
  of their stock-in-trade consisted of** a ' tasbih ' or rosary, an ' asa ' or staff of ebony or
  blackwood, a , beragan '[text-layer?] or forked stick used to support the forehead for meditation or
  repose, ' dhaga ' or a girdle of black twisted wool, ' gano ' or black wool mixed with red strands
  used as a necklace, a ' gabri ' or ·wallet[text-layer?] to contain food and necessaries, and a tumbi
  '[text-layer?] or beggar's gourd used for collecting alms and as a drinking vessel." (pp. 299–300,
  folios 290–291) **Ten glossed terms, two economic characterisations in Sorley's own voice —
  "professional beggars" and "stock-in-trade" — and one named income mechanism, "collecting alms".**
  The same cloak and gourd return as a dismissal: "no . wandering[text-layer?] beggar clad in woollen
  cloak and clutching in his hand a hollow gourd" (p. 303, folio 294).
- **Zikr as the instrument that produced conviction in the laity.** "Another great influence in the
  development of mystical practice came from the ' remembering of God ' (zikr) , the great source of
  devotional exercise which was carried to exfremes[text-layer?] by the darwish orders. In fact in its
  more violent forms this practice of zikr developed into a species of autohypnotism used to produce
  the holy trance or ecstasy so prominent in the discipline of the mendiCant[text-layer?] orders.
  **It was precisely this form of emotional excitement which made the strongest appeal to the common
  man and convinced him of the divine inspiration and saintliness of the hqly[text-layer?]
  mendicant.**" (p. 301, folio 292) And the itinerant's own performance: "the itinerant fakir who
  calls on Allah with unwearied reiteration till exhausted he tinkles his bell while his mind wanders
  in amazed ecstasy" (p. 303, folio 294).
- **The darwish dance at Tatta, with a venue count and a suppression history.** From the
  *Tarikh-i-Tahiri*: "' Besides the shrine of the Shaikh of Shaikhs, Shaikh Patta, **there are (i.e.
  in Tatta) some ten or twelve other places where darwishes perform their dance.** These excitable men
  often work themselves into such a state of holy ecstasy that they cast them­selves[text-layer?] on
  the rocks of the mountain of Makali : but by the blessing of their learned doctors and teachers no
  harm befalls them. **This custom, however much opposed to the laws of Islam, has been transmitted
  from generation to generation and all attempts of wise teaGhers[text-layer?] and just governors have
  never succeeded in putting _a[text-layer?] stop to it.**'" (p. 298, folio 289, footnote 1 citing
  "Tiirikh-i-Tiikiri[text-layer?].   Elliot : I, pp. 2 72-4[text-layer?].") **A count of dance venues
  in one city, named performers, the physical form of the ecstasy, a protective blessing by "learned
  doctors and teachers", a legal objection, and repeated failed suppression by both clerical and state
  authority. The word "dhamal" is not used** — **(not stated)**. Sorley cites the same passage
  elsewhere as evidence that the critique was internal to Islam, not only European (pp. 168–169,
  folios 159–160).
- **A drug used specifically to produce religious ecstasy, and the class that used it.** "' kohi bhang
  ' from a kind of henbane, consumed by fakirs and mendicants to produce aberration of the intellect
  and induce ecstasy, a poison which was eaten and not smoked in the Chinese fashion." (p. 147,
  folio 138) **No order is named** — **(not stated)**.
- **The `daira` as a sited institution with a count, a clientele and a gift economy.** "The grog shops
  and ale houses of eighteenth century England had their counterpart in Sind in the form of ' daira '
  or -intoxicating drug dens, which were found outside all the larger towns, and the resorts of the
  hemp drinkers, frequented by the lower classes, a few Sayids and munshis and generally by the Jelali
  fakirs. Burton says that near every large town there were fifty or sixty such places, that the
  keepers of the dens received from the frequenters presents of money and clothes, and that they were
  accused of inducing young men to drink bhang." (p. 147, folio 138; footnote 2 "ibid., p. 402")
  **Three things matter here: the `daira` sited *outside* the towns; Sayids and Jelali fakirs named as
  clientele; and keepers receiving "presents of money and clothes" — a gift-economy formula identical
  in shape to shrine offering.** The daira reappears as a venue for animal fighting (p. 149,
  folio 140).
- **Wandering ascetics as a visible feature of the countryside.** "The fakirs and holy men who wander
  about the land submit themselves. to severe asceticism, .u ttering[text-layer?] :qo[text-layer?]
  word arid roaming amongst the people ' quietly '" (p. 267, folio 258). **No khanqah, no langar, no
  order and no patron** — **(not stated)**. And in Sorley's summary of the rural landscape, tombs and
  Sayids stand beside livestock: Sind is "a land of cultivators and herdsmen, keepers of camels,
  buffaloes, sheep, goats and oxen, **of tombs and Sayids**, of dust and heat, of the annual rise and
  fall of the waters of the Indus" (p. 265, folio 256).
- **The ascetic vocabulary the book's own glossary fixes.** "Ades is[text-layer?]  Type of wandering
  ascetics ; literally ' people without country '."; "Bairigi[text-layer?] or , Berigi[text-layer?]
  Type of Hindu ascetic."; "Kh�hOris[text-layer?]  A kind of wandering religious ascetics."; "Ningas
  .[text-layer?]  Hindu ascetics who wear no clothing. Literally ' naked ones ' ."; "Sanyisi[text-layer?]
  A type of Hindu ascetic."; "Giirii[text-layer?]  Hindu religious Master and Instructor,"; "Swami
  Hind u[text-layer?] religious teacher."; "Joga  Sindhi form of Yoga."; "ThOri[text-layer?]  Name of
  a wandering tribe." (pp. 430–431, folios 421–422) **Nine terms, four of them itinerant religious
  specialists. The glossary says these words are "FOUND IN THE POEMS" (p. 430, folio 421) and does not
  say in which sur** — **(not stated)**.
- **The ascetics' own establishment, from inside the verse: a seat, a fire and a kept substance.** "In
  · th.eir[text-layer?] .'holy seat' .th�y[text-layer?] · keep- the _ sacred ambergris" (p. 357,
  folio 348); "The third day �ri[text-layer?] . �h,�ir[text-layer?]. sacred seats . / They let ·the
  fire's dull siri<?ulder[text-layer?] glow" (p. 359, folio 350). **A perpetual fire and a kept
  substance; the book does not say who keeps it, where it is or who may enter** — **(not stated)**.
  Their buildings are named only to be dismissed: "The huts they have are nothingness" (p. 362,
  folio 353); "Leave the wastes and to clustered huts pass" (p. 363, folio 354). **No dargah, no
  khanqah, no takiya and no endowment appears in that whole section** — **(not stated)**.
- **Cult objects, dress and bodily marks across the Holy Men sections.** Horns blown at morn (p. 357,
  folio 348; "The Adesis blew their. horns at early morn", p. 360, folio 351); threads washed on the
  seventh day (pp. 358, 359, folios 349, 350); topknots fashioned by hand (p. 358, folio 349); the
  rosary counted "In mind" (p. 361, folio 352); the bored ear (p. 362, folio 353); the loincloth as
  the sole possession (p. 363, folio 354); nakedness (p. 363, folio 354); dust on face and head and
  dust as incense (pp. 363–364, folios 354–355); "blanket-rugs" (p. 359, folio 350); panniers and
  bedding (p. 360, folio 351); holy oil (p. 360, folio 351); "the mendicant's dress" and the
  prohibition "Deck thyself out in no showy garb" (p. 364, folio 355). **The prayer and vigil
  timetable is reckoned in watches: "daytime's four long watches" (p. 358, folio 349) and "The whole
  day's space and eight long watches through" (p. 360, folio 351).**
- **An order of women renunciants at Tatta, reported with open hostility and otherwise unidentified.**
  Manrique describes "' a certain order of hypocritical women devotees, or to put it more plainly,
  prostitutes '", who "while professing to despise the world, `e:µgaged`[text-layer?] in the most
  shameful form of sexual immorality in the very streets" (pp. 85–86, folios 76–77), adding "' But
  what is worse is that these women who 'act in this disgraceful way in less frequented streets are
  held to be the greatest renunciators of the world and its ways.'" **The book gives the order no
  name, no location beyond Tatta, no affiliation and no date** — **(not stated)**; **the only datum
  that survives the polemic is that renunciation carried public prestige.**
- **Sufism becoming an institution, stated as a process with an end state.** "Sufism had in fact
  become swallowed up in the Islamic church, and, within Islam, made itself into a religious system of
  its own . It became both a school of thought and a system of practice and belief" (pp. 286–287,
  folios 277–278); "It produced its orders of adherents, its darwishes and its saints, and showed all
  the outward signs of an institution of fixed character" (p. 287, folio 278); "When Sufism became a
  system of practice it became associated with the rise of various orders of darwishes and mendicants."
  (p. 298, folio 289) **A devotional movement becoming a fixed institution that produces saints as an
  output.**
- **And Sorley's flat denial of any continuous Sindhi tradition, which contradicts the "coherent
  system" of p. 302 two paragraphs earlier.** "**That there has been,[text-layer?] any consistent
  progress of Sufism in Sind I do not believe.** What seems to have happened is that occasionally an ·
  exceptional[text-layer?] man versed in the literary heritage of Persia emerged from among his
  fellows and gained a reputation for learning and sanctity." (p. 302, folio 293); "**Siifism[text-layer?]
  in Sind has been handed down by a handful of learned men from age to age.**" (p. 303, folio 294)
  **Both are kept; the book does not reconcile them.**
- **The terminological complaint that follows from it.** "But a mighty gulf yawns between the thinking
  of Shah Abdul Latif and the utterances of the itinerant fakir … **Yet popular nomenclature would
  make both men Siifis[text-layer?].** We might as well expect Plato and the village mummer to speak a
  common language." (p. 303, folio 294) **Sorley says the word "Sufi" in popular Sindhi usage covers
  two things he regards as incommensurable — which bears directly on how any Sindhi source's use of
  the word should be read** — **bhit-bhit-shah**.

### Music, recitation and the performing classes

- **The best Sindhi music and singing is performed at shrines — the book's one explicit statement
  placing performance inside shrine practice.** "Though · this music , is a�tually_[text-layer?]
  Indian music, it has not bee:r:i[text-layer?] ·una£;ected[text-layer?] by Muslim P.ractice[text-layer?]
  . . For it is 1 noteworthy that much of tl;le[text-layer?] best music ' and . singing .
  i�[text-layer?] ·associated[text-layer?] with religious observance at t01pbs[text-layer?] and
  shrines of Muslim saints." (p. 249, folio 240)
- **Weekly recitation of the poems at the mausoleum, still running at the time of writing.** "Since
  the poet's death the poems have been recited weekly on Fridays in the mausoleum which has become a
  kind of holy shrine which still draws its crowds." (p. 185, folio 176) The recitation is called a
  "' wake '" and the 1882 account gives "the wide courtyard", the sound "occasionally broken by a
  hearty " Allahu "" and a congregation of "more than a hundred men, women and children" (p. 185,
  folio 176) — **bhit-bhit-shah**. **This is the only congregation figure for any shrine in the book.**
- **Music as an act of worship, stated as doctrine, and the mosque as the training ground of the
  ear.** "The Muslim idea of music as ·part[text-layer?] of the service of God has also played some
  part in the making of Sindhi poetry." (p. 250, folio 241); "the practice of ·rpusical[text-layer?]
  intoning which is to some extent acqu,ired[text-layer?] in the. mosque'[text-layer?] by
  e:very[text-layer?] 'Muslim brought..up[text-layer?] iri[text-layer?] the tenets of liis[text-layer?]
  faith have certainly done somethirtg[text-layer?] to :qi9uld[text-layer?] the form of
  poeti.cal'[text-layer?] composition amongst ' Muslims. They have . done more to standardize · the
  natur�[text-layer?] of the ·.[text-layer?] music that appeals fo[text-layer?] them." (p. 249,
  folio 240) And the Arabic side: "This chanting is heard iri[text-layer?] the intoning of the Koran
  in the mosques… Monotony is, therefore, a characteristic of Muslim music.'" (p. 229, folio 220)
- **Music as a closed hereditary monopoly, said three ways.** Indian music "remaining a ·
  c;losed[text-layer?] system ,in th.e " control ·of �pro­fessional[text-layer?] ·p�rformers[text-layer?]
  and musicians' wh�[text-layer?] have kept the practic�.[text-layer?] of it by. tradition�l[text-layer?]
  Jjiethods[text-layer?] in their own- hands.:" (p. 227, folio 218); "a closed field of.
  semi..:se�ret[text-layer?] lore" (p. 227, folio 218); "the practice of the art has been confined in
  the typical Hindu manner to castes of per­formers." (p. 231, folio 222) **And the mechanism of the
  monopoly is mystification:** the performer's dexterity "was sufficient to create in the minds of the
  listeners a belief t.hat this music was _a kind of myste'rious and almost superhuman art ' closed to
  the bulk of m'arikind[text-layer?]." "For this naive and childlike attitude_ of thought the
  vagtie[text-layer?] sentimental mythology which linked music .with religfon[text-layer?] as one -of
  the mysteries of grace and light was largely responsibfo:[text-layer?] Music in fact was still a
  form of mumbo-j'umbo." (p. 228, folio 219) **The reform movement against it is described as active
  in the book's present and its stakes as proprietary — notation would break the monopoly**
  (pp. 227–228, folios 218–219).
- **The minstrel class: occupation, standing and household.** "The professional musicians are drawn
  mostly from the minstrel class, which . is · held in low esteem." (p. 232, folio 223); "the
  professional minstrel, . who is usually a person of poor education and is often indeed illiterate"
  (p. 228, folio 219); and the account of why: "The disreputable character of the minstr_el's[text-layer?]
  private life was often responsible for his low social standing; for he commonly associated with
  persons who indulged in opium, bhang · and _other intoxicants… and his womenfolk were engaged in the
  une4ifyi�g[text-layer?] . occupations of prostitution and nautch dancing." (p. 228, folio 219)
  **This is the class that carried Shah Abdul Latif's verse** — **bhit-bhit-shah**. Against it: "There
  are many . capable amateur perforrµers[text-layer?]… because Sind has long had a reputation for
  proficiency in musical execution prevailing amongst all classes." (p. 232, folio 223)
- **Two hereditary supplier groups, an occasion market, and the demand Sorley says they failed to
  meet.** "not fully catered for · by the Manghanhars, Bhats and inf.erior[text-layer?] wandering
  minstrels who supplied. the common people with their music· and their rough verse for ' weddings
  arid[text-layer?] festlvals[text-layer?] and similar �eremonies[text-layer?]." (p. 246, folio 237)
  **Two named groups, a product, a customer and three occasion-types — and the argument that the
  Risalo met a religious demand they did not.**
- **The Doms as a professional oral-transmission class with a circuit into Sindhi villages.** Baluchi
  poetry "is wholly oral and … is sung by professional minstrels called Doms or Dombs who sing the
  songs in the gatherings of the clans. This oral tradition is exceed­ingly[text-layer?] strong."
  (p. 255, folio 246); "The Doms visited the villages of Sind wherever there were Baliichis[text-layer?]
  to listen to them." (p. 258, folio 249) **A demand-following itinerant circuit.**
- **The instruments of Sind, named twice and not identically.** "his sitar, sarangi or bina·[text-layer?]"
  (p. 228, folio 219); "The instru­men�s[text-layer?] e'rriployed[text-layer?] are chiefly the ektar,
  the sitar, the sarangi, the tambur, and the various kinds of pipes and drums employed else­where ·
  in . India. The biria[text-layer?], or vina, is hardly ever seen." (p. 232, folio 223) In the verse:
  a fiddle carried on the shoulder (p. 338, folio 329), "Violas, fiddles, violins / And drums the
  happy people played" on the arrival of the rain (p. 344, folio 335), a fiddle "decked … Of tassels
  �nd·[text-layer?] rattles" (p. 353, folio 344), "his lute and ·.zither string" and "thy-:pipe's
  tune" (p. 354, folio 345), and kettledrums broken in the same breath as the Prophet's door is made
  the only door (p. 339, folio 330).
- **The named Sindhi genres, serious and light — and the saint-praise genre among them.** "The chief
  kinds of serious poetical composition cul­tivated[text-layer?] by Sindhis have been ' madahs ', '
  munaj ats '[text-layer?], ' rnarsiyahs '[text-layer?] and ' kowars ' or ' lanats '. The first are
  praises of **God, the Prophet and Saints**. The second are the religious hymns p er s e[text-layer?]
  . The thi�d[text-layer?] are elegies generally concerned with the martyrdom of Hassan and Husain.
  The fourth are compositions which deal with th�[text-layer?] moraJ[text-layer?] virtues and the
  vices of mankind." (p. 249, folio 240) And the lighter forms: "Sindhi boasts fafohnamos[text-layer?]
  _ or songs of victory, kafis ot[text-layer?] wais, a form of amatory verse, baita-or[text-layer?]
  couplets and san'.yaros[text-layer?] or love' messages." (p. 249, folio 240) **"madahs" is a
  saint-praise genre named as a standing category of Sindhi composition.** Burton's parallel list adds
  the instruments and audiences: "' fateh­namo ' or song of battle composed by Langahs…, the ' kafi
  1[text-layer?] or ' wai ', generally amatory, the ' baita ', or couplets sung to the
  tambftr[text-layer?] or guitar, the ' dohad[text-layer?] accompanied on the ' duhad 1[text-layer?]
  or kettledrum, and the ' sanyiiro[text-layer?] ', or e1;morous[text-layer?] missive, sung to the
  music of the nai or pipe and particularly popular amongst the wilder clan people." (p. 232,
  folio 223)
- **A calendar and clock rule governing when a melody may be sung.** Jones: "each rag with his
  fai;nily[text-layer?] is appropriated to a distinct season in which alone his melody can be sung or
  played at prescribed hours of the day or night." (p. 231, folio 222) For the Risalo specifically,
  the poems fall into "some melodic form suitable to the type of subject ·matter, the time of the
  year or the time of day ' when the song should be sung" (p. 232, folio 223). **A liturgical
  timetable attached to music, with six seasons, one god per mode, five raginis per mode and eight
  sons per ragini printed as a numbered system** (p. 231, folio 222).
- **The transmission chain before print: singer to singer, and mother to child.** The poems would have
  "remained a rather vague body of sung and recited verse handed down from one singer to another"
  (pp. 233–234, folios 224–225) — **which makes the minstrel class the archive of the text**; and the
  third and largest category of poems is "the Sindhi love stories told by mothers to thei,:[text-layer?]
  children in tlie[text-layer?] cradle" (p. 235, folio 226). **A second, domestic and female
  channel.**
- **Court patronage of a musician, itemised over six nights inside the verse.** The performance —
  "Beside. the fort the man of music . �ang[text-layer?]", "in Girnar a. loud commotion rang",
  "plucked strings and folk's attention drew", singing "i�ll[text-layer?] t_he_[text-layer?]
  daWn'[text-layer?]" (pp. 353–355, folios 344–346); the escalating tariff — "gifts that will delight
  'tl?-Y[text-layer?] J;ieart[text-layer?]", "Of .ricl�[text-layer?] ab:unqant[text-layer?] gifts take
  -thou thy. fill … there are more / Tomorrow I shall <1;dd[text-layer?] to swell the store", "Great
  ·wealth of silver", "�anie·[text-layer?] couches, . cushions, _palanquins", "N,ine·[text-layer?]
  lakhs ' of inoney[text-layer?] and nonillions 'o'er", "The . elephants", "A _mint' of :money[text-layer?]"
  (pp. 353–354, folios 344–345). **The fullest patronage sequence in the book, and it is inside a
  poem.** Sorley's two readings of the same cycle do not agree: the poems are about "the
  chaffering_ for -the musician's reward ·and the · grief of Sor�th[text-layer?]" (p. 352, folio 343)
  against "the mystery that brings the music listener into communion with the divine" (p. 250,
  folio 241). **Both are printed.**
- **Caste named and its relevance denied, inside the Musician's section.** "The gift,s[text-layer?] of
  the Almighty do hot[text-layer?] depend on caste. / The _ wor:ker[text-layer?] - is .the finder."
  (p. 338, folio 329)
- **The ceremonial market and its debasement.** Brass bands and "the handblown harmonium" are "employed
  to give an impression of opulent upto­dateness at . weddings- and similar ceremonies once graced by
  the performariGe[text-layer?] of genuine Indian music by proficient exponents of the minstrel class."
  (p. 228, folio 219)
- **And the flat denial that any of this is Sindhi.** "There is. nq[text-layer?] distinctively Sindhi
  school of music. The music of Si�d[text-layer?] is part of tlie[text-layer?] musical heritage of
  Hindustan." (p. 230, folio 221); "There' is nothing distinctive in the music of Sind." (p. 232,
  folio 223). **If an entry wants to claim the surs as a Sindhi musical tradition, this book denies it
  in terms** — and the index records the same verdict, "(no Sindhi school of) 221, 223" (p. 438).
- **The cross-community explanation Sorley builds on an occupational fact.** "Most of the musicians in
  Sind have been Hindus and not Mussulmans. Possibly in this circumstance may be found another reason
  why the Islamic poetry of Shah Abdul Latif exercises so strong a spell over the non-Muslim
  inhabitants of the land." (p. 231, folio 222) — **bhit-bhit-shah**. **"another reason" points back to
  the demographic explanation at p. 175 (folio 166), which is given without the musical one; the two
  are not joined.**
- **The print-and-commentary economy that grew up under British rule.** "This literature, both
  critical and expository, owes its origin largely to the influence of English education in India
  during the 1ast[text-layer?] eighty years." (p. 233, folio 224); "The .vast deyelopment[text-layer?]
  of general education in Sind under British rule… com­bined with, the loye[text-layer?] and reverence
  with which the common people regard the poetry of Shah Abdul Latif, has in several generations
  produced a �ultltude[text-layer?] of books written about the poems and the poet." (pp. 233–234,
  folios 224–225) **A stated demand mechanism for devotional publishing: mass reverence plus colonial
  literary education** — **bhit-bhit-shah**. "There �eems[text-layer?] to be no end to the number of
  publications which recount in simple language ·the tales of the Sindhi lovers" (p. 268, folio 259).
- **Textual production as a shrine institution.** The Risalo is "collated by scribes and formed into a
  regular collection which exists in several manuscript forms" (pp. 183–184, folios 174–175), the named
  amanuenses being "Tamar and Hashim" (p. 184, folio 175); Sorley's own manuscript access is recorded,
  "I have consulted a very;[text-layer?] inferior manuscript copy of the poems in the library of the
  India Office, London" (p. 184, folio 175, footnote 1) — **bhit-bhit-shah**.
- **Oral tradition as a dated, exhaustible resource.** "Until the eighties of last century it was
  still possible to trace oral tradition up to its source because old men were still alive who could
  recall what their grandfathers had told them and some at least of these grandfathers must have been
  contemporaries of the poet." "This source is now stopped. There is no likelihood of any further
  accretion of hard facts, though this will not prevent the oral tradition from developing further."
  (p. 179, folio 170) **A methodological statement with a date-window — the 1880s, which is also the
  decade of the 1882 wake account.**

### Education as an endowed institution

- **The two institutional forms, both attached to the mosque.** "Educa'tion[text-layer?] centred round
  the mosque to · WhiCh[text-layer?] the ' maktab ' was attached. There were ' madressas ' for '
  higher study of a medieval scholastic nature" (p. 220, folio 211); the learned got their learning
  "at the ' maktabs • and ' madressas · presided over by ' mullas ', · ' akhunds ' and ' ustads '"
  (p. 218, folio 209). **Maktab attached to mosque; madressa for higher study; three grades of
  teacher named.**
- **The six madressas of Talpur Sind, named and located — a complete institutional census.** "In
  tl?-e[text-layer?] time o�[text-layer?] the Talpiirs[text-layer?] Sind possessed six ' madressas ',
  at Sehwan,- Trippat near Sehwan, }\hohra[text-layer?] north of . Sel!wan[text-layer?],
  Matiar�[text-layer?], Mohar · or- Walhari near Umarkot and Chothiyari on the Nara river" (p. 225,
  folio 216). **Three of the six are at or near Sehwan.** Place-level: **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**, **shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar** (the book's "Sehwan", "Trippat near Sehwan", "}\hohra[text-layer?] north of Sel!wan[text-layer?]"); **shrine-at-odero-lal-udero-lal-teerath-asthan**, **shrine-of-makhdoom-nooh-hala** (the book's "Matiar�"[text-layer?]); **shrine-of-makhdoom-abdul-rahim-girhori**, **umarkot-amarkot-shiv-mandir** (the book's "Umarkot").
- **The learned class of Old Sind was a religious class.** "Thu�[text-layer?] mo�t[text-layer?] of .
  the learned men-- of Old Sind .were Sayids, Kazis, Mullas or per�mns[text-layer?] concerned with
  t�e[text-layer?] teachi_ng[text-layer?] of the Koran and the exposition of the Muslim faith -in one
  way or another" (p. 218, folio 209); "In a land whe�e[text-layer?] the Muslim theocratic theory
  prevailed so strongly it was inevitable · that religion and education shoul_d[text-layer?] go hand
  in ha�d[text-layer?]" (p. 218, folio 209).
- **The religious personnel of the village, in the book's own spellings.** "the saintly man, the
  interpreter of the simple creed of the Koran, the ' mullo ', the ' shaikh ', the ' akhund ' and the
  ' ustad ' exercised a kind of authority which departed when life became more complex" (p. 214,
  folio 205); "The ' mullo ' and the mosque corresponded to. the priest and the church. The
  darwjshes[text-layer?] were a_ kiJ?.d[text-layer?] of wandering friars" (p. 213, folio 204).
- **The teacher's income: fees, gift-days and perquisites.** "It does no�[text-layer?] appear that the
  teaching profession has ever been held in any great respect in Sind. Burton comi:p.ents[text-layer?]
  'on the _practice[text-layer?] of givirig[text-layer?] perquisites to . teachers to
  supple'mep.t-their[text-layer?] meagre earnings. The ' a_khund[text-layer?] ' refus,ed[text-layer?]
  to start �nything[text-layer?] new' without a _fee[text-layer?]" (p. 225, folio 216); "Teachers were
  ·seldom paid more than half a. rupee· by each pupil per month" (p. 225, folio 216). **The ʿid
  product and its price band:** "At the three great ids of Bakr, Fitr and Ba.rat[text-layer?] the
  t�acher[text-layer?] used to write two or tl:tree[text-layer?] couplets upon crudely
  ornament�d[text-layer?] p'aper[text-layer?]. For this he received from four. anrias[text-layer?] to
  a rupee in payment. . These com­positions ' were called ' "idis ' and were hung upo�[text-layer?] the
  walls of the house" (p. 225, folio 216).
- **Captain Hart's Karachi schedule of 1840 — the fullest fee list in the book.** "' Each scholar
  tak.es[text-layer?] a handfut[text-layer?] of rice an�[text-layer?] a few sticks with him as a
  present to the mas�er[text-layer?] daily and a !"upee[text-layer?] or two is paid monthly by the
  parents. The J;>ersian[text-layer?] language is taught by the Mullas of whom ten or twelve. have

  . for employment in the service of the' government are there instructed, the charge varying from a

  **Daily payment in kind, a monthly cash fee, a graduated Persian-class fee, a completion present,
  classes held in mosques, "ten or twelve" Mullas teaching in Karachi, and girls taught Koran
  reading.** Place-level: **bhagnari-mandir**, **darya-lal-mandir-darya-lal-sankat-mochan-mandir**, **jhollay-lal-mandir**, **shree-ratneshwar-mahadev-temple-karachi**, **shri-laxmi-narayan-mandir-native-jetty-bridge**, **shri-panchmukhi-hanuman-mandir-karachi**, **shri-swaminarayan-mandir-karachi**, **shri-varun-dev-mandir**, **shrine-of-abdullah-shah-ghazi**, **shrine-of-pir-mangho**, **shrine-of-qalandar-baba-auliya** (the book's "Karachi").
- **The curriculum, primary and higher.** Primary: the articles of faith, the throne verse and the
  last two verses of sura lix — **both Quranic references are destroyed in the text layer and are not

  responses' of. the azan) the ' different kinds of washings and the P.rayers[text-layer?] fa the ·
  �osq�e[text-layer?], �nd[text-layer?] th�[text-layer?] procedure of j oint[text-layer?] prayer"
  (p. 222, folio 213); writing on "tablets or boards (takhti)", and — **directly relevant to how
  hagiography circulated** — "Legends of the prophets and stories of holy men formed part of . the
  regular course. Poetry was also studied but all eroticism in poetry was strictly excluded" (p. 223,
  folio 214). Higher study runs grammar, Nahw, logic, dialectics, Hanafi theology, Koran commentary,
  hadis and rhetoric through fifteen-plus named textbooks (pp. 223–224, folios 214–215), with "the
  occult sciences of geomancy, astrology' and divination by numbers" at the top of the syllabus
  (p. 224, folio 215). **Memorisation is the method** (pp. 223–224, folios 214–215), and "Sind has
  always had a number of persons called ' hafiz ' who know the Koran by heart. But this does not mean
  that they have any scholarly knowledge of the Arabic language" (p. 219, folio 210).
- **The Sindhi reading course, and the saint-lore in it.** "(r)[text-layer?] the Niir[text-layer?]
  Namo of Abdul Hashim…, (2) the works of Makhdiim[text-layer?] Has_hirp.[text-layer?] beginning with
  the Tasfir, (3) tales in prose and verse, such as the adventures- of Saiful or Lail-i-Majano, **the
  adventures and sayings of celebrated saints of the golden · age of Islam** and books on the life and
  death of the Prophet" (p. 225, folio 216). **A confessional split in medium:** "The_ Sindhi
  Ia.nguage[text-layer?] was much neglected as a medium of instruction except.by Hindus, who used a
  non-Arabic script" (p. 226, folio 217); "Burton states, however, that a boy ¥(a·s[text-layer?] nine
  years old before he began the systematic study of his own language in Sindhi" (p. 225, folio 216).
- **Learning converting into sanctity — the book's causal account of how shrine authority is
  produced.** "But in an age,when learning is rare the scholar a:uains[text-layer?] �[text-layer?]
  soi-t[text-layer?] : of sanctity' which attaches to him a small band of admirers." (p. 222,
  folio 213); "This peculiarity of Muslim learning in the special conditions c;>f[text-layer?] Sind
  accounts fully for the reverence with which men learned in this lore were held by the uneducated
  populace" (p. 224, folio 215). Against a general illiteracy: "The great mass of the people
  remained- illiterate. Beyond the smattering of Arabic required for their: re°Iigious[text-layer?]
  devotions the common people had little acquaintance with.the writi:en[text-layer?] word" (p. 222,
  folio 213).
- **Sind's Sufi reputation as a pull factor — an explicit market in religious prestige.** "Both the
  Afgh�ns[text-layer?] and the Moghuls showed a tendency . to encourage the se't'tl�m'ent[text-layer?]
  of men of this character who were further attracted by the �eputation[text-layer?] which Sind had

  and TarkMns[text-layer?] was specially notable for · the entrance of this kind of intellectual
  aristocracy" (p. 219, folio 210). And the consequences he draws: "an ·absence ·of bitterness between
  Sunni and Shia, 'and a kind of rapprochement between the -de_eper[text-layer?] mystical
  ide�s[text-layer?] of Muslim and Hindu thought" (p. 225, folio 216) — **the book's standing
  explanation for shared shrine use.** Note the tension with its own statement that the Sayid leaders
  "were nearly all Shias by persuasion" against a Sunni majority (pp. 172–173, folios 163–164).
- **And the methodological warning that governs all of it.** "We have Iio[text-layer?] contemporary
  account of �he[text-layer?] working of this educational system in the seventeenth and eighteenth
  centuries in Sind. The best description of it has been given by Burton who … elicited the facts

  had been hanqed[text-layer?] . down to the91[text-layer?] or told them by their elders" (p. 220,
  folio 211). **Nineteenth-century informants are the source for an eighteenth-century description.**

### Land, revenue and the tax schedule

- **The framing claim.** "The system of land tenure in Sind, so far as is known, has always been
  feudal. In all feudal systems the chief method by which the overlord assured himself of the homage
  of his vassals was by the grant of fiefs. In Sind these grants took a great variety of . forms."
  (p. 163, folio 154) The jaghirdar tribes are listed, "Talpurs, Nizamanis, Lagharis, Marris, Jamalis,
  Bhugrais, Bhagranis, Rinds, Changs, Chalgaris, Lasharis, Nuhanis, Lunds, Kalvis, Jettors, Nundanis,
  Jelalanis, Kh6sas[text-layer?], Kh6khars[text-layer?], Abras, Katians, Sayids, Pirs and Pathans. The
  vast majority of these are Baluchi tribes." (p. 165)
- **The crop share, three authorities, three answers, kept side by side.** The *Ain-i-Akbari* "states
  that a third share of the crops was taken from the husbandmen"; "Postans says that under . the later
  system of the Talpiirs (probably following the `Kalhorn`[text-layer?] system) the
  `roy�l`[text-layer?] share of the produce was two-fifths, one-third and one-fifth according to the
  character of the land cultivated" (p. 154, folio 145); the Commissioner in Sind in "1_8-47" reported
  "' The bulk of the land revenue . is assessed and realized in kind at rates not exceeding one-third
  of the gross produce with some small additions to meet charges of irrigation and collection '"
  (p. 155, folio 146).
- **The three land categories and the cash assessment.** "first, land near the river that was easily
  irrigable without much artificial aid, second, land some distance from the river requiring the use
  of canals and water wheels, and, `thifd`[text-layer?], waste land that had to be cleared of jungle
  and scrub by the process of `jungal-shagafi`[text-layer?]." (pp. 154–155, folios 145–146) Money
  assessments were "based on a standard bigha, somewhat exceeding half an acre in extent…
  (`bighOti`[text-layer?], i.e. measurement by bighas)" (p. 155, folio 146); the cash unit was "the
  jireb (about 100 feet square)", and "Opium and indigo, both valuable crops, paid 20 and 80 rupees
  per bigha" (p. 154, folio 145, footnote).
- **Collection in kind, public granaries and farmed disposal.** "The Government share of the revenue
  in kind was kept in public granaries until disposed of and the revenue collectors used their
  discretion as to its disposal, by auction or `contr�ct`[text-layer?]…" (p. 155, folio 146); the
  khirman rule from Postans: "' On the crops being gathered in, a Government officer attended to
  estimate their amount and to take the government share from the khirman `cir`[text-layer?] general
  heap, previous to `whiCh`[text-layer?] it was not allowed to be touched under heavy penalties. '"
  (p. 155, folio 146, footnote 1)
- **The heads of revenue, and the cesses.** "Under the Moghuls the chief heads of central revenue were
  land revenue, customs, mint, inheritance, presents, monopolies and indemnities." (p. 156, folio 147)
  **"inheritance" is the escheat head.** "The local cesses consisted of a variety of duties levied on
  artisans, retailers, prooucers[text-layer?] and consumers, transit dues of every description, called
  loosely rahadari and j agat. The total incidence of these was exceedingly heavy." (p. 156,
  folio 147) "Sarkar cites no fewer than fifty-four of them, but his · list is `no.t`[text-layer?]
  exhaustive." (p. 155, folio 146) In Sind specifically: "besides the land revenue the chief taxes
  were sar shumari, a poll tax . on `nc;m-Baluchi`[text-layer?] and Jat Muslims, Sair, transit dues,
  and a variety of `cases`[text-layer?] called abwab." (p. 155, folio 146)
- **Burton's schedule of imposts, as Sorley prints it** (introduced "Burton has cited a number of
  these exactions prevalent under the old regime iii $ind[text-layer?]", p. 157, folio 148):
  **Peshkash-i-mahajan**, on banias, "five to ten rupees annually for the privilege of trading";
  **Sar shumari asnafguran**, "a poll tax of .Rs. 31[text-layer?] per annum paid by artisans like
  dyers, carpenters, smiths and masons"; **Dalali**, monthly on brokers; **Hawai**, on wreck goods;
  **Furui**, on straying animals found on assessed land; **Charkhit**, "a tax paid on water wheels,
  garden and grain land"; **Ijareh kola�ha**[text-layer?], on inundated lands and lakes farmed to
  government servants at one-third of profits on reeds, wild fowl, water lilies and edible roots;
  **Ijareh putta**, on ferry passengers; **Sardarakhti**, given twice and differently — "at the rate
  of one-half or one-third on the production of mango, date and other fruit trees… Claimed at the time
  of getting the fruit" and "at the rate of one anna per tree charged before the tree was ripe, and
  collected by the kardar"; **Tarazu**, on bazaar produce "at an average rate of one pice for five
  seers"; **Shlkar-i-mahi**[text-layer?], on fishermen, "one third or · even one-half of their catch";
  **Salamati kishti**, "four annas for this, six annas called ' nath ' … and eight annas for every
  horse safely landed"; **Mawesi**, "one anna on the sale of every horse, mule, ass, camel or cow";
  **Rezki**, retail, "an anna per rupee… If the sale exceeded roo[text-layer?] rupees, 5'[text-layer?]
  per cent was charged but sawkars and wealthy men escaped with 4 per cent"; **Panachari**, grazing —
  "Camels paid 8 annas, buffaloes 4 annas, cows 2 annas, sheep and goats I[text-layer?] anna per
  month"; **Gutta, or ijareh sharab**, annually on each distiller's profits; **Amini**, "a fee charged
  for the settlement of disputes. It was also called chouth because the fee was one-fourth of the sums
  disputed" (pp. 157–158, folios 148–149). **A litigation tax at twenty-five per cent of the matter in
  dispute — set it against the claim that "The administration of justice costs little or nothing to
  the state" (p. 162, folio 153).**
- **The fishery, taxed thirteen ways at Karachi.** "' There were thirteen separate cesses at Karachi
  exacted by the tax farmer on fishermen. '" (p. 158, folio 149; footnote 3 "Account of the Fisheries
  of Sind by John Macleod, Collector of Customs, Karachi, ·1 847[text-layer?]…, p. 704.") The cesses
  themselves run on tanner's bark, jungle wood for burning and for building, charcoal boats, boats
  quitting harbour for Bombay or Muscat, "on tindal, serang, cook and khalasis", dondas to Keamari,
  "an export tax of 20 per cent on fish by the land route ; a tax of 9 per cent on fish taken to camp
  for sale ; a tax on twigs used in the walls of house�[text-layer?]" (pp. 158–159, folios 149–150).
  **Crew members are taxed by rank.** A witnessed levy: two Baluch soldiers boarding Karachi fishing
  boats to demand a share, "This was the levy of the Shikar~i-mahi[text-layer?] in operation."
  (p. 158, folio 149) The earlier form of the same right, farmed to the fishing caste with its own
  collector: Del Hoste, 1832, "' Fisheries are established at different places, and the rivers and
  water courses portioned off to the Mohanas or `:fishermen`[text-layer?]. At each of these places
  there is a Mehta to collect the duties which consist of one-third of what is daily caught.'"
  (p. 109, folio 100, footnote 4) Place-level: **bhagnari-mandir**, **darya-lal-mandir-darya-lal-sankat-mochan-mandir**, **jhollay-lal-mandir**, **shree-ratneshwar-mahadev-temple-karachi**, **shri-laxmi-narayan-mandir-native-jetty-bridge**, **shri-panchmukhi-hanuman-mandir-karachi**, **shri-swaminarayan-mandir-karachi**, **shri-varun-dev-mandir**, **shrine-of-abdullah-shah-ghazi**, **shrine-of-pir-mangho**, **shrine-of-qalandar-baba-auliya** (the book's "Karachi").
- **A parallel craft poll tax.** "The paper manufacturers of Llrkana[text-layer?] had to pay a poll tax
  of Rs . 8-12-0 per annum." (p. 158, folio 149, footnote 1) Place-level: **swami-dharmdas-darbar-larkana-kennedy-market** (the book's "Llrkana"[text-layer?]; the printed form is damaged and the place-level join rests on reading it as Larkana — see Doubts).
- **Tax farming named as the central abuse, and the collecting bureaucracy described.** "One of the
  great curses of Moghul, Kalh6ro[text-layer?] and Talpiir[text-layer?] rule alike was the farming of
  the right to collect taxes and imposts of various kinds… The system was a bad one as it admitted a
  middle­man who had no interest in the incidence of the taxation he collected. It opened the door to
  all kinds of corruption and oppression, to which contemporary records refer copiously." (p. 157,
  folio 148) "The tax farmers, ij aredars and contractors were multitudinous and. formed a close
  bureaucracy. Theywere[text-layer?] mostly Hindus who knew very well just how far they could go with
  impunity." (p. 157, folio 148) **A named case where the farmer and the taxed are of one community:**
  Karachi's Hindu majority "' prosecute a most extensive commerce in despite of the heavy customs and
  duties that are levied by one of their own tribe who farms the revenues of Karachi '" (p. 127,
  folio 118) — **no name, no sum, no date** — **(not stated)**.
- **The customs farm, with figures.** Pottinger: "In 1809 the Hindu customs farmer paid into the
  treasury ninety-nine thousand rupees and himself received twelve thousand ; and in 1816 the
  e.mount[text-layer?] paid · in was one lakh twenty-three thousand and the ijaredar obtained nearly
  twenty thousand for his trouble." (p. 159, folio 150) **The figures are spelled out in words and so
  survive the text layer.** The cumulative burden on one consignment: "the duties on a camel load of
