  English manufactured piecegoods from the time of landing in Sind till they reached the north end of
  the country by land amounted to 58 rupees" (p. 156, folio 147, footnote).
- **What the proceeds were spent on, in Sorley's view — and the counterfactual he declines to make.**
  "Had the proceeds of this taxation been employed generously on public works and on education, there
  would have been something to say in favour of it. But the money thus wrung from the pockets of the
  populace was employed to enrich the ruling house and to enable it to amass gre<:tt[text-layer?]
  stores of jewels and treasure for personal aggrandizement, pleasure and display." (p. 159, folio 150)
  Compare "That kind of conscience is reserved for governments of a later · day" (p. 94, folio 85).
- **Hoarding as fiscal behaviour, with a figure.** "The Talpiirs were undoubtedly miserly, and
  exceedingly anxious to amass large sums of money which they converted into jewels or else stored as
  bullion in their treasuries." (p. 141, folio 132); "When Mir Fateh Ali died in 1801 he left
  thirty-five lakhs to divide amongst his brothers." (p. 141, folio 132, footnote 2)
- **Two conflicting revenue totals, and Sorley's refusal to choose.** "Burnes in 1830 calculated the
  `gloss`[text-layer?] revenue of the state at 40 lakhs, whereas under the early Kalh6ra it had been
  `Bo`[text-layer?] lakhs. But these figures are guesswork." (p. 154, folio 145) Against which
  Pottinger has revenue rising "from 42 to 61 lakhs" between 1807 and 1816, with actual tribute paid
  to Kabul "not aver[aging] one-sixth" of the 13 lakhs demanded (p. 154, folio 145, footnote); Sorley
  concludes "The belief, therefore, that revenue fell greatly with the Talpiirs is not corroborated."
  And his standing caution: "Estimates of the amount of revenue are all unreliable." (p. 154,
  folio 145)
- **The tribute schedule to Herat and Kabul, four steps.** "successively reduced · from 21 lakhs
  (Nadir Shah `l7J9`[text-layer?]) to 14 lakhs (Ahmad Shah 1747) , t o `I I`[text-layer?] lakhs (Ahmad
  Shah about 1760) , to , 7 lakhs (Timiir Shah about . 1775)." (p. 154, folio 145) **The first date is
  destroyed and unrecoverable; carried as printed.** Tribute was paid partly in kind and at a
  fraudulent valuation: "' to pay tribute to the King of Kabul in great portion in the manufactures of
  `Tafoi.`[text-layer?] which they first `obta.ined`[text-layer?] cheap and then transferred to His
  Majesty at false and exorbitant valuation . '" (p. 154, folio 145) — **`Tafoi.` is a damaged place
  or product name and is not repaired.**
- **The British abolition, dated, and what was left standing.** "When the British annexed Sind in 1843
  they took immediate steps to abolish the more pernicious of these local exactions, which under the
  Kalhora and Talpiirs had attained a remarkable degree of com­prehensiveness." (pp. 156–157,
  folios 147–148) What remained: "land, fisheries, sea and frontier customs, excise on spirits and
  drugs, judicial fees, fines and miscellaneous items of small importance." (p. 157, folio 148)
  **Sorley's continuity claim cuts against a communal reading:** the old transit dues are "the direct
  descen­dants" of present-day octroi, terminal and toll taxes, and the whole system "was derived from
  the practice of Hindu dynasties" and merely adopted by the Mughals (p. 156, folio 147).
- **The zamindar as the working unit of the system.** "the zamindars occupied the position · of a
  honourable middle class… He paid the revenue, he made regular advances to the cultivators, and he
  enjoyed a large number of feudal privileges in respect of the husbandmen who tilled his land"
  (p. 165); he "was always held individually responsible for the revenue of the whole" (p. 165); he
  paid "his workpeople of every description in grain, even the carpenter who mended the wooden plough
  and the patwari who weighed the grain at the time of batai" (p. 165); "In some places the zamindar
  elected a sort of representative for the management of public affairs. He was called Mukhadam or
  Arbab, a title that has not yet died out in Sind." (p. 165) His standing in the village is social as
  well as fiscal: "He was a kind of father confessor to his husbandmen and his influence was often able
  to settle their never-ending disputes about women, and the theft of cattle." (p. 166, folio 157)
- **Jaghirs as an obstacle to state control of water.** "Canals could often be seen running parallel
  with each other for long distances and the frequent jaghirs made it very difficult for the
  Government to do much in the way of unifying' control." (p. 140, folio 131) **The chunk gives no
  example, no grantee and no religious grant** — **(not stated)**.
- **The shikargah question, raised and left open.** "In the times of the Kalh6ra it does not appear
  that the best shikargahs … had been developed to anything like the extent they reached in the early
  nineteenth century. But it would be unreasonable to believe that shikargahs did not exist in the
  days of the Kalhora, who maintained an almost regal state." (p. 148, folio 139) European observers
  asserted "that the shikargahs had swallowed up a large share of the ·assessable cultivable land"
  (p. 154, folio 145, footnote), an assertion Sorley says "is not corroborated".

### Customs, trade and the commercial institutions of Sind

- **Taxation described as comprehensive in one sentence.** "·An elaborate system of taxation impinged
  on every part · of the business of manufacture, distribution and sale, with profession taxes, transit
  dues, customs and imposts on retail sales." (p. 95, folio 86) **Four named heads, no rates.**
- **The customs house as a working institution, described from four directions.** Manrique's imperial
  pass gave "free passage through all the choquidares or customs posts" (p. 82, folio 73); at Bakhar
  "There was little trouble at the custom house" (p. 83, folio 74); at Sehwan "the custom house was
  passed `�i�hout` `trq.uble`[text-layer?]" (p. 84, folio 75); at Tatta arrivals waited on board for
  the officials, and bedding and "' other necessary utensils '" were "not liable `fo`[text-layer?]
  duty" (p. 84, folio 75). **How an imperial order was recognised and executed:** the Governor of
  Tatta "placed it most submissively three times on `his_head`[text-layer?], kissing it a similar
  number of times. After reading it, he sent for· the customs officials and before `n;ie`[text-layer?]
  told them to pass· the merchandise through at `o�ce`[text-layer?]; charging only low
  `rate�`[text-layer?] ·of duty" (p. 84, folio 75). **The cash value of an exemption:** Manrique was
  pressed to lend his firman to merchants' goods — "' my `orcler`[text-layer?] would have secured two
  thousand rupees '" — and refused (p. 84, folio 75).
- **Port clearance at Laribunder, quoted whole from the *Tarikh-i-Tahiri*.** Signal gun, guard-house
  reply, word sent to "the `mer�hants`[text-layer?] of Tatta", boats out to the guard post,
  questioning of "Every vessel and every trader", and the rule that a stranger ship "can go no farther
  ; its cargo is transferred to boats and forwarded to the city" (p. 99, folio 90, citing "Elliot : I,
  p. 277."). **A full working description of a pre-colonial port procedure, signal to discharge.**
- **The parwana as an instrument that is issued and not enforced.** "Ghulam Shah `KalhOro`[text-layer?]
  did grant such a parwana to the second factory, but he was always ready to allow native interlopers
  from the Persian Gulf to trade in saltpetre." (p. 104, folio 95); the Resident's protest: "'
  Notwithstanding your perwanna that the Honble English Company should alone purchase and export
  saltpetre from your country, the Porebunder and other merchants at Shahbunder publickly purchase and
  export it without the least impediment from your officers…'" (p. 67) The two parwanas of 1758 and
  1761 are given with their exact fiscal terms — a duty ceiling of "one and a half per cent… above the
  market price" on purchases and half the ordinary rate on imports, no duty on returned goods, ships'
  supplies free, help to build a house "at the bunder or at Tattah", and a monopoly clause excluding
  all other Europeans, plus **salvage rights** (pp. 59–60, folios 50–51; p. 46). **And a Kalhora ruler
  alienating a fiscal right of the state:** "he relinquished in favour of the Company the old imperial
  prerogative of the government to all wrecks and wreckage and salvaged goods." (p. 116, folio 107)
- **Escheat as the tax on visible wealth, and the trader's response to it.** "This all men exclaime
  againest, being the worst (they say) of all ill customes that ever came upp yett ; for now if a
  merchant of fashion or any other that hath meanes dyes his wealth will all be seized on though he
  hath children and brothers to leave it unto." (p. 54, folio 45) And its behavioural consequence:
  brokers and dalals "probably managed to amass · considerable wealth, though it was fatal to display
  any kind of opulence lest the capricious covetousness of the local · officials should devise some
  method of confiscating it" (p. 100, folio 91) — **which is Sorley's naturalistic explanation of
  bania frugality, offered against a pious one.**
- **Bribery, farmed customs and "regular tips".** "The need felt by the Company's officers to keep on
  good terms with the Sind officials led to the giving of presents and inducements to **the farmers of
  the customs**, the customs officers and the clerks employed" (p. 68, folio 59), with the sums —
  about 60 rupees of perpets as "a custom long since introduced by Mr. Sumption" — and a fixed
  anchorage of "25 rupees to the Jham" at the bar (p. 68, folio 59). Sorley euphemises and flags that
  he is doing so: "Without these illegal gratifications or (to put it more politely) regular tips the
  Company's business would have been hampered more than it was already" (p. 68, folio 59). And at
  Bucker and Derbella, a boat owing "`!`[text-layer?] of a rupee" in dues paid "6 rupees officers fees
  (or rather bribes)" (p. 55, folio 46).
- **The dadan (advance) system: how export capital reached the weaving household.** "The ordinary
  method of `busine&s`[text-layer?] was for the Company to employ Hindu brokers or gumashtas, who
  `arr�nged`[text-layer?] direct with the producers of the goods, paid them advances made by the
  Company, and provided the goods under the supervision of the `factor�`[text-layer?]" (p. 113,
  folio 104); Sarkar, quoted, on "' dadan '' or advances to individual workmen and looking after them
  in their `cotta.ges`[text-layer?]" (pp. 113–114, folios 104–105). **And the enforcement, in Sorley's
  own voice — the clearest statement in the book of state office rented to commercial interest:** "The
  subedar or faujdar had often to be bribed to bring pressure on the workmen to work honestly."
  (p. 114, folio 105)
- **The kafila as an armed commercial institution, with two incompatible scales.** "It was the
  practice to protect the camel caravans (kafilas) with a guard of one hundred to two hundred horses"
  (p. 91, folio 82); Hamilton's own was "' of 1500 beasts and as many more men and women, besides 200
  horse for our guard '" (p. 92, folio 83); Thévenot says "caravans commonly consisted of above . two
  hundred wagons" (p. 91, folio 82). **Both are printed.** Guards colluded: "' But often these
  protectors suffer the cafillas to be robbed… and afterwards come in shares with the robbers.'"
  (p. 91, folio 82) The seasons: kafilas "had regular seasons for travel which avoided the worst of
  the hot weather and inundation in Upper Sind and the intense winter cold" (p. 98, folio 89).
- **The serai as an institution, described twice.** Hamilton's stage was "a ' mud wall fort called
  Dungham '", built "midway between Tatta and Laribunder to secure the kafila from being set upon· at
  night, ' who all lodge within it, men and beasts promiscuously '", with "' about twenty little
  cottages built close to it '" selling fowls, goats and sheep (p. 92, folio 83). Manucci's general
  account gives the rules — gates closed at sunset, horses picketed, "' three warnings '" at six in
  the morning, doors kept shut until lost property is found, soldiers excluded, capacity "' from 800
  to `rooo`[text-layer?] persons with their horses, camels, carriages '" (pp. 92–93, folios 83–84).
- **Indigenous banking, named as an institution that still exists.** Navaldas "must have been one of
  the merchants called Multanis, who have given their name to an efficient banking business, men whose
  names are still current in the indigenous banking and commercial transactions of this portion of
  Asia." (p. 113, folio 104)
- **Three currencies at once, and the small-change problem that hit the weaver.** "three kinds of
  rupees were in circulation in Sind" (p. 70, folio 61), with "N. Rs." glossed "i.e. Nadir Shah
  rupees" and "34 Ticca rupees a piece" as a third (pp. 70–71, folios 61–62); the subdivision in use
  is the *rea* (p. 69, folio 60; p. 76, folio 67). "The shortage of small coin often hindered the
  factors… when they had to make payments in the villages to weavers, who . being poor men, thought
  and worked in terms of the copper coinage." (p. 134, folio 125) **The village weaver paid in copper
  is the one household-level economic actor the book describes directly.** Two currency classes are
  named: "At any particular time the current rupee (chalani) was accepted as standard, and earlier
  issues (khazana) were accepted subject to differing amounts of discount." (p. 133, folio 124) There
  was an imperial mint at Tatta, and "There must have been a mint master in charge of the mint at
  Tatta but I have not come across any reference to him." (p. 150, folio not printed)
- **The industries of Moghul and Kalhora Sind, listed, and what did not exist.** "During Moghul and
  Kalhoro times the chief industries were the manufacture of cotton goods; saltpetre and indigo,
  leather and fish." (p. 101, folio 92) "Flour milling as an industry did not e·xist. Everyone ground
  his own flour in his own j andri (grinding mill)… Oil pressing was another village industry, widely
  diffused, the camels and bullocks turning the ghans (oil presses) , crushing the j ambho and rape.
  seed just as they do today. The potter's craft was equally `primitiv�`[text-layer?]
  `.arid`[text-layer?] can still be seen in Sind exactly as it was in Moghul and Kalhoro days."
  (p. 101, folio 92)
- **The cotton geography of Sind, and its weaving towns.** "almost all the important cotton
  manufacturing places were on the river or within easy reach of it-Bakhar, Rohri, Darbelo, Gambat,
  Kandiaro, Sehwan, Sann, `Nasarpiir`[text-layer?] and Tatta" (p. 98, folio 89); "There were large
  communities of weavers at Lahore, Multan, Sukkur, Tatta and other towns on the rivers of the Indus
  system" (p. 102, folio 93). **Household counts from the first factory: Nasarpur "near upon 3000
  families of weavers"; Sehwan "About rooo[text-layer?] households of weavers"; Tatta "about 3000
  families of weavers" (p. 50).** Place-level for the weaving-town sentence: **abul-faiz-qalander-ali-suharwardi**, **bibi-pak-daman**, **darbar-abul-muali-qadri**, **data-darbar**, **ganj-e-inayat-sarkar**, **ghazi-ilm-din-shaheed**, **gurdwara-babay-nanki**, **gurdwara-baoli-sahib-guru-arjan-dev-ji-lahore**, **gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-jhalian-jhalian-dhilwan**, **gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-mozang**, **gurdwara-chowmala-sahib**, **gurdwara-dera-sahib**, **gurdwara-guru-ram-das-ji**, **gurdwara-patshahi-chhevin-hadiara-lahore**, **gurdwara-shaheed-bhai-taru-singh**, **gurdwara-shaheed-ganj-singh-singhnian**, **jain-mandir-lahore**, **khawaja-feroz-ud-din-gharib-nawaz**, **krishna-mandir-ravi-road**, **loh-temple-lava-temple**, **madho-lal-hussain**, **mazar-e-iqbal**, **peer-makki**, **samadhi-of-maharaja-ranjit-singh**, **shah-jamal**, **shrine-of-baba-shah-chiragh**, **shrine-of-baba-shah-kamal**, **shrine-of-mauj-darya-bukhari**, **shrine-of-mian-mir**, **shrine-of-miran-hussain-zanjani-zanjani-sahib**, **shrine-of-shah-inayat-qadiri**, **tahir-bandagi-qadri**, **tomb-of-qutbuddin-aibak**, **valmik-mandir-naqi-road**, **wasif-ali-wasif** (the book's "Lahore"); **prahladpuri-temple**, **shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya**, **shrine-of-hafiz-muhammad-jamal-multani**, **shrine-of-hazrat-shah-ali-akbar-shah-ali-akbar-shamsi**, **shrine-of-shah-rukn-e-alam**, **shrine-of-shah-shams-ud-din-sabzwari**, **shrine-of-shah-yusaf-gardez**, **shrine-of-syed-musa-pak** (the book's "Multan"); **gurdwara-dash-mesh-pita**, **gurdwara-pehli-patshahi-jind-pir-sukkur**, **kalka-cave-temple-asthan-of-kalka-devi**, **sadh-belo-sadh-belo-island-temple**, **sant-baba-asudaram-darbar-panno-aqil** (the book's "Sukkur"); **gurdwara-pehli-patshahi-jind-pir-sukkur**, **sadh-belo-sadh-belo-island-temple** (the book's "Rohri"); **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**, **shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar** (the book's "Sehwan").
- **Saltpetre, and the fact that it was dug out of old inhabited ground.** It is "extracted from the
  earth on which towns or villages formerly stood or where any number of men and cattle have been
  encamped for any considerable time" (p. 67); "Indeed it was camping grounds and old inhabited sites
  which were the chief source of supply of saltpetre in those days, the mineral being removed by the
  refinement of the manure­laden soil." (p. 92, folio 83) **Directly relevant to settlement
  archaeology in Sind: old sites were mined.** Output "was estimated up to 10,000 maunds (crude)
  annually" (p. 104, folio 95), the grounds near Tatta were "exhausted" by 1799 (p. 105, folio 96),
  and "Saltpetre was a commodity strictly watched by the Moghul administration." (p. 104, folio 95)
- **The import and export lists of Sind at the annexation.** Imports: "English piecegoods, velvets,
  woollens, sugar, spices and metals-mostly from Bombay ; cotton, coarse cloths and silk
  `fabi-ics`[text-layer?] from Marwar and Guj arat ; silk manufactured goods from
  `Bahawalpiir`[text-layer?] and Multan ; dyes, drugs, dried -fruits, horses from the North-West."
  Exports of Sind's own production: "rice, ghi, opium, indigo, dried fish and leather." Internal
  trade: "Upper Sind supplying Lower Sind with dry grains…, ghi, cotton and indigo and receiving in
  return rice, fish, manufactured leather and arms." (p. 101, folio 92) **Talpur autarky is stated as
  policy:** "This was the condition of trade after half a century `ofTalpiir`[text-layer?] rule during
  which the chief object had been to shut Sind off from the outside world." (p. 101, folio 92)
- **The closed country as deliberate policy from 1701.** "The aim of all Sind policy from 1701 onwards
  was to make Sind independent of Moghul, Persian and Pathan, to diminish the payment of tribute to
  the suzerain authority, to preserve the land as a closed terrain into which no foreigners of any
  sort were allowed entrance except with the utmost difficulty." (pp. 35–36, printed 26–27) **The
  result:** Sind remained "a terra incognita" despite two factories (p. 36, printed 27), and after
  1775 "Sind relapsed into a condition of lethargy which it maintained till, -after 1843, it was again
  drawn into the maelstrom of world politics" (p. 116, folio 107).
- **The two factories, dated, and the ninety-six-year gap between them.** A factory "from 1636 to 1662
  and again from 1758 to 1775" (p. 28, printed 19); "twenty-seven years in the seventeenth century,
  and for seventeen years in the eighteenth century. Between the two periods there was a complete
  blank of ninety-six years" (p. 48); the chronology dates them "1636 English �actory[text-layer?]
  established at Tatta", "1662 English · Factory in Sind given up", "1 758[text-layer?] English
  Company's Factory established at Tatta", "1 775[text-layer?] Misgovernment in Sind. English factory
  at Tatta given up" (pp. 200, 202; folios 191, 193). **The Sind establishment had a surgeon but
  "There appears never to have been a Chaplain. So the Sind factories have no shrewd commentator of
  the stamp of Terry or Ovington to give graphic details of the everyday life." (p. 111, folio 102) —
  a direct statement about the evidential base of the whole book.**
- **The urban and commercial map shifts in the later eighteenth century.** "the rise, towards the
  second half of the eighteenth century, of the four large towns of modern Sind, Karachi, Hyderabad,
  Sukkur and `Shikarpiir`[text-layer?], which displaced Tatta, `Nasarpiir`[text-layer?], Rohri,
  Sehwan, Kandiaro and a host of small `pfo.ces`[text-layer?] in the economic scheme" (p. 30, printed
  21); "Both H yderabad and Karachi date from the second half of the eighteenth century.
  Shikarpiir[text-layer?], founded by the Daiidpotras[text-layer?] in 1616, did not attain any size
  till the later decades of the eighteenth century… Sukkur as it now exists is a very modern place.
  Prior to the nine­teenth century it was a small riverside village in no way so important as its
  neighbour on the opposite bank, Rohri, which it has managed to outgrow to almost four times the
  size." (p. 20, folio 11) Place-level: **bhagnari-mandir**, **darya-lal-mandir-darya-lal-sankat-mochan-mandir**, **jhollay-lal-mandir**, **shree-ratneshwar-mahadev-temple-karachi**, **shri-laxmi-narayan-mandir-native-jetty-bridge**, **shri-panchmukhi-hanuman-mandir-karachi**, **shri-swaminarayan-mandir-karachi**, **shri-varun-dev-mandir**, **shrine-of-abdullah-shah-ghazi**, **shrine-of-pir-mangho**, **shrine-of-qalandar-baba-auliya** (the book's "Karachi"); **guru-gurpat-mandir-db-80-sirey-ghat** (the book's "Hyderabad"); **gurdwara-dash-mesh-pita**, **gurdwara-pehli-patshahi-jind-pir-sukkur**, **kalka-cave-temple-asthan-of-kalka-devi**, **sadh-belo-sadh-belo-island-temple**, **sant-baba-asudaram-darbar-panno-aqil** (the book's "Sukkur"); **gurdwara-sach-khand-sahib**, **khatwari-darbar-shikarpur**, **sant-bhagat-kanwar-ram-temple-chak** (the book's "Shikarpiir"[text-layer?]); **gurdwara-pehli-patshahi-jind-pir-sukkur**, **sadh-belo-sadh-belo-island-temple** (the book's "Rohri"); **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**, **shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar** (the book's "Sehwan").
- **The Shikarpur caravan trade and its collapse.** Merchants came down annually "bringing with them
  both money and other goods, such as putchuck, ring shawls etc., and carrying back quantities of the
  different kinds of imported merchandize which were from thence distributed in to the internal
  provinces"; after "the last eight or ten years hardly any have ventured down" (p. 62, folio 53).
  Place-level: **gurdwara-sach-khand-sahib**, **khatwari-darbar-shikarpur**, **sant-bhagat-kanwar-ram-temple-chak** (the book's "Shikarpur").

### Population, wages, prices and the condition of the poor

- **Four incompatible population estimates, set side by side and not resolved.** Del Hoste "calculated
  in 1832 that the population was 850,000, of which about one-fourth were Hindus" (p. 126, folio 117);
  Burnes "in 1828 estimated the population at about one million" (p. 126, folio 117); the Commissioner
  in Sind in 1847 "put the total population of Sind at l,274,732[text-layer?]" (p. 127, folio 118);
  Pottinger's Tatta figures are quoted and discarded, "But Pottinger is rightly distrustful · of the
  accuracy of these figures for the KalhOro days" (p. 126, folio 117). Sorley: "From this conflicting
  evidence it is very difficult to reach any very satisfactory conclusion" (p. 127, folio 118) — and
  then concludes anyway: "during Moghul and Kalhorn[text-layer?] days . the population cannot have
  exceeded one million altogether, or less than a quarter of what it is at the present day. The
  difference today is accounted for by the vast extension of irrigation since the date of the British
  annexation." (p. 127, folio 118) Burnes's town breakdown is given as "25,000 were in Shikarpiir,
  20,000 in Hyderabad, 18,ooo[text-layer?] in Tatta, 15 ,000 in Khairpiir and Karachi, and
  ro,ooo[text-layer?] in Sehwan and Mirpiir" (p. 126, folio 117) — **two of the numerals are damaged
  and the Khairpur/Karachi figure is a single number for two places.**
- **The one demographic fact Sorley treats as certain.** "But one fact is certain, namely that between
  1740 and 1 800[text-layer?] there was a great shifting of the population of Sind, the chief
  concentration altering from Tatta to Shikarpiir, Hyderabad and Karachi." (p. 127, folio 118)
- **The largest mortality figure in the book, and it is about weavers.** "Hamilton in 1699 mentions
  that three years previously, because of a severe plague caused by the absence of rain in Tatta,
  80,000 weavers perished." (p. 143, folio 134); "80,000 people (from Tatta and the neighbourhood) who
  manufactured cotton and silk and above one half of the city was deserted and left empty." (p. 103,
  folio 94) **The book never performs the arithmetic to 1696 and that year is (not stated).** **Set it
  against the book's own headline claim that "famines such as devastated less fortunate parts of India
  had ordinarily no terrors for the Sindhi peasant" (p. 129, folio 120) — the two are not
  reconciled.**
- **Famine and plague in the factory records, 1659–60.** "being the famine and plague in Scinda is so
  great that it hath swept away most part of the people" (p. 56, folio 47); and 13 April 1660: "in the
  latter place neaver famine raged worse in any place, the living being hardly able to burye the
  dead." (p. 56, folio 47) Also a three-season compound failure in Aurangzeb's own jaghir, "drought,
  locusts, plague and floods in three successive seasons" (p. 143, folio 134), and war scarcity: "In
  1759 there was great scarcity of grain caused by the demands of the troops engaged in the Sind civil
  war." (p. 143, folio 134)
- **Famine denied for Sind as a structural claim about the Indus.** "Vincent Smith quotes Sir Harcourt
  Butler to the effect that ' Famine lies broad written across the pages of Indian history.' This, too,
  is a half truth grossly misleading as a `.generalization`[text-layer?]… Of Sind it is particularly
  untrue. Sind is one of those lucky lands that are not seriously troubled if the
  `monsoon·rains`[text-layer?] fail, because Sind is the creation, the slow geographical accretion, of
  a river that provides life-giving water with unfailing regularity" (p. 118, folio 109). The
  calamities that do occur are itemised: "unchecked `inunda­tion`[text-layer?], of drifting sand that
  covers fertile land, of changing river channels and of the capricious vagaries of a wandering flood
  that washes villages away in a night." (p. 118, folio 109)
- **The wage evidence, and the fact that almost none of it is Sindhi.** "There is very little extant
  evidence of theJates[text-layer?] of wages paid in Moghul and KalhOro days in Sind" — so Sorley
  imports the *Ain*'s statistics and figures from Masulipatam, Surat, Agra and Akbar's court, on the
  ground that there is "no reason to believe that the statistics of the Ain … are untrue as far as
  Sind is concern_ed" (p. 130, folio 121). **Almost every wage figure in the book is therefore not a
  Sindhi observation.** The table as printed: ordinary labourers "2 or 3 dams per day, that is,
  one-twentieth of a rupee"; skilled workmen "Rs. 3 or 3![text-layer?] per month"; household servants
  "Rs. 2 per month"; "One and a half dams were sufficient to provide a man with daily susten­ance"
  (p. 130, folio 121). Soldiers under the Amirs: "the smallest sum paid to footmen under native rule
  was about three and a half rupees per mensem, but some of the horsemen got the respectable salary of
  one rupee a day" (p. 131, folio 122). A doctor at Shahbunder in 1764, "Haji Mahmud at the rate of
  Rs. 15 per month" (p. 144, folio 135). **The purchasing-power conclusion:** "the value of the rupee
  in terms of food fell in the ratio of from 7 to .I[text-layer?] between the close of Akbar's reign
  and the twentieth century… The wages of Rs. 3 to Rs. 5 per month … thus correspond fairly closely in
  actual purchasing power . with present-day standards." (p. 131, folio 122)
- **Food prices as a livestock list.** "' In Sind a m�ch[text-layer?] cow costs 12 rupees, the best
  camels 25 or 30 each, a cow for slaughter 6 rupees, th&[text-layer?] best sheep one and a half
  "rupees, goats from r[text-layer?] to 4 · rupees and the finest fowls are ten for one rupee.'"
  (p. 129, folio 120) At Laribunder in December 1635, "henns at four pice each, sheep at a rupee each,
  rice and butter very cheap" (p. 49). Against which, siege prices at Bakhar in 1659: "' I bought two
  calves for six hundred rupees and paid `o'ne`[text-layer?] rupee for every ounce of butter… I
  bought one chicken for thirteen rupees. '" (p. 89, folio 80)
- **Debt, and the moneylender's mechanism, described precisely.** "The peasant and the poor man in
  India have always been at the mercy of the moneylender, who in Sind employed the resource of
  advancing money against a share of the crop, and making the loan partly in grain at prices which
  suited the moneylender but bore heavily against the cultivator and the artisan. The system still
  prevails and legislation can apparently do little to check it." (p. 132, folio 123) The
  Commissioner's 1847 version: cultivators "may in fact be considered as mere labourers, the wages of
  whose labour is limited to a mere subsistence while the creditors are a class by whom, as the means
  of providing the agri­cultural stock are advanced, its profits are realised.'" (p. 132, folio 123)
- **Five reasons the poor could not save, itemised by Sorley — and the fifth is ritual expenditure.**
  "the climatic conditions of the country, the risk of uncertain yield of crops, lack of industrial
  development, overpopula­tion in comparison with the resources available, and **social customs which
  require heavy expenditure for special occasions, despite the fact that the purse is nearly empty**"
  (p. 132, folio 123). **Sorley does not say what the occasions are** — **(not stated)**. His named
  principal burden is elsewhere: "The real burden on the peasant was the arbitrariness of the
  autocratic form of government with its constant exorbitant demands." (p. 132, folio 123)
- **Zakat and the Hindu joint family as the two poverty-relief institutions — named, and judged
  inadequate.** "The religion :of[text-layer?] Islam provided a means of. assuaging poverty in the
  system of zakat and the Hindu social polity employed the joint family system to the same end. But as
  the effectiveness of both these institutions depended largely on private conscience, which works
  capriciously, they were poor alleviations of the distress caused by the autocracy and oppression of
  those in power. An active public conscience "in these matters had not yet made itself felt in
  India." (pp. 132–133, folios 123–124) **This is the only passage in the book that treats religion as
  an economic institution of relief, and it mentions no shrine, no endowment and no langar.**
- **Forced labour as the actual builder of the canal system.** "In the eighth year of his reign
  Aurangzeb had issued orders against forced labour (begari) . But it is quite certain these orders
  were not enforced in Sind… Indeed several of the biggest canals were dug by labour con­scripted in
  this way. One of the best canals in Upper Sind is actually still called the ' Begari ' ." (p. 130,
  folio 121) **The regnal year is given; the calendar year is (not stated).** "A whole district was
  liable to be called out to do canal clearance and usually received a certain quantity of food in
  payment." (p. 140, folio 131), with the failure case in the footnote — the Meani tribe conscripted,
  "' but as neither pay nor food was for any length of time affo_rded[text-layer?] these poor people,
  they invariably deserted '". The abolition file is named: `E. OFFICIAL CORRESPONDENCE ON THE
  ABOLITION OF STATU'I'.E[text-layer?] OR FORCED LABOUR IN $IND[text-layer?]. Vol. 34. 1856.`
  (p. 190, folio 181).
- **Child labour as a barrier to schooling, in Sorley's own present tense.** "even today in Sind one of
  the chief obstacles in the education of the peasant class is the fact that children of eight and
  upwards earn a few rupees a month by herding cattle and camels, and so cannot without financial
  sacrifice be sent to school. It is certain. that in Moghul and KalhOro days similar conditions were
  universal" (p. 132, folio 123). **A 1940 statement used as evidence about the eighteenth century.**
- **Cheap pleasures, and the drugs named.** "smoking, usually indulged · in to excess, while ' a
  strong spirit distilled from giir[text-layer?] is in great request amongst all who can afford to
  purchase it. The lower orders use bhang and intoxicating and very deleterious drugs obtained from
  hemp in large quantities ' ." (p. 133, folio 124) The named liquors — "' `giir jo damn`[text-layer?]
  ', made of molasses and babul bark, and ' `kattal jo damn`[text-layer?] ', a spirit extracted from
  dates"; "' `angiiri`[text-layer?] ' made of grapes at Hyderabad, Sehwan and Shikarpiir…, ' soufi '…,
  ' mushki '…, ' `taranji`[text-layer?] '…, ' misri '…, ' gulabi '… and ' kaysari ' stained with
  saffron" (p. 147, folio 138) — **and the repeated word "damn" is almost certainly not the book's
  word; it is carried unrepaired.** The hemp preparations are given with their methods: "' bhang ' ('
  sawo ' or ' sukho ')…, ' `ganj o`[text-layer?] ' the top of the hemp plant, which was smoked in
  hookahs and ' charas ' the gum of the hemp", plus "' majur '", "' khosh-khosh '" and "' `dhatiira jo
  bij`[text-layer?] '" (p. 147, folio 138). Place-level for the wine towns: **guru-gurpat-mandir-db-80-sirey-ghat** (the book's "Hyderabad"); **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**, **shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar** (the book's "Sehwan"); **gurdwara-sach-khand-sahib**, **khatwari-darbar-shikarpur**, **sant-bhagat-kanwar-ram-temple-chak** (the book's "Shikarpiir"[text-layer?]).
- **Sport, gambling and dance, defended by Sorley as evidence in a welfare argument.** Wrestling "by '
  malhs ' was as popular as it is today… The best wrestlers were the African negroes, brought across
  originally as slaves from Abyssinia, but the sport was also indulged in by the pahilwans (or strong
  men) of the agricultural class in the villages." (p. 149, folio 140) **The only reference to slavery
  in that chunk, given in passing with no date, number or route** — **(not stated)**; the wording is
  Sorley's and is of its period. Also "`Kabiitar-bazi`[text-layer?], betting on pigeons, kukar bazi or
  cock fighting, `ghOta bazi`[text-layer?] or ram fighting, popular at the daira" (p. 149, folio 140);
  board games "shatranj (chess) , nard (backgammon) , pachis, dhara (dice) , `chOwpar`[text-layer?],
  gunjifa (cards)… at which the women were very adept, if Burton is to be believed" (p. 149,
  folio 140); "folk dances, of which Sind possessed and still possesses a great variety and in which
  all classes joined with the utmost abandon" (p. 149, folio 140); kite flying (patang) (p. 148,
  folio 139). **A negative finding stated as such:** "Polo … does not appear to have been played in
  Sind" (p. 148, folio 139). **And the whole recreation section's purpose, stated by Sorley himself:**
  it exists to rebut Moreland, whose "gloomy picture of social conditions … must therefore be
  considerably toned down" (p. 149, folio 140).
- **Public health and the disease register.** From Burnes: "fatal epidemics and frightful pestilence,
  resembling in some points the plague of Egypt"; "ague, asthma, rheumatism and pulmonary consumption";
  "Dropsy and enlargement of the spleen … and at certain periods a virulent ophthalmia caused by clouds
  of fine dust" (p. 144, folio 135). Carless in "1 836-7" found coastal villagers besieging him for
  medicines, the ravages of disease "' disgustingly apparent '" (p. 144, folio 135). **And a lever of
  diplomacy:** "The Company's surgeons were in continual demand both in the seventeenth and the
  eighteenth century for attendance on the local officials and the rulers of Sind. The Kalhora in
  `partiq1lar`[text-layer?] were . very unwilling to release the Company's surgeon once he had been
  permitted to attend their Court." (p. 143, folio 134) Sorley notes that this whole class of suffering
  "receives no mention at all in the pages of any of the native annalists" (p. 144, folio 135).
- **Housing and the economic logic of impermanence.** The poor lived in "the grass hut by the river or
  in the fields, the dwelling of twigs covered with a conical roof like a beehive…, or unsightly
  erections of mud" (p. 122, folio 113), because such huts were "capable of . speedy erection in the
  fields and removable when the floods inundated the land", and because "such residences in a land
  liable to be overrun at any time by riotous soldiery could be burnt with small trouble and so by
  such devastation help to dissuade the soldiers from turning that way." (p. 123, folio 114)
  **Impermanent building read as a defence against plundering troops.** The *wandh* is defined and
  sized: "' a collection of hovels, built of straw and wattles · and some plastered over with mud…
  The `popuJation`[text-layer?] of these wandhs seldom exceed 600 or 700 but there is one called
  `Kahorani`[text-layer?] forty miles south west of Hyderabad which is stated to contain 4,000 souls.
  '" (p. 125, folio 116, footnote 3) Villages were raised on mounds and sometimes ditched against the
  inundation (pp. 125, 136; folios 116, 127), and the zamindar's house was the fortified exception,
  "mud fronts with a tower at each corner", with watch towers "in almost all the villages" (p. 125,
  folio 116, footnote 2) and "' kots ' or high walls" for shelter (p. 161, folio 152). **The Talpur
  palace at Hyderabad is measured and dismissed:** "' there is not a single room in the palace which
  exceeds 30 feet in length… The bazaar of Hyderabad is very mean '" (pp. 123–124, folios 114–115,
  footnote 1).
- **The village as the book's baseline unit.** "Communications were poor. The villages were isolated.
  Capital was not easily available. The small craftsman working independently for himself was the
  chief source of production." (p. 95, folio 86) "The glowing accounts of such cities as Ahmedabad,
  Lahore, Agra, Delhi, Tatta and Surat did not apply to the villages where the bulk of the population
  lived on a bare subsistence level." (p. 95, folio 86) Village demography from Lieutenant James:
  Chandookah "contained no fewer than three hundred and ninety-two of such villages. The population in
  most of them would not have exceeded five hundred and most of them were inhabited by
  `t4e`[text-layer?] family and relatives … of the head man of the place, a sort of petty feudal lord"
  (p. 125, folio 116). **And the social geography inside the village:** the built core "was usually
  inhabited by the petty Hindu traders while the Musulman cultivators dwelt in their brushwood landhis
  round about it under the protection of the feudal chief. or headman" (p. 126, folio 117).
- **Social mobility from the subsistence level, stated as a general law — and the example is the rise
  of mendicants to thrones.** "It is absurd to say of Sind that the common man lacked opportunity when
  weavers conquered the country of `Bahawalpiir`[text-layer?], and the `off­spring`[text-layer?] of
  mendicants and shepherds from the `Baliichistan`[text-layer?] hills sat successively upon the throne
  of Sind." (p. 118, folio 109) **"mendicants" is the book's word here and it does not gloss it, does
  not say *faqir*, and does not name the dynasties meant** — **(not stated)**.

### Irrigation as an institution

- **The blunt opening claim, and whose work the system is.** "There is no evidence that the Moghul
  administrators did anything to maintain or improve the irrigation of Sind." (p. 135, folio 126);
  "the present system of canals is the work of generations of cultivators who by rule of thumb methods
  succeeded … in bringing water on to the land" (p. 135, folio 126); "The Sind canals, Persian
  `wheeis`[text-layer?] and wells were constructed by the natives of the land without any assistance
  from the Empire of Delhi, which showed no interest in them at any time." (p. 122, folio 113)
- **The four canal-clearance regimes, as printed — the most institutionally precise passage in the
  book.** "(r)[text-layer?] the clearance of the large canals (called ' wah ') was done by the
  Government alone in some cases ; (z)[text-layer?] in other cases the clearance was done by the
  Government but the cost was partially recouped from the cultivators, called ' sherakati ' (or
  sharing system) ; (3) in the case of other canals, the smaller ones, the Government made an
  allowance of a certain number of ' khasas-'r[text-layer?] -in the ' kharar 'I[text-layer?] of
  produce as the government share of clearance­mukhadimi ; (4) in all other cases the cultivators
  cleared each watereourse[text-layer?] and smaller channels, called ' kario ' or ' kasi ', entirely
  at their own expense." (p. 140, folio 131) **The measures, from the footnote: "' Khasa ' i s 1/60
  Kharar, and ' Kharar 850 lb." — the quotation marks in that footnote are misplaced in the text
  layer.** "In Hyderabad and Karachi districts there were no fewer than fifty-six sets of rules
  governing the payment of clearance charges." (p. 141, folio 132)
- **The labour hierarchy, the measures and the corruption.** "The kardar superintended the
  `'Y�rk`[text-layer?] of the kamrios (labourers) and was assisted by amins and mohiris. The amin
  measured the depth of the excavations and the mohiri the length and breadth of them." (p. 141,
  folio 132) "the measure was the ' guz ', which had different standard lengths in almost each
  pargana"; "A cotton rope was used to measure . the work and the rise and fall of the land were
  measured by the eye only." (p. 141, folio 132) Two grades of earthwork are named and priced —
  "' `dhiili`[text-layer?] '" (basket) against "' uchal ' (throwing)", the difference "worth six guz
  (yards) per rupee" (p. 141, folio 132) — and half the payment was in grain: "Usually half of the
  amount paid for was paid in kind and not in money." (p. 141, folio 132) Sanction was discretionary:
  "The Amirs sanctioned as much as they thought fit and the amount depended more on their idea of the
  kardar's honesty than on the necessities of the work… it is certain that there was much corruption
  in carrying out clearance." (p. 141, folio 132) And the field technique that survives: "small
  portions of the canal beds, called takis (bench marks of earth) were left in their original state
  for measurement purposes, **exactly as is done in canal clearance in Sind today**" (p. 141,
  folio 132).
- **The Kalhora record is lost; the physical evidence is read instead.** "Col. Scott says that the
  canal clearance records of the Kalhora are not recoverable, but, from the `temains`[text-layer?] of
  sluices and very large canals, much more trouble and expense appear to have been incurred in days
  previous to the `Talpiirs`[text-layer?]. Amongst all the accounts Col. Scott was able to find only
  one for a brick sluice." (p. 141, folio 132) **A direct statement about the survival of
  eighteenth-century administrative records in Sind, useful well beyond irrigation.**
- **Two named Kalhora canals, a Talpur bund, and a corvée canal.** The Shah canal, "dug by Niir
  Muhammad Kalh6ro, whence i�s[text-layer?] name and also the name of the village of Shahpiir";
  "The Sarfaraz Wah in the Hyderabad district … ascribed to Sarfaraz Khan Kalh6ro" (p. 138,
  folio 129); "the bund or embankment at Ali Bunder across the Fuleli, which is mentioned by Pottinger
  as the work of Fateh Ali Talpiir in 1799" (p. 142, folio 133); and the Begari (p. 130, folio 121).
  **Oral tradition as the evidence for the first class:** "Oral tradition in Sind today ascribes many
  of the older canals now existing to the energy of the Kalh6ra[text-layer?]." (p. 138, folio 129)
- **Ten named canals of one purgunnah, measured — and every figure damaged.** `APPENDIX III / TABLE
  SHOWING THE LENGTH, BREADTH AND DEPTH OF THE CANALS IN THE CHANDOOKAH PURGUNNAH` (p. 198,
  folio 189) lists `M'.ittah[text-layer?] or Nusrat`, `Heerah`, `Kli.yrah[text-layer?]`,
  `Be�rah[text-layer?]`, `Datah`, `Shah`, `Maksoodah`, `Nau.rung[text-layer?]`, `Chulah`, `Meer wah`.
  **Every figure in that table is damaged and none should be used without the PDF.** The same page
  counts the wells: "P�kka[text-layer?] wells,_ 997 ; kutcha wells, 264 ; temporary wells, 2,030 ;
  minor �ari�Is[text-layer?], 877. The number of wells out of repair, 256" (p. 198, folio 189).
- **The technology, named in Sindhi, and the crop–water classification.** "excavate ' khuhados ', at
  which Persian wheels worked" (p. 136, folio 127); "the ' nar ', a big wheel driven by camels, and
  the ' hurlo ' or small wheel driven by a pair of bullocks… Such cultivation is called ' lift '
  because the water has to be lifted on to the land on an endless chain of earthenware pots" (p. 139,
  folio 130); "' lift '" supported "' dry ' crops like bajri, jowari, cotton, sugar cane and
  vegetables", "Wheat and barley were generally grown on flood water, ' sailab ', or on · land that
  had a watering before the floods subsided, called ' b�si[text-layer?] '", and rice was a "' flow '"
  crop (pp. 136, 139; folios 127, 130). **And the water wheel as a social unit:** "each droning water
  wheel, z[text-layer?] with its small ' landhi ' and little orchard of cucumbers and castor oil
  plants and standing trees, is a petty social centre" (pp. 139–140, folios 130–131).
- **Sorley's revisionist thesis — blame the river, not the ruler.** "It may, therefore, be quite
  possible that much of the decay attributed to their carelessness and parsimony by English observers
  was due partly at least to changes in the set of the river"; "if there had been deterioration, it was
  more likely to have been due to changes in the river Indus than to the stupidity of man" (p. 142,
  folio 133). And its corollary: the system "must, within its limits, have been a very important source
  of the country's wealth and was by no means a fit object of harsh and ill-informed criticism."
  (pp. 142–143, folios 133–134) **He also names the structural reason nothing better was done:**
  "Possessing no public works department and with the help only of venal, unreliable and unscrupulous
  revenue officers, they could hardly have been expected to indulge in costly experiments" (p. 142,
  folio 133).
- **And the tension he does not resolve.** Population was static because "irrigation was
  statiC[text-layer?]" (p. 127, folio 118), yet "Sind has always been a pioneer of irrigation in India
  and has had a system of canals and watercourses since the beginning of historical time, tended by
  people who knew very well how to use the bounty of nature" (p. 138, folio 129). **An ancient, skilful
  indigenous system that nevertheless held the population flat; the book does not reconcile the two.**
- **Akbar's Canal Act of 1568, quoted whole and then discounted.** The sanad — "' My govern­ment is a
  tree… my·desire[text-layer?] purer than water is to supply the wants of the poor… by digging canals
  and founding cities, by which too, the revenues of the Empire will be increased.'" (p. 122,
  folio 113, footnote 1) — followed by Sorley's verdict: "The promise, however, was but poorly
  fulfilled. Sind certainly received no benefit in this respect from the Moghuls." **And a model of the
  book stating a silence and deciding anyway:** "This work, however, is noticed by neither Ferishta nor
  the Ain-i-Akbari ; but there seems no reason to disbelieve the authenticity of the evidence."
  (p. 122, folio 113, unnumbered footnote)

### The state: offices, army, tribute and the Kalhora–Baluch bargain

- **The administrative skeleton, and the full office list.** "Sind consisted of two sarkars (divisions)
  of the siibah (province) of Multan. The headquarters of the sarkars were Bakhar for Upper Sind and
  Tatta for Lower Sind and each had a governor (a nabob as the Company letters called him)… The sarkars
  were divided into parganas and mahals." (p. 150, folio not printed) "There were of course,
  `a.mils`[text-layer?], kanungos, faujdars, amins, daroghas, mahalkaris, patils, chaudharis,
  mukhadams, patwaris, zamindars, killedars, kotwals, kazis and munsifs." (p. 150, folio not printed)
  **The kazi is the only religious office in the administrative apparatus, and the book adds nothing
  about its jurisdiction there.** Ecclesiastical office inside the Mughal bureaucracy is noted from
  Vincent Smith: "The only considerable officials who did not take rank as army officers were charged
  with purely ecclesiastical and civil legal duties, such as the Sadars and the Kazis" (p. 33, printed
  24). Place-level for the sarkar of Multan: **prahladpuri-temple**, **shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya**, **shrine-of-hafiz-muhammad-jamal-multani**, **shrine-of-hazrat-shah-ali-akbar-shah-ali-akbar-shamsi**, **shrine-of-shah-rukn-e-alam**, **shrine-of-shah-shams-ud-din-sabzwari**, **shrine-of-shah-yusaf-gardez**, **shrine-of-syed-musa-pak** (the book's "Multan").
- **The army's four classes and its foreign core.** "the tributary chiefs, the troop of mansabdars, the
  dakhili or supplementary troops and the ahadis or gentlemen troopers"; "The best soldiers under the
  Moghul system were foreigners, Pathans, Sayids, Moghuls and Rajpiits." (p. 150, folio not printed)
  Tribal capacity is given in figures: "the Kalmani. Baliichis near Lakhi who mustered 20,000 horsemen
  and the Nahmurdi or Niimria near Sehwan who mustered 300 horsemen and 7,000 foot while the MazarL
  Baliich were a thousand strong." (p. 151, folio 142)
- **The political mechanism Sorley proposes, stated twice.** "It was primarily because the KalhOra
  strengthened the Baliichi elements in Sind and won their confidence that they became powerful enough
  to attain practical independence. It was primarily because the later KalhOra alienated the Baliichi
  clans that they themselves had to give place to the Talpiirs." (p. 150, folio not printed) "When the
  KalhOra quarrelled with the Baliichis they sealed their own fate." (p. 151, folio 142)
- **The fiscal continuity claim, and the change of spirit.** "The structure of the Moghul state
  remained unaltered and its fiscal arrangements were never disturbed by either Kalhoro or Talpiir."
  (p. 152, folio 143); "the government became more a sort of co-operation between the KalhOra and the
  chief Baliichi tribes than it had been in Moghul days." (p. 151, folio 142) And the chapter's formula:
  "while the framework of the Moghul administration in Sind remained little altered in essentials from
  1600 to 1840, the change in spirit was considerable" (p. 153, folio 144), the retained features being
  "its feudal character, its dependence on the good will of the classes possessed of military strength,
  and its efficiency as a revenue collecting machine."
- **Tribute, arrears and evasion as the Kalhora's chronic condition.** They "were supposed to collect
  the revenue, remit what was necessary to Delhi, and maintain law and order" (p. 32, printed 23);
  after 1737 "They were required to pay an annual tribute and usually did their utmost to avoid doing
  so", and "the tribute was gradually reduced in amount and was usually very much in arrears" (p. 36,
  printed 27). Suzerainty passed from Delhi to Persia after Nadir Shah and "about 1747" to "the Pathan
  kingdom at Kabul" (p. 35, printed 26). Tribute was paid partly in English woollens bought cheap and
  transferred "at a high valuation" (p. 57, folio 48), and Ghulam Shah "reduced the tribute to about
  eleven lacks" (p. 61, folio 52). **The Afghan tribute is the structuring fact of Kalhora finance and
  the hinge of the whole quarrel with the Company** (pp. 76–77).
- **Three internal wars dated, and the capital shift.** "The reign of the Kalhora was interrupted by
  three acute spasms of civil war, the first on the death of `Niir Muhammad Kalh6ro`[text-layer?] in
  1754, the second on the deposition of `Sarfataz Khan Kalh6ro`[text-layer?] in `l7J5`[text-layer?],
  and the third and fatal internecine conflict that resulted in the supersession of
  `Kalh6ro`[text-layer?] by `Talpiir`[text-layer?]" (p. 36, printed 27) — **the second date is
  destroyed**. "The imperial officers ceased to be servants of Delhi and the importance of Bakhar and
  Tatta declined with the building of Khudabad and Hyderabad." (p. 151, folio 142)
- **The chupper as an institution.** Afghan officers "under the `appealation`[text-layer?] of Chuppers
  who were sent here for provideing all sorts of necessaries for the army"; everything they bought
  "`we.s` [text-layer?] free of all duties or impositions", which made them "highly disagreeable to the
  Prince" (p. 61, folio 52). **A duty-free purchasing agency of a foreign power operating inside Sind.**
- **A durbar and an investiture, with its full protocol.** At Shahpiir[text-layer?], 28–29 March 1764,
  the Prince gave "his sirpah etc. consisting of kimcob vest, shawl and turband, a knife and sword
  mounted with gold…, a ziga or cluster of emeralds and rubys and a Persian horse with silver plated
  bridle and furniture", and received "his customary gold rupee and other presents in the Honble
  Company's name" (p. 74). **A footnote records "considerable delay in obtaining the money as the
  Prince said he had to collect it from revenues"** (p. 74) — revenue collection as the limit on the
  ruler's liquidity.
- **A class of privileged, tax-exempt people announced and deferred.** "they produced in the end a
  badly proportioned social system with a large number of privileged sections of the population who
  battened on the rest and drew their livelihood from them. These privileged classes were a strange
  mixed throng of very differing interests, of which some account will be given in a later chapter."
  (p. 153, folio 144) **The forward reference lands in the privileged-classes chapter recorded above.**
  Moreland's summary sits in the same footnote: "' It is safe to say that a relatively large number of
  producers contributed half of their gross income to the support of a relatively small number of
  economic parasites. '" (p. 153, folio 144, footnote 1)

### Justice, policing and dispute settlement

- **The stated theory and the stated practice.** "Sind was a Muslim state and in theory justice was
  administered according to the tenets of the Koran. In practice, however, the Shariat did not suffice
  to cover adequately the field of jurisprudence. The Koran is not a legal code ; and not the combined
  force of sunna, ijma and qiyas was adequate for dealing with day-to-day problems… it would be idle to
  pretend that the kazi in seven­teenth and eighteenth century Sind performed the functions of the
  magistrate and the criminal and civil courts of today." (p. 159, folio 150) "In Moghul and
  Kalhorn[text-layer?] days every man was to some extent the defender of his own . rights." (p. 159,
  folio 150)
- **Three forums under the Talpurs.** "Un�er[text-layer?] the Talpiirs there were no regular civil
  courts. Litigants were mostly referred to the kazi or panchayat or to munsiffs selected by the
  parties." (p. 161, folio 152) **Kazi:** "The chief cases decided by the kazi . were disputes
  concerning hereditary property, marriage daims[text-layer?] and the like. They were dealt with under
  the . principles of Muhammadan law. The_ kazi · decided which party sho}lld[text-layer?] take the
  oath and gave a written finding, which was drawn up very carefully and had all the authority . of the
  rulers' sanad." (p. 161, folio 152) **"disputes concerning hereditary property" is the category that
  would cover succession to a shrine gaddi or an endowment; the book does not say so** — **(not
  stated)**. **Panchayat:** "Panchayats dealt with disputes between Hindus, usually in cases where
  there was a question of local custom invol�ed[text-layer?]. The pancliayat[text-layer?] was presided
  over by the mukhi" (p. 161, folio 152). **Munsiff:** used "by Muham­madans and Hfodus[text-layer?]
  alike", with boundary disputes settled "by callirig[text-layer?] on. one of the parties to take the
  oath with the Koran on his head" and a written decision to the winner (p. 161, folio 152). Riverine
  island boundaries were settled "by letting pots float down the river to see on which bank they came
  to rest." (pp. 161–162, folios 152–153)
- **The kardar and the mufti.** "The machinery of the law consisted of kardars who decided case·s
  according to the Koran, which was expounded by muftis, who were learned in such lore." (p. 160,
  folio 151)
- **Policing, quantified, and collective responsibility.** "Judged by present-day standards the police
  force was. lamentably insufficient… **A dozen of these poor creatures were con­sidered sufficient for
  any large town.**" (p. 159, folio 150) "The Kotwal was the chief functionary . invested with
  execu­tive police powers but his jurisdiction did not usually ext�nd[text-layer?] far beyond the town
  `�or`[text-layer?] larger village where he was statiOne�[text-layer?]" (p. 160, folio 151). The
  collective rule: "the village or 1ocality[text-layer?] was responsible for stolen property traced to
  it and proved riot to have gone beyond It[text-layer?]." (p. 160, folio 151); "A stranger arriving at
  a · village could claim ' the protection of the village watchman and if he lost his property, the
  responsibility for the loss lay on: the village." (p. 160, folio 151) **And Sorley's rhetorical
  move:** he quotes a description of collective responsibility, then reveals it is "not a description
  of Sind … but of medievalEngland[text-layer?]", adding that it "might very well apply word for word
  to the Sind of Moghul and Kalh6ro[text-layer?] days" (p. 160, folio 151).
- **Trackers as a profession, and their abuse.** "Sind contained then many expert thieves, and, as a
  sort of natural compensation, · equally expert trackers (pagis or peris) capable of following the
  footprints Of camel, hor�e[text-layer?] or man over· miies[text-layer?] of countryside." (p. 160,
  folio 151); a zamindar "might find themselves at the mercy of a dishonest tracker who alleged that he
  had traced the stolen property to their land" (p. 166, folio 157).
- **Punishment, commutation and the fiscal upside of conviction.** "Mutilation by chopping off
  th�[text-layer?] left hand or cutting off ears and nose was almost the · extrem�[text-layer?] form of
  punishment. The government w·as, however� usually ready to comm1:Jte[text-layer?] these barbarous
  penalties for a suitably heavy fine." (p. 160, folio 151) From James: prosecution "devolved
  principally upon the injured party"; a produced thief "was tobbed[text-layer?] of the whole of his
  property, arid the surplus … was carried to the credit" of the government (p. 160, folio 151).
- **Trial by ordeal, named in Sindhi.** "' The t�ial[text-layer?] by water (tubi) was as follows. The
  accused was placed �nder[text-layer?] �ater[text-layer?] whilst a man shot an ar~ow[text-layer?] from
  a bow as far as he could… The tfial[text-layer?] by ·fire (charr) was equally difficult. A trench was
  dug seven cupits[text-layer?] in length and filled with firewo:Jd[text-layer?] which was lighted and
  the accused, with his legs and feet bound, had to go from one end to the other.'" (p. 161, folio 152)
  **"cupits" is almost certainly *cubits*, carried unrepaired.**
- **Burnes's summary judgement, with its qualification intact.** "' As the state religion of Scinde is
  taken from the E:oran[text-layer?], so the' system of jurisprudence is derived from the same source
  and **when exercised between two subjects of the more favoured creed** would appear to be pretty
  fairly administered by the kazi. . . . The administration of justice costs little or nothing to the
  state. . . . The Hindus for the most part settle their differences among themselves by panchayats…
  The Baliichis seem generally to take the law into their own hands and to act on the simple principle
  of retaliation. '" (p. 162, folio 153) **The qualification is Burnes's own and must not be dropped.**
- **The limits of the state against a privileged tribe, in one case.** Del Hoste at Hyderabad: two
  camels stolen and tracked "to the tents of Baliichis some distance away… despite clear proof of the
  theft and the guilt of the thieves, the Mir said he was powerless to get them restored but would
  replace the camels himself by two others altogether." (p. 170, folio 161)

### Hindus, Baluchis and the distribution of the fiscal burden

- **The three-class division of the population, from Pottinger.** "the first was the privileged class
  consisting of Amirs, Chiefs, Baliichis, Sayids and religious mendicants, the second, of the pastoral
  agricultural and other Mussulman tribes found all over Sind in the rural areas and constituting the
  majority of the population, and the third, of the Hindus who were ' tyrannized over in . the most
  infamous and open manner by all classes of Mussul­mans '" (p. 170, folio 161; footnote 2: "For the
  maltreatment of Hindus see Pottinger, Postans, Burnes and Burton passim.")
- **Sorley's summary of the transfer.** "The plain facts were that the unprivileged classes had to
  support, in the manner already described, by their toil and industry the privileged so that the
  latter could lead lives of display, extrava­gance and indolence." (p. 170, folio 161)
- **What changed from Moghul to Kalhora, and then to Talpur.** "Under the Kalhora … it became no longer
  necessary to support an alien Moghul bureaucracy. The position of privilege formerly occupied by the
  Moghu1[text-layer?] aristocracy was takeri[text-layer?] by the rulers themselves, the jaghirdars and
  leading zamindars." (pp. 170–171, folios 161–162) "The Hindus' position deteriorated still further.
  They came to be regarde�[text-layer?] as a sort of gold mine to be drawn upon at will . All the
  wandering immigrants, mostly Hindus from Gujarat, Jaisalmir and Marwar, who entered Sind, became
  thereby liable to the taxes that fell upon the indigenous Muslim population." (p. 171, folio 162)
- **Why the system held.** "as Postans has remarked, the common people were reconciled to it and had no
  means of . resisting. The Hindus regarded the numerous demands upon them as .a sort of capricious
  income tax and doubtless they managed tc»[text-layer?] pass some of them on to the consumer, as
  without the Hindus as traders and accountants the commerce of the country would have stood still and
  the revenue system would have been unworkable." (p. 171, folio 162)
- **The Hindu commercial role, itemised.** "Hindus continued to live in Sind advancing money to the
  zamind.ar[text-layer?] class, financing trade and commerce, farming the revenue, and owning all the
  petty shops in the villages. There is no doubt that m�ny[text-layer?] of them became rich, though
  they had to adopt all kinds of subterfuges to conceal their wealth." (p. 171, folio 162) And the
  occupational split as it stood at the annexation: "by the time of the Lodi Afghans in the sixteenth
  century the agricul­tural[text-layer?] population· and the · land-holding class were almost entirely
  Muslim, the Hindu popµl�tion[text-layer?] being confined to tl�e[text-layer?] · business of writing
  and to trade and commerce. This was largely the state of affairs that persiste<;l[text-layer?] down
  to the British annexationin r�4J[text-layer?]." (p. 248, folio 239) **The date itself is destroyed in
  the text layer.** Sorley adds, on the Talpurs, that they employed "Hindus for everything that the
  Muslims - could not do so well" while treating them "with a strange mixture of apathy, cruelty and
  contempt" (p. 152, folio 143) — **directly relevant to the archive's Hindu and Nanakpanthi rows in
  Sind.**
- **Baluch tax immunity as the other structural exemption.** "The Balflchis[text-layer?] were by this
  time becoming too powerful to tax properly and claimed immunity from the taxation that pressed . on
  the indigenous Muslim population. This immunity they became more and more successful in obtaining.
  Thus the system was in essence less just than that of the Moghuls." (p. 171, folio 162); "in Karachi
  several Baliich tribes were exempt from taxation." (p. 170, folio 161) Place-level: **bhagnari-mandir**, **darya-lal-mandir-darya-lal-sankat-mochan-mandir**, **jhollay-lal-mandir**, **shree-ratneshwar-mahadev-temple-karachi**, **shri-laxmi-narayan-mandir-native-jetty-bridge**, **shri-panchmukhi-hanuman-mandir-karachi**, **shri-swaminarayan-mandir-karachi**, **shri-varun-dev-mandir**, **shrine-of-abdullah-shah-ghazi**, **shrine-of-pir-mangho**, **shrine-of-qalandar-baba-auliya** (the book's "Karachi").
- **Tribes that never submitted, named by a British officer.** "' During the period when the sacred
  tribe of KalhOra[text-layer?] brought under their control nearly all districts, Gujerat on the
  eastern bank of the Indus, the Karmatis and Jokhias towards the sea, the Niimrias[text-layer?]
  between the villages of Shaul and Jinger and the Chandias along the hills above Sehwan still
  maintained their inde­pendence or became tributaries of Khurasan and were never even by the Talpiirs
  more than partially subdued.'" (p. 169, folio 160, footnote 3) Place-level: **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**, **shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar** (the book's "Sehwan").
- **A 1843 proclamation that puts the three communities under one law.** Napier: "' Indians, Balooches,
  Afghans, Hindoes… Men shall find safety and protection in Scinde. The merchants and the travellers
  shall pass along the road with safety ; death be to the robber and the `murderers .I`[text-layer?]
  **Such is the law of God who rules alike over the Mahometan, the Hindoo and the Christian. His will
  be done !** '" (p. 120, folio 111, footnote 1, "Proclamation by Sir Charles Napier, `2 1 October
  1843`[text-layer?]")
- **Cross-communal religious practice, recorded three times and never explained.** "How are we to
  explain the extraordinary phenomen.on[text-layer?] of Hindus visiting Mussulman shrines and making
  votive offerings at them…?" (p. 175, folio 166); the Sehwan gathering "of both Mussulmans and Hindus
  every year in a strange mixture of worship" (p. 257, folio 248); the Kira-tal tank on Makali hill,
  "men and women of all classes, Hindus and Mussalmans" (p. 146, folio 137); and the Khwajo Khizr cult
  on the Indus (p. 178, folio 169). Place-level for the Sehwan gathering: **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**, **shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar** (the book's "Sehwan").

### Sorley's account of Sindhi religion: conversion, "debasement" and the quasi-theocratic state

- **The problem the chapter is built to solve, and his framing device.** "how is it possible to
  reconcile the poet's lofty etherealized religion with the actual practice of the common folk ?"
  (p. 172), framed through Dean Inge: "' A religion as believed and practised cannot be far in advance
  of the mental and moral capacity of its adherents. A religion succeeds not because it is true but
  because it suits its worshippers. '" (p. 172)
- **His verdict on Sindhi Islam, and his word for it.** "In seventeenth and eighteenth century Sind a
  great gulf yawned between Islam's ideal and the practical working of Islamic . belief." (p. 172);
  "As regards the accretion of superstitious practice upon the plain austerity of Islam in Sind the
  evidence is overwhelming." (p. 176, folio 167); "**The debasement of Islam** found in these and
  similar practices seems undoubtedly to have been due to the popular religion of the indigenous
  Sindhis, sometimes called Jats." (p. 178, folio 169) **The same evaluative register governs his
  account of Sufism: "Sufism is merely one of the forms in which mysticism has *altered* the simple
  religion of the Prophet" (p. 284, folio 275).**
- **The causal thesis: late mass conversion left the Hindu substrate intact.** "while the earlier
  leading families of Sind were influenced by Shiaideas[text-layer?]…, when the mass Sunni conversions
  occurred they did so amongst a population deeply wedded to Hindu practices and Hindu superstitions.
  The result was twofold ; first to break down very considerably the clear-cut distinction between
  Sunni and Shia…: and second, to make it inevitable that Islam, as far as the common people were
  concerned, would be to a great extent influenced by Hindu pre­dilections." (p. 175, folio 166)
  **This is an argument about why Sindhi shrine religion looks as it does.** The three results are
  named: "an almost incredible veneration for Sayids as such, a great accretion of superstitious
  practice in the belief of the multitude, and a common basis of under­standing, by Muslim and Hindu
  alike, of the mystical message of Islam as expounded in the teachings of the Sindhi Muslim mystics."
  (p. 175, folio 166)
- **The chronology of conversion, given twice and not the same twice.** "Most of the population was
  converted to Islam in the fourteenth and fifteenth centuries, though the ruling autocracy of Sumra
  appears to have been converted earlier." (p. 174, folio 165) Against: "In fact Sind was converted to
  Islam not by the Arabs but by the central Asian peoples -who[text-layer?] took up the tale of Muslim
  penetrati.on[text-layer?] of India **from the twelfth century onwards**" (p. 248, folio 239), ending
  "by the time of the Lodi Afghans in the sixteenth century" (p. 248, folio 239). **Kept side by
  side.** The Arab period left nothing: "Yet tl;le[text-layer?] Arabs left no permanent impress upon
  the land. The reasori[text-layer?] is that they were merely a military garrison living amongst
  strangers with whom they did not mingle." (p. 247, folio 238) The two Arab-period taxes are named
  twice from the same source, "the land tax (kharaj) and the capitation tax (jizya)" (p. 174,
  folio 165; p. 248, folio 239).
- **The catalogue of popular institutions Sorley takes from Crooke and says all exist in Sind.** "There
  were miracle-working tombs. There was the canonization of reputed holy men. There was the personal
  worship of pirs. There was the institution of caste saints. There was the working of miracles by holy
  men. There- were nine-yard tombs. There was the strange cult of Khwajo Khizr. There was the efficacy
  of holy relics for the cure of disease. Examples of all could be cited from Sind." (p. 177,
  folio 168) **Eight named categories, including "nine-yard tombs" and "the institution of caste
  saints", both otherwise unexplained** — **(not stated)**. And talismans as a mass-market religious
  good: "Demonology and magic were rife and there was the universal use of talismans, **especially by
  the peasant class**, as a protection against the evil eye and all kinds of human ills." (p. 177,
  folio 168)
- **The limits Sorley sets on his own argument.** "Whether the exaggerated respect for the pir is due
  to the remains of Hindu influences of hero-worship cannot be predicated ; but it is possible that
  some such influence has been at work in Sind." (p. 175, folio 166); "the Siifi[text-layer?] strain of
  thought which permeates the whole of the verse has in Sind always had an appealt o[text-layer?]
  Hindus, though to what extent this appeal is due to its being '. influenced by·[text-layer?] Hindu
  religious iqeas[text-layer?] is a subject of the greatest- obscurity." (p. 247, folio 238); and
  "Whether, as Macdonald thinks, the main influence comes from the religion of Muhammad himself, or
  whether it has been wrought to final perfection by the peculiar bent of the Persian mind … matters
  not." (p. 297, folio 288) **Three refusals to adjudicate the question he keeps raising.**
- **Sectarian composition, and the paradox he states as one.** "The majority of the population were
  followers of the Sunnat. But very influential sections held Shia tenets, though none of them embraced
  the thorough-going heterodoxy of the Persians." (p. 172) Against which the religious leaders "were
  nearly all Shias by persuasion" (p. 173, folio 164), and "Though the religious leaders belonged
  largely to a school of religious thought at variance with the ideas of the greater part of the
  population, they did not thereby lose their position of predominance." (p. 173, folio 164) Earlier
  heterodoxy is also recorded: "there had been more than a trace of Qarmatian doctrine" among the
  governing oligarchy, affecting native rulers "from the eleventh century onwards" (p. 302, folio 293)
  — against p. 248 (folio 239), where the Qarmatian heresies are placed at the end of the tenth
  century. **Both kept.**
- **His thesis about the origin of religious intolerance, offered in the first person as his own
  reading.** "Baliichis as a class are not at all intolerant… It seems that the intolerance which
  ultimately `developed�`[text-layer?] was due to the influence of the Kalh6ra who, **as fanatical
  Muslims**, were able to draw upon the prejudices of an ignorant Muhammadan population only too
  inclined to take a superficial and external view of the meaning of their religion. **At least this is
  my reading of the conflicting `eyidence`[text-layer?], to which I have given a great deal of
  concentrated study.**" (p. 152, folio 143) **He labels it his own; "as fanatical Muslims" is his
  phrase.** He states the difficulty the thesis must overcome: "It is hard `fo`[text-layer?] reconcile
  the accounts of the happy camaraderie of all classes that prevailed in Tatta during its prosperous
  days with the distressing stories of religious bigotry common in the records of English observers in
  the nineteenth century." (pp. 152–153, folios 143–144) And he makes the Talpur role inherited:
  "The Talpiirs finding the tendencies towards bigotry well established took no trouble to correct
  them… They followed, therefore, the easy policy of letting sleeping dogs lie." (p. 153, folio 144)
- **The three-regime moral ranking, stated flatly.** "The Moghuls were autocratic but in essence they
  tried to be just . The Kalhora - were tyrannical and capricious… The Talpiirs had no reason to fear
  the Baliichis but were bent upon keeping Sind as a close preserve of Muslim power, with the result
  that under them non-Baliichis and Hindus alike were treated with less consideration than they had
  been under Moghul or Kalh6ro." (p. 152, folio 143)
- **The two-tier model of Sufism, which governs everything he says about shrines.** "THE practice of
  Sufism by the common people in Sind was a very different thing from the refined system . of the poets
  and theologians." (p. 298, folio 289); and at the chapter's end, "The real message of tasawwuf is
  with the intellectuals, not with the professional performers of routine." (p. 303, folio 294)
  **And the defence of tasawwuf against his own evidence, in two consecutive sentences:** "Nothing is
  more certain than that the criticisms of this system passed by Burnes and Burton are more than
  deserved." / "Nor would it be fair to judge tasawwuf in Sind by the standards of the lowest
  pretenders to it . **It is not fair to value Siifism[text-layer?] by its aberrations.**" (p. 300,
  folio 291) **He concedes the criticism in full and then rules it inadmissible; record both halves.**
- **Sufism as a literary import rather than an institutional transmission.** "Another link in the
  strength of this Persian literary dominance of Upper India was the Sufi philosophy which, expressed
  most felicitously in poetry by the great Persian masters, found in India and in Sind particularly a
  very congenial home." (p. 252, folio 243) **No order, no khanqah and no chain of transmission is
  named there** — **(not stated)**. Likewise: "The extent to which Sind attracted contemplative and
  religious-minded persons during the middle age of Islam is well known and is the foundation of its
  school of Sufi thought." (p. 173, folio 164) — **again no order, no silsila and no khanqah**
  — **(not stated)**.
- **The eight doctrines of the Sufis, which Sorley makes the book's key to the Risalo.** He summarises
  al-Kalabadhi under eight heads — the characteristics of God; the Gnosis of God; Spirit
  (`riih`[text-layer?]/`nafs`/`{rih)`[text-layer?]); Union; Love; Separation; Revelation and Veiling;
  and the Seeker and the Sought, "' for the man who seeks God only seeks Him because God first sought
  him '" (pp. 289–290, folios 280–281) — and then says of them, "**All these ideas will be found in the
  poetry of Shah Abdul Latif**" (p. 290, folio 281) — **bhit-bhit-shah**. The two worlds of the soul
  are named beside them, "the alami itlak" and "the alami takayyud or binding world" (p. 292,
  folio 283).
- **And the admission that the history cannot be written.** "The history of Islam in Sind is
  extra­ordinarily complicated. It has not yet been adequately described in any book." (p. 172); "The
  facts are, however, very obscure." (p. 174, folio 165); "**It is not possible to trace in any detail
  the progress of tasawwuf in Sind.**" (p. 298, folio 289) — **after which he traces it for five pages
  on Burton, Elliot, Jethmal Parsram and the Tarikh-i-Tahiri, all four of which he disparages.** "There
  is no reliable account of the progress of the movement in Simi[text-layer?]." "But this kind of
  information is useless." "But the rest of them must be taken on trust… we have very little
  information about them." "there is very little that can be. said to be securely established in the .
  way of verified historical fact." (pp. 298–299, folios 289–290) **Every Sindhi Sufi fact taken from
  this book inherits those four disqualifications.**

### Sorley's sources and how he weighs them

- **The five classes, and the verdict on the first.** Sind chronicles; general Moghul histories; East
  India Company records "(a) 1635-62, (b) 1758-75"; European travellers; and the papers of the British
  occupation (p. 24, printed 15). "The native historians dealing especially with Sind are of little
  help for the times of Shah Abdul Latif" (p. 24, printed 15); "The native historians and annalists
  taken as a whole must `th�refore`[text-layer?] be classed as thoroughly inadequate and
  unsatisfactory" (p. 27, printed 18). **The reason is a class argument, made through Elliot:** "of
  domestic history we have in our Indian annalists absolutely nothing… A fact, an anecdote, a speech, a
  remark which will illustrate the condition of the common people… is considered too insignificant to
  be suffered to intrude upon a relation which concerns only grandees and ministers, thrones and
  imperial powers." (p. 26, printed 17); Sorley adds in his own voice, "Most of the native annalists
  wrote as Court flatterers… They did not speculate on or describe the economic structure of the feudal
  society in which they lived and which they did not in fact understand." (p. 26, printed 17) **And the
  defect is not peculiar to India** — the same neglect is found in eighteenth-century England (p. 27,
  printed 18).
- **The travellers, graded one by one.** "Not one of the outstanding commentators of the Moghul Empire
  visited Sind… Tavernier, Bernier, Thevenot are alike of little value for social conditions in Sind";
  Withington, Manrique and Manucci are by contrast "persons of unusual intelligence, and reliable
