  observers of events and places that were within their personal knowledge" (p. 43). Manrique "is
  generally `unsympathetic�`[text-layer?] in particular, towards Islam while regarding the practices of
  Hinduism as the delusions of a poor misguided portion of humanity. He is, however, an acute and
  accurate observer but is no stylist and he does not hesitate to borrow without
  `acki:iowledgement`[text-layer?] from the works of other observers." (p. 81, folio 72); his judgement
  of Tatta was "largely coloured by his religious prejudices" (p. 85, folio 76). Manucci "is a vivid
  story-teller and probably something of a romancer as well" (p. 86, folio 77); Hamilton "appears to
  have been uncommonly well educated" for "a seaman of those days" (p. 89, folio 80), and where sources
  conflict, "Hamilton as the . eye witness is entitled to greater credence" over Thévenot (p. 91,
  folio 82). **And he judges the Dutch archive not worth consulting at all** (p. 44, footnote).
- **The *Ain-i-Akbari* dismissed first-hand, and then used anyway.** "I have studied the statistics for
  Sind given in·- the Ain and can find very little of value in them. They offer no reliable guide to the
  social condition of the time, and they are maddeningly vague just where the historian desires them to
  be precise." (p. 97, folio 88) **He then quotes it for the 40,000 boats with "{probably a gross
  exaggeration)" attached (p. 97, folio 88, footnote), for the Lower Sind fish staple without
  qualification (p. 109, folio 100), for the Sind revenue divisions (p. 150, folio not printed,
  footnote 1) and for the crop share (p. 154, folio 145) — and his own index records the verdict,
  "(statistics for Sind unhelpful) 88 and nn" (p. 432). An internal inconsistency worth flagging.**
- **Burton refused on one subject and relied on for another, without comment.** "The view of Burton
  that Sind came into British `hanas`[text-layer?] in an almost exhausted state … is one that cannot be
  accepted without considerable qualification." (p. 143, folio 134) **Yet Burton is his chief authority
  for intoxicants four pages later (p. 147, folio 138), for the madressa curriculum and the teacher's
  fees (pp. 220–225, folios 211–216), for the Jelali kit list (pp. 299–300, folios 290–291), for the
  price of a pir (pp. 299–300, folios 290–291) and for the whole schedule of imposts (pp. 157–158,
  folios 148–149).** On his temperament: "It was futile to expect a man of Burton's temperament to be
  anything but critical and supercilious of what he saw in Sind. **He despised Sind for what he
  considered its lax Muhammadanism.**" (p. 120, folio 111) **This is the only religious
  characterisation of Sind in that chunk, it is reported as Burton's, and Sorley does not say whether
  he agrees.** The index runs the same rehabilitation as a structure: Burton "(unjust to
  Talpiirs[text-layer?]) 22. 94 ; (unduly critical of Sind) I I I[text-layer?], I34·[text-layer?]
  2 1 0[text-layer?]" (p. 433), Postans "(unduly critical) 94" (p. 438), "Victorian point of view, 21,
  31, 1 1 1[text-layer?]" (p. 441).
- **The reformers grouped and dismissed as a school.** "Pottinger, Del Hoste, . Postans were all of the
  same school of thought. They were eager Western reformers anxious to change anything they
  `con­sidered`[text-layer?] bad by the standards of an England charged with a civilizing mission. It
  is not fair to gauge an Oriental land by such ethical measuring rods. Only a few sagacious
  fair-minded observers like Heddle and Burnes offered a slightly protesting voice against the chorus
  of disapproval." (p. 120, folio 111) **And the methodological warning that follows, which applies
  directly to how the archive should weigh colonial-era shrine reporting:** "It was this aspect of the
  `Tfilpiir`[text-layer?] rule which so unfavourably impressed English observers in the nineteenth
  century and led them to draw entirely erroneous conclusions about the political conditions that had
  prevailed in Sind in previous centuries." (p. 151, folio 142)
- **A plagiarism charge against one of his own sources.** "Postans in his Personal
  ObseYvations[text-layer?] on Sitidh[text-layer?] has incorporated (without clear acknowledgement)
  large portions of Mr. Callendar's Narrative." (p. 191, folio 182) — repeated at p. 35 (printed 26),
  "Postans writing in 1843 took over, almost verbatim and without acknowledgement, much of what Crowe
  had written". **Anything the archive takes from Postans may in fact be Callendar, Crowe or Carless.**
  And Postans' nineteenth-century trade lists are explicitly back-projected: "In the main, however,
  Postans' lists are accurate enough for the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries if the cardinal fact
  of the decline in the importance of the cotton industry … is borne in mind." (p. 100, folio 91)
- **His method with oral tradition and hagiography.** "Sayid-worship being however what it is, many of
  the items in the oral tradition must be treated with great caution. Many of them of the miraculous
  order must be ruled out altogether. They are merely part of the halo of a growing sainthood. Miracles
  of this kind are like· fairies. They cease to exist when people no longer believe in them." (p. 179,
  folio 170) On Mirza Kalich Beg: "Many of the facts collected by Mirza Kalich Beg are prefaced by some
  such phrase as ' Men say that ' or ' They write that.' We must, therefore, employ the tests of
  evidence to compute their worth." (p. 179, folio 170) **And the reframing that the archive can
  reuse:** "To the scholar these childish stories are interesting not for the alleged facts they assert
  but as showing the working of popular psychology in the c:r;eation[text-layer?] of an oral
  tradition." (p. 184, folio 175)
- **Five explicit disclaimers about his own source base, all in sixteen pages.** "But the verifiable
  history of these people is· .obscure[text-layer?] and the authorities are all unsatisfactory."
  (p. 247, folio 238) "Whatever the real facts be, it is certain that…" (p. 247, folio 238) "Actually
  the topography of the story is most confused." (p. 257, folio 248) "Whatever the real facts may be…"
  (p. 257, folio 248) "Beyond that it is perhaps not safe to generalize." (p. 254, folio 245)
- **A methodological move he makes openly against himself.** "But I do not know on what
  authoritv[text-layer?] he holds that Shah Abdul Latif went so far." (p. 182, folio 173, footnote 1) —
  **his own disclaimer against Gurbuxani, and the reason the Hinglaj and Lahut mentions in this book
  must not be treated as its assertions.** And on place-names generally: "The qmfusion[text-layer?]
  that has resulted between Sind and Baliichi[text-layer?] place-names must be the result of local
  influences and the operation of **popular philology**." (p. 257, folio 248) — **a warning the archive
  can reuse for shrine toponyms**, and one the index repeats in its own voice with reciprocal
  question-marked entries, "Bhambhbr[text-layer?] (? =Bhuimpiir[text-layer?]) 248" and
  "Bhuimpiir[text-layer?] (? = BhambhOr[text-layer?]) 248" (p. 433), plus the warning that
  "Sind�[text-layer?] (Sindee, Scinda, Scindy) apparently=Tatta , 37, 39, 56, 57, 63, 102 ;
  (occasionally = Sind) ; not= Tatta, 90" (p. 440).
- **What the bibliography offers the archive as leads.** Three direct ones in one sub-section:
  "Bilimoria, N. N. Bibliography of Publications on Sind and Baluchistan. 1930."; "Jethmal Parsram.
  Saints of India and Sind. 1926." (with his "Sind and its Si1fis[text-layer?]" (1924) in sub-section
  (d)); and "Tahilram Assudomal. Biography of Saint Udero Lal. 1926." (p. 192, folio 183). Two more
  from the Islam section: Macdonald's "' Emotional Religion in Islam as affected by Music and Singing.
  ' J.R.A .S., l90I[text-layer?]" — **the only item in the whole bibliography about devotional
  music** — and "Herklots' Islam in India" in Crooke's 1921 edition (pp. 192–193, folios 183–184).
  **And the fact that Sorley assembled an eight-item music bibliography at all** (p. 195, folio 186)
  **is the strongest indication in the apparatus that he treats the Risalo as sung material — though
  he names no sur, raga or singer there** — **(not stated)**.
- **The book's own footnote habits, which affect every citation taken from it.** "op. cit." is used
  with the antecedent given only in the body text (p. 299, folio 290, for Jethmal Parsram; p. 298,
  folio 289, for Elliot); one source is cited under two different titles for the same book and page —
  "The Masnawi. Translated by Whinfield, p. 3 1[text-layer?]" (p. 294, folio 285) against "The
  Spiritual Couplets of Ma�lana[text-layer?] ]aliiluddin[text-layer?] Rumi. Translated by Whinfield,
  p. 3 1[text-layer?]" (p. 253, folio 244) — **two titles, one source; do not merge them silently.**
  Several footnote markers print as `r` for `1`, which is this book's commonest fault.

### Sorley on the poetry, and on its reliability as history

- **Three criteria of poetical value, set out before the Risalo is reached, and applied to it.** "There
  is, first, an examination of external form, words, language, rhythm, subject matter and musical
  composition. There is, second, an intellectual judgement as to the meaning and significance of the
  thought expressed in this external form. There is, third, an emotional attitude called up by the
  aesthetic effect of the poetry on the mind. The first is the exoteric point of view. The second and
  third together constitute the esoteric point of view." (p. 209, folio 200) **Note that Sorley applies
  "exoteric" and "esoteric" — words with a precise Sufi sense — to literary criticism, not to
  mysticism, on the first page of a book about a Sufi poet** (p. 208, folio 199). **"Musical
  composition" is listed among the elements of external form.**
- **He denies that poetic meaning is mysterious, in a book whose subject is a mystic.** "There is
  nothing whatever mysterious or difficult about poetic meaning… In poetry the method of expression is
  definitely calculated, by means of tricks allied to the tricks of music, to play upon the emotions of
  **the listener**." (p. 210, folio 201) **"The listener", not "the reader", throughout that passage —
  Sorley's implied audience for poetry is an audience that hears it.**
- **The literary verdict on the poet, and its terms.** "He is incompar­ably the greatest man whom Sind
  has yet produced in the realm of imaginative art." (p. 178, folio 169); "He has written poetry that
  deserves a wider public than it has yet attracted." (p. 178, folio 169); and his criterion for
  originality: "It it[text-layer?] is the function of a poet not merely to express felicitously the
  ideas of his time but also to use his own language musically in order to bring out ·great truths,
  then Shah Abdul Latif has established his claim to be an original poet of his own right and not a
  soulless copyist" (p. 217, folio 208) — **bhit-bhit-shah**. **Against which his placement of the poet
  elsewhere is much lower: the Risalo "shares to a considerable extent in this lapse from artistic
  perfection" (p. 259, folio 250), "The Risalo for all its virtues is not free from this criticism"
  (p. 260, folio 251), and his general verdict on the genre is that "Oriental poetry as a whole is
  always something of a hotchpotch . . Gems of beauty and wisdom are inextricably j umbled with
  passages of utter bathos and banality" (p. 259, folio 250). Both must travel with any citation of his
  praise.**
- **The defence of the poet against the religion around him — the argumentative purpose the shrine
  chapter serves.** "The real form and meaning of Islam in Sind is not to be found in these ebullitions
  of popular and incredible fanaticism, but is to be read in the beautiful spiritual poems of Shah
  Abdul Latif. It will be most unfair to judge Sind by the former and forget the latter." (p. 169,
  folio 160) — **bhit-bhit-shah**. **An evaluative separation of the poet from the shrine culture the
  book has just spent four pages condemning.**
- **The argument that the Risalo's textual corruption is structural, not incidental.** The poems were
  never written down as composed; they were compiled by the unqualified; they contain asides and other
  poets' verses; the manuscripts are "very discrepant"; therefore the received text "are instead the
  verses as subse­quently written down by others, altered by them, and edited by them" (p. 233,
  folio 224). **A four-step argument and the strongest statement in the book against treating the
  Risalo as the poet's own words** — **bhit-bhit-shah**. He adds the colonial-benefit counterfactual
  without hedging: "without . the stimulus of English education and · the interest in literature
  produced by it the poems of Shah Abdul Latif would still have been today in much the same condition
  as that in which the 'J3riti?h[text-layer?] found them in 1843." (p. 234, folio 225) And on the
  Muntakhab's order: "The arrangement of the poems in the Muntakhab is very unsatis­factory… This
  haphazard · arrangement is not of course without its merit. lt[text-layer?] makes clear, for.
  instance, with considerable fidelity · the · utterly unstudied way in which the poetry itself was
  born . . But it is not useful for a scholarly appraisement" (p. 235, folio 226) — **he concedes that
  the musical arrangement is evidence of how the poetry was made, and discards it anyway.**
- **He declares the biography permanently unfinishable.** "There is no really satisfactory account of
  Shah Abdul Latif's life. Nor will there ever be." (p. 179, folio 170) — **bhit-bhit-shah**. And
  against the untaught-genius tradition: "it is a typical conceit of hero worship to pretend that all
  knowledge came to him as a sort of special revelation"; "The mystical form of his poetry could never
  have been achieved without a deep and sympathetic understanding of the mystical development of Islam
  that came to India through the work of the great Persian poets." (p. 183, folio 174)
- **The Risalo's limits as a social-history source, stated by Sorley himself.** "We shall, for instance,
  find in it nothing like the descriptive passages of the Odyssey which enable us at this late day to
  picture for our­selves the houses and house furnishings…, the kind of harness the horses wore, the
  manner in which the harvest was .cut[text-layer?] and gathered in, the tackle of the
  boats\"[text-layer?], or the character of the games in whicp[text-layer?] the common people indulged.
  Shah Abdul Latif, like most Oriental poets, is not concerned with these things in themselves"
  (p. 265, folio 256); "His place names might be any place names" (p. 266, folio 257); and the material
  he does extract he calls "obiter dicta" from which "scholars must now obtain their indirect
  knowledge" (p. 266, folio 257). **But he then affirms that the picture is true, and dates the claim
  to his own present:** "No one familiar · with Sind will dispute how true a portrayal this is of - a
  simple rustic civilization of husbandmen and cattle herds, and how typical it all is *still* of the
  predominant features of this agricultural countryside" (pp. 267–268, folios 258–259). **The two sit
  three pages apart and the book does not reconcile them.**
- **His dating method for the folk stories: absence of Islam taken as evidence of pre-Islamic date.**
  Of Momul and Rano, "It manifestly relates to a period when this portion of Sind had not been
  converted to Islam"; of Umar and Marui, "There is nothing in it which suggests any connexion with
  Islam"; of Lilan and Chanesar, "the story as it is found in the Risalo gives no indication of anything
  Muslim. We are justified in holding that the tale goes back to a time before this part of the country
  was converted to Islam" (p. 270, folio 261). **The inference is from silence throughout, and "We are
  justified in holding" is the strongest form he uses.** The blanket claim, with its own softening
  between two sentences: "The stories are all Hindu in origin. It is plain that *most* of them relate
  to the Sind of pre-Islam days" (p. 269, folio 260), summed at p. 271 (folio 262) as "one of.
  therii[text-layer?] frorn[text-layer?] t4e[text-layer?] Punj a.9[text-layer?] , two o f them from
  Cutch or Kathiawar, and the rest are .stoiies[text-layer?] of Lower -Sind".
- **The performed poetry is still performed, and he says so without naming a tune.** "the names of the
  tunes to which the poems were and still are sung" (p. 246, folio 237) — **no tune is named**
  — **(not stated)**.
- **The comparative frame, declared as a method rather than a flourish.** "Mysticism is essentially
  similar wherever it arises. The mystics are engaged in ploughing and reploughing the same small plot
  of land. Even if the ploughmen are differently garbed and handle their ploughs with a variety of
  techniques we do not see that these circumstances matter very much to the character of the furrows
  they carve from the selfsame soil" (p. 283, folio 274). **This is the licence under which Sorley
  reads a Sindhi Muslim poet through St Bernard, Crashaw and Isaac Watts, and it is a stated method.**
  The resulting placement: "The nearest English parallels to Shah Abdul Latif in his love religious
  mood are Crashaw and Isaac Watts." (p. 240, folio 231) — **bhit-bhit-shah** — with the differentiator,
  "Love is a means to an end and not an end in itself" (p. 240, folio 231), and the composite verdict at
  p. 241 (folio 232). **His epigraph page acts on it: Quarles, Herbert, Crashaw and Watts set beside one
  Persian quatrain from Abu Sayid Ibn Abu'l Khair, with no comment on the pairing** (p. 318).
- **The translator's dilemma, and the terms on which every Sorley verse quotation should be read.**
  "The traµslator[text-layer?] is always faced with a dilemma. Is he to reproduce the words or the
  spirit of the original ? Is he to be a photographer or an artist ? There is no resolving this
  dilemma." (p. 306, folio 297) His six claims about his own practice: he strove "to catch the spirit of
  his poetry"; kept the words "as close to-[text-layer?] the text as circumstances will allow"; was
  "generally faithful to the original, except where the exigencies of metre make some latitude
  inevitable"; retained the metaphors and similes "even where they may appear strange and unfamiliar to
  English readers"; found it "impossible to reproduce the brevity and succinctness of the Sindhi text";
  and used "a vast variety of metres. Most of these are based on conventional English models." (p. 306,
  folio 297) **And the editorial decision that matters most for citation: the translation abandons the
  sur as the unit of presentation and substitutes English thematic titles — "MERCY AND GRACE" (p. 320,
  folio 311), "THE DAILY ROUND" (p. 342, folio 333), "LOVE" (p. 368, folio 359) — so that no Sindhi sur
  title appears as a heading anywhere in it, and "The reader of the translation alone would not learn
  that the Risalo has surs at all", except from the one word "Surs" in the concordance table head
  (p. 317, folio 308).**
- **The quietist reading of the poet's public life.** "He took no active part in the great movements of
  his time. In this respect he recalls the instances of Kant and Hegel who, living in the midst of
  stirring political events, took no notice of them." (p. 180, folio 171) — **bhit-bhit-shah**.
- **Why the poems are loved in his own day, which is not the reason they were loved then.** "It comes
  partly from the development of a literary taste that is quite modern. It springs partly from a
  sentimental regard for the fine things of the past characteristic of all peoples at certain stages of
  their cultural development" (p. 215, folio 206) — **a dated interpretation of a living shrine's
  reception, and it is the book's own present** — **bhit-bhit-shah**.

## Figures and sites not in the archive

Nothing mapped above is repeated here. These are the figures and sites that ruling (a) and the
person rule did **not** resolve — either because no archive row's `location_short`, `name` or
`principal_figure` matches, or because the identification would have to come from outside the book.
Strong candidates for future rows are marked.

**Shrines, tombs and cult sites with no archive row**

- **Shaikh Patta, "Shaikh of Shaikhs" — shrine at Tatta. STRONGEST CANDIDATE FOR A FUTURE ROW.** A named saint, a named shrine, a named place, a named recurring observance and a dated textual authority: the Tarikh-i-Tahiri is "quite frank about the undesirable nature of many excesses of popular super­stition shown in Tatta in the dancing of darwishes before the shrine of Shaikh Patta, Shaikh of Shaikhs", and "' This custom, · however much opposed to the laws of Islam, ' he says, ' has been transmitted from generation to generation and all attempts of wise teachers and just governors have never succeeded in putting a stop to it. '" (pp. 168–169, folios 159–160, footnote `rElliot : I, p. l 274.`[text-layer?]); "' **Besides the shrine of the Shaikh of Shaikhs, Shaikh Patta**, there are (i.e. in Tatta) some ten or twelve other places where darwishes perform their dance. …'" (p. 298, folio 289, footnote 1) — **the "(i.e. in Tatta)" is Sorley's own interpolation**; indexed "Shaikh Patta (Shaikh o f[text-layer?] Shaikhs) I 6o[text-layer?], 289" (p. 440). No death date, no silsila, no custodian — **(not stated)**.
- **Sayid Muhammad Husain, called Pir Murad, called Murad Buksh Shah — tomb in Tatta. SECOND STRONGEST CANDIDATE.** "the famous Sindhi saint Sayid Muhammad Husain called Pir Murad whose tomb is in Tatta, and to whom is attributed the saying, ' It is better to restore one dead heart to eternal life than life to a thousand dead bodies.'"; the Baluchi poem names him again, "' Let n o[text-layer?] one cherish my Pir, the Husaini, son of light and fosterer of the poor, Murad Buksh Shah who comes down in a light to his disciples'" (both p. 257, folio 248, footnote 2); repeated with a weaker attribution, "Pir Murad who is reputed to have said…" and Sorley's inference that he "would appear to have believed that faith is a more reliable weapon than works" (p. 300, folio 291); indexed "Sayid Muhammad Husain (Pir Murad) 248, 291" (p. 439). **The same index sends his third name-form elsewhere — "Murad Buksh Shah . (See Lal Shahbaz)" (p. 438) — and that collision is unresolved.** No death date, no silsila, no observance at his tomb — **(not stated)**.
- **The Makali hill / Makali mountain, Tatta — a pilgrimage site, a monthly festival and a miracle, and no row.** "' Each month ', the author writes, ' has several ids for them, the first Friday after the new moon they call in the Sindh language ' ' `Mah-pahra JU.ma[text-layer?]` ''. Such a crowd of men and women flood on this day to the Makali mountain that there is scarce room to stand.'" and "' It has become a custom among many classes to consider the similar festival of Mah-pahra `Siimar[text-layer?]`, or the first Monday in each month, a great day for making pilgrimages. The pleasure of visiting each other induces them to go in large parties taking with them abundance of sweet river water and food such as they can afford. **The day is spent in amusements and visits to the shrines.**'" (pp. 145–146, folios 136–137, footnote 1 citing "Elliot : · I, pp. 2 `72-4[text-layer?]`"). Also the darwish miracle: "' These excitable men often work themselves into such a state of holy ecstasy that they cast them­selves[text-layer?] on the rocks of the mountain of Makali : **but by the blessing of their learned doctors and teachers no harm befalls them.** '" (p. 298, folio 289). Indexed "Makali (hill) 136, 137, 289" (p. 437) — **exactly the pages the index also gives for Id, Holi, Diwali and Hindu and Muslim feasts, which makes this the strongest unexploited lead in the book.** **No saint, tomb or shrine at Makali is ever named** — **(not stated)**.
- **Kira-tal, the sweet-water tank on the Makali hill** — a shared Hindu–Muslim water-site observance: "When rain fell on the `Kira-tal[text-layer?]`, a sweet-water tank on the Makali hill, men and women of all classes, Hindus and Mussalmans, crowded there from morning till night, cooked their meals and feasted." (p. 146, folio 137). Unnamed as a festival and undated.
- **Moghulbhin — a mosque endowed with fishing rights.** "At Moghulbhin the fishing rights had to be granted to a mosque and there were many other instances of valuable alienations in favour of the priestly caste." (p. 169, folio 160). **The institution is a mosque, not a shrine, and the endowment is a fishing right, not land; the place is otherwise unidentified.**
- **Lahut / La.hut / Lahrit / Lihut — a village in Baluchistan and a Sufi station. NOT MERGED, and a human ruling is needed.** "In the Sur Kohiari there is a play of meaning on the word ' Lahut ' which in its mystic sense is one of the stages on the path towards fulfilment-non-existence-and in its plain sense is the name of a village believed to have been in the Mekran country near Sind. **The holy mendicants pass on their way to Lahut over the waste of sand.**" (p. 294, folio 285); eight further occurrences in the verse (pp. 357, 361, 362, 363; folios 348, 352, 353, 354), including "The footprints of . the · holy men are in Lahut" and "townsmen of Lahut" twice; indexed "Lahut, - 285" (p. 436); glossed "Lihut[text-layer?]  Name of a village in Baluchistan ; metaphorically one of the �tages[text-layer?] i�[text-layer?] the �iifi's[text-layer?] search for his ideal ; literally means non-existence ." (p. 431, folio 422). **The archive row in range is `shah-noorani-shrine-syed-bilawal-shah-noorani` ("Lahoot Lamakan valley, Khuzdar District, Balochistan"), and it is deliberately NOT taken: the book names no shrine, no tomb, no saint, no Bilawal Shah Noorani, no Khuzdar and no Balochistan valley at Lahut, and identifying "Lahut" with "Lahoot Lamakan" is an identification the book does not make. This is not a bare-toponym question — Lahut carries an observance — so ruling (a) does not decide it either way.** The one place the two words stand together is Gurbuxani's rejected itinerary, "La.hut[text-layer?], Lamakan" (p. 182, folio 173, footnote 1), **where they are printed as two separate items in a list of places, not as the name of a valley or a shrine.**
- **Ulwahet[text-layer?]** — the Sufi station above Lahut. "They who have passed 'Lahut re ached[text-layer?] Ulwahet[text-layer?] : / And not one word from them ·escapes." (p. 362, folio 353); glossed "Ulwahet  One of the stages in the Siifi's[text-layer?] progress." (p. 431, folio 422). No index entry.
- **The unnamed saint's tomb where a prayer is said for a ship.** "A prayer is said at the tomb of t;tie[text-layer?] saint for the saf�ty[text-layer?] of the ship at sea" (p. 267, folio 258). **The saint is not named and the tomb is not located; Sorley reports it as something the Risalo depicts, not as something he observed.**
- **Edward Cooke's tomb at Tatta** — "a Mr Edward Cooke, of whom nothing is known, died in Tatta in 1743, and his tomb is still visible in the ruins of Sind's one-time metropolis" (p. 42, printed 33). **The only tomb described as standing anywhere in chunk 003, and it is a European's.**
- **The density claim for Sindhi village tombs, from a source Sorley calls useless.** Elliot on the author of the Tuhfat-al-Kiram: "' very credulous in recording the miracles of saints so numerous that **there is· scarcely a village in that priest­ridden[text-layer?] country which has not its tombs of holy men** whose lives and powers are here recorded with implicit faith. '" (p. 298, folio 289). Sorley: "But this kind of information is useless." **Both halves must travel together.**
- **The belief that underwrites tomb devotion in Sind, in Sorley's own summary.** "the saints are not dead, but merely sleeping. They are within their tombs. Their tombs have become their houses which they can leave and whence make journeys. They are accessible to prayer and grant "f,;lvours[text-layer?]." (p. 302, folio 293) — reported as a belief that "prevails throughout Islam" and called "the solid foundation" of Sindhi tomb devotion. With it, the eleven powers ascribed to Sind's saints: "causing the birth of children, especially in cases of barrenness and old age ; curing complaints and diseases like madness, deafness, dumbness and blindness ; appearing in person at a distance to protect friends against unknown dangers ; exercising dominion over birds, beasts and fishes ; breaking through chains, fetters, doors and walls ; living without food, drink or sleep ; compellin�[text-layer?] inanimate objects to behave as if possessed of life and volition ; still ing[text-layer?] storms and changing female into male children. There was hardly a power in the long records of hagiology which could not be found credited to some sacred shrine or holy man in Sind." (p. 177, folio 168, footnote 1 citing "Burton, pp. 229-30.") — **none of the eleven is attached to a named saint or shrine.**

**Figures with no archive row**

- **Adam Shah Kalhoro** — the sainted ancestor of the dynasty; canonised by execution at the Governor of Multan's hands (p. 37, printed 28); "1558 Death of Adam Shah Kalhoro." (p. 200, folio 191); the Kalhora were "themselves invested with a halo of sanctity as murshids, in view of their descent from the sainted Adam Shah" (p. 169, folio 160), and Captain Pelly calls them "the sacred tribe of KalhOra" (p. 169, folio 160). **No tomb, place of burial, shrine or silsila** — **(not stated)** — though clubs were hung at his tomb after the Panhwar conquest (p. 37, printed 28).
- **Mian Muhammad Mihidi[text-layer?]** — "During the time of the Samma a fakir called Mian Muhammad `Mihidi`3 came and settled in Sind attaching to himself a number of followers of his ascetic cult" (p. 37, printed 28), footnoted "Described by Goldsmid as `• Shea Sayed of Jampiir '`[text-layer?]".
- **The holy men of Sehrah Khatibah** — keepers of the copper plates bearing the Kalhora genealogy; their fiefs confiscated and their villages destroyed for objecting (p. 34, printed 25, quoting Burton); indexed "Sehrah Khatibah, 25n" (p. 439).
- **Shaikh Musa, ancestor of Abul Fazl, at Rel in Sewistan** — "' In the ninth century by the decrees of heaven he settled in quiet retirement at Rel, a pleasant village of Sewistan…'" (p. 173, folio 164); called "his fifth ancestor" there and "great-great-great-grandfather" at p. 219 (folio 210); made a template for Shah Abdul Latif's own habit (p. 180, folio 171, footnote 1); indexed "Shaikh Miisa[text-layer?], I64[text-layer?], I 7 I[text-layer?], 2 1 0[text-layer?]" (p. 440).
- **Shaikh Khizr, Abul Fazl's ancestor, a pilgrim to the saints of India** — "' In the beginning of the tenth century (i.e. Muhammadan) Shaikh Khizr set out impelled with the desire of visiting the saints of India. '" (pp. 219–220, folios 210–211). **A distinct person from Khwajo Khizr; no year is given** — **(not stated)**.
- **Shaikh Yusuf Sindhi** — one of the holy men visited on that journey, "who had traversed the fields of secular and mystic lore and had acquired many perfections of the religious life" (p. 220, folio 211). **No place, tomb or dates** — **(not stated)**.
- **Halakhandi** — a bare index headword, "Halakhandi, I6o[text-layer?]" (p. 435), at a printed page inside the privileged-classes and mendicants ranges. **No description at all** — **(not stated)** — and an unexploited lead.
- **Nur Muhammad Bhatti / Niir Mahomad**[text-layer?] — the poet's named preceptor (p. 212, folio 203). **Not to be merged with Nur Muhammad Kalhoro.**
- **The learned Kazis of Tatta, as Mirza Kalich Beg lists them** — "Kazi Namat Ullah, Kazi Hamad, Kazi Abiqullah, Kazi Shakikh · Muhammad" (p. 218, folio 209).
- **The Makhdums and Shaikhs of Tatta, as listed** — "Shaikh Sadaruldin, Makhdum Rukhanaldin, Makhdum Miran walad Moulana Yakub, Makhdum Fazalullah, Makhdfim[text-layer?] Feroz,- Makhdfim[text-layer?] Usman walad Makhdum Bahawaldin, Mulla Muhammad · Damagbi[text-layer?], Makhdum Abdul Jamil, Makhdfim[text-layer?] Faizullah, Makhdfim[text-layer?] Hamza Waiz, Moulana Muhammad Tahir, Makhdum Mahmud, Makhdfim[text-layer?] Adam mian Abul Hasan, Makhdflm[text-layer?] Rahamatullah and many others" (p. 218, folio 209). **Several resemble archive figures elsewhere in Pakistan and none is mapped.**
- **The poets and writers of Tatta** — "Muhammad Mukim, Mulla Abdul Rashid, Abdul Kayfim[text-layer?], Mulla Mohabat Ali, Mulla· Salami, Mulla Abdul Hakim, Mulla Yar Muhammad Khadam, Mulla Raza · Hashmi, Mirza Ghulam Ali Momin, Asadullah Tabah and others" (p. 218, folio 209).

(p. 430, folio 421); indexed "Inayat, 1 75[text-layer?], 224" (p. 435). **Two archive rows carry the name — `dargah-roza-sufi-shah-inayat-shaheed` (Sufi Shah Inayat Shaheed, Jhok Sharif, d. 1718) and `shrine-of-shah-inayat-qadiri` (Shah Inayat Qadiri, Mozang Chungi, Lahore, d. c. 1728) — and the book identifies neither, gives no dates, no place, no tomb and no relationship to either.** **NOT MAPPED: the person rule requires that the person BE the row's principal figure, and identifying the book's Inayat with either would have to come from outside the book. A human ruling is needed.**

(pp. 430–431, folios 421–422); indexed at printed p. 224 each (p. 436). **No archive row carries either name.**

He appears again as "Al Hallaj" for the doctrine of `huliil`[text-layer?] (p. 288, folio 279), **with no execution mentioned there.**

- **The Kerbela cast** — the Imams, Hassan, "the Mir Husain" / "Lord Husain", "great Ali", Yazid, and Hur, the only one given a death scene of his own, "With wound sore�stricken[text-layer?], . yielding life, / A martyr Hur became" (p. 351, folio 342); "The Holy Sayid" of the narrative, **unnamed and not the poet** (p. 349, folio 340); "The men of Kiifa[text-layer?]" / "Kufa's host" (p. 351, folio 342). **No tomb, shrine, ʿurs or date is attached to any of them** — **(not stated)**. Glossed at pp. 430–431 (folios 421–422).
- **Munkar, Nakir and Azrad[text-layer?]** — three named beings met on the road (p. 383, folio 374); glossed "Munkar  One of the angels of death." / "Nakir  One of tne[text-layer?] angels of death." (p. 431, folio 422). **"Azrad" is printed thus and must be checked against the PDF.**
- **The poet's circle** — Tamar and Hashim, amanuenses and "favourite disciples and secretaries" (p. 184, folio 175); Bilal, "A great friend of the poet" (p. 184, folio 175); Mirza Moghul Beg, killed by robbers in 1713 and the poet's father-in-law (p. 182, folio 173); Idan of Sukkur, the mason (p. 185, folio 176).
- **The editors and scholars of the Risalo** — Mirza Kalich Beg, H. M. Gurbuxani, Lilaram Watanmal, Dayaram Gidumal, Tarachand Showkiram, Ernest Trumpp, Kazi Ahmad, Mahmud Buksh Wasif, M. M. Gidwani, C. A. Kincaid and Jethmal Parsram; their pages and dates are given in full in the `bhit-bhit-shah` subsection above. **None is a candidate row; listed here only so the second section is complete.** Sorley's objection to Jethmal Parsram is worth keeping: his "understandfog[text-layer?] is � however, tinged with the ideas of the Hindu Vedanta and a · kind of theosophical universalism which is quite unlike the Islamic mystiCism[text-layer?] ofthe[text-layer?] ·Risalo itself" (p. 268, folio 259).
- **The named professional performer communities, none of them attached to a shrine** — the **Manghanhars and Bhats**, "who supplied. the common people with their music· and their rough verse for ' weddings arid[text-layer?] festlvals[text-layer?] and similar �eremonies[text-layer?]" (p. 246, folio 237); the **wandering Hindu bards**, whose "rhymes and jingles" the Risalo is said to have displaced (p. 246, folio 237); and the **Doms or Dombs**, Baluchi professional minstrels who "sing the songs in the gatherings of the clans" and "visited the villages of Sind wherever there were Baliichis[text-layer?] to listen to them", speaking "amongst themselves a dialect of Sindhi or Western Punjabi" (pp. 255–258, folios 246–249). **No payment, endowment or shrine attachment is stated for any of them** — **(not stated)**.
- **The four religious classes of Sind, as the book divides them** — "the Sayids, the Kureishis or Siddikis, the Alawis and the Abba.sis[text-layer?]. Of these the Sayids were incomparably the most influential"; "The Sayid families were either Hassani or Husaini. Most of them were named from their place of origin, like Bukhara or Shiraz, or from the places in which they eventually settled in Sind. **They were nearly all Shias by persuasion**" (both p. 173, folio 164); "Most of the Sayids were of Shia persuasion" (p. 302, folio 293); "Kureishis, 164" (p. 436).

**Toponyms with no matching archive row**

- **Tatta / Tattah / Tuttah / Tahtah / Thatta** — the metropolis of Sind and the book's most heavily documented place after Bhit: capital and emporium (pp. 44, 84, 91, 94, 119); "the ruckle of decay that is Tatta" (p. 20, folio 11, fn 1); founded "on a lucky day settled by Brahmans and astrologers" (pp. 174–175, folios 165–166); "such a fortunate moment was the foundation of this place laid that trouble and affliction have never visited its inhabitants" (p. 119, folio 110); sacked and burnt by Portuguese mercenaries in 1555 (p. 43, printed 34); site of a Moghul mint (pp. 54, 150); forty thousand weavers in Nadir Shah's day against twenty thousand souls by Pottinger's (p. 126, folio 117); ruined by 1842 (p. 103, folio 94); Hamilton's claim of "above four hundred colleges", which Sorley refuses — "Even one hundred schQois[text-layer?] , would. ·_have[text-layer?] been �x�essive[text-layer?]" (pp. 220–221, folios 211–212); one of the three centres of learning "famous for tlw[text-layer?] residence of Sayids and holy . men attached to tombs, mosques and shrines" (p. 218, folio 209); indexed at length with the sub-heads "(Sayids and Kazis in) 158, 208, 209" and "(learning in) 2 1 1[text-layer?]" (p. 441). **Two named shrines and a pilgrimage hill stand at Tatta (above) and there is no archive row for the city at all.**
- **Bakhar / Bukkur** — island fortress in the Indus, besieged 1659 (pp. 82–83, 86–89; folios 73–80); head of the overland kafila route (p. 98, folio 89); "five hundred boats could be assembled for hire at Bakhar in a week" (p. 100, folio 91, fn 2); headquarters of the sarkar of Upper Sind (p. 150); granted to Aurangzeb as a fief in November 1649 (p. 151, folio 142). Many Sayid families "traced their first settlements to Tatta, Bakhar and other places 'in the Indus valley" (p. 167, folio 158). **No row.**

'" (p. 97, folio 88, fn 4); silting up by the mid-seventeenth century (p. 98, folio 89). **No row.**
- **Shahbunder, Aurangabunder / Islamgur / Kukrallo, Vikkur, Ghorabari, Shahgur, Vesta Bunder, Novabunder, Jooa Bar, Ali Bunder, Keamari, Son Miani, Darajah** — the delta ports, river posts and fishing stations of the factory records (pp. 47, 54, 58–76, 98, 105, 124–125, 142, 158–159; folios 38, 45, 49–67, 89, 96, 115–116, 133, 149–150). **No rows.**
- **Nasarpur, Kandiaro, Darbelo, Gambat, Sann, Bubak, Mirpur, Mirpur Sakro, Mirpur Bathoro, Shahdadpur, Khudabad, Kashmore, Rajkan, Mundaji, Shaul, Jinger, Khairo Garhi (Garhi Khairo), Lah (near the Runn), Saranne, Difi[text-layer?], Chakar Hala, Trippat, Khohra, Chothiyari, the Nara river, Pali, Sibi, Kohistan, Kotri, Hala Haweli, Muhammad Tur, the pargana of Dirak, Shakapur, Brahmanabad, Mohenjodaro, Chanhudaro, Mansura / Mansurah** — named as revenue units, manufacturing centres, ruins or waypoints, with no shrine, tomb, saint, ʿurs or custodian attached to any (pp. 20, 39–56, 78–79, 98, 105–106, 113, 119, 124–128, 137–151, 177, 181, 225, 247; various folios). **No rows.** **"Difi[text-layer?]" (p. 72, folio 63) is unplaced: the chunk says only that captured "Cooleys" "inhabited near Difi[text-layer?]", with no gloss and no footnote; it may be a corrupted toponym.**

- **Sewistan** — a sarkar and district name (pp. 119, 150, 151, 173, 439). **The book nowhere equates it with Sehwan, and the equation is not made.**
- **FALSE FRIENDS — toponym strings that match an archive row's `location_short` but are demonstrably a different place in this book, and are therefore NOT mapped.** (i) **Shahpiir / Shahpur**: the book's is a Sind village named after the Shah canal dug by Niir Muhammad Kalh6ro (p. 138, folio 129) and a durbar camp at "Shahpiir[text-layer?]" on 28 March 1764 (p. 74); `toponym_map.txt` would send it to `shah-yousuf` (Shah Yousuf, Shahpur, Punjab). (ii) **Mehar**: the book's Mehar is the herdsman of the Suhini cycle, a person (p. 401, folio 392); the map would send it to the two Mehar, Dadu District rows. (iii) **Gujerat "on the eastern bank of the Indus"** (p. 169, folio 160) and "Gujarat" the Mughal province (p. 221, folio 212); the map's "gujrat" is `shrine-of-hazrat-shah-daula-daryai` in Punjab. (iv) **Chak, Radhan, Daharki, Qambar, Dadu** and the other Sindhi settlement names in the map are not in this book at all. **All four cases go to the Doubts worker.**
- **Province and region names the join ruling does not resolve** — **"Sind" / "Sindh"**, **"the Punjab"** (p. 271, folio 262; indexed "(source of Risalo folk-tales) 261, 262", p. 439), **"Baluchistan" / "Balfichistan"[text-layer?]**, **"Lower Sind"**, **"Upper Sind"**, **"Kohistan"**, **"the Deccan"**, **"Hindustan"**. `toponym_map.txt` carries no province-level key, so ruling (a)'s join does not reach them; mapping "Sind" or "the Punjab" would take most of the index at once. **Flagged rather than mapped.**
- **Pilgrimage destinations outside the archive's geography** — **Mecca** (pp. 175, 183, 335; folios 166, 174, 326), named as a voyage's destination and as the pilgrimage the poet did not make; **Medina** (p. 347, folio 338); **Kerbela** and **Kufa / Kiifa[text-layer?] / Kfifa[text-layer?]** (pp. 183, 243, 348–352, 430, 436); **Sinai's mourit[text-layer?]**, in a simile only (p. 362, folio 353); **the pagoda at Tirupati**, reached by "more than 2,000 persons… who came `irom[text-layer?]` the direction of Tatta with their idol which they carried in a rich palanquin" (p. 146, folio 137, fn 2) — **a long-distance Hindu pilgrimage originating from Sind, and outside Pakistan.**
- **The Near-Eastern and European place names of the Sufism chapters** — Damascus, Baghdad, Balkh, Edessa, Wasit, Basra, Mesopotamia, Iraq, Syria, Egypt, Arabia, Persia, Transoxiana, Cairo, Cordova, Samarkand, Bukhara, Shiraz, Herat, Kabul, Kandahar, Khorasan, Turkistan, China, Ceylon, Delhi, Agra, Surat, Bombay, Bengal, Jerusalem, England, Europe (pp. 215–222, 284–291, and passim). **None touches an archive row.**

## Citable passages

Fifteen of the hundred and twenty the chunk notes offer. Every damaged word keeps the
`[text-layer?]` flag the Pass-1 worker attached to it, and every page reference is given in the form
the source used — `(p. N, folio N)` where the printed folio was read off the page, `(p. N, printed N)`
where the chunk notes used that form, `(p. N)` alone where the page printed no number. The book
prints no Sindhi, Persian or Arabic script anywhere, so no original-script form exists to set beside
any of these.

- "the only classic which the language of Sind has yet produced in the realm of deeply imaginative
  literature" (p. 7, folio vii) — Sorley's own valuation of the Risalo, usable wherever an entry needs
  the book's estimate of its subject.
- "the government had a strong theocratic basis with much virtual power in the hands of the priestly
  class of Sayids" (p. 23, folio 14) — the one-sentence statement of the political position of
  Sayids in eighteenth-century Sind, behind any claim about shrine power.
- "His tomb today, perched on its rocky hill at Sukkur, is still a place of veneration and respect"
  (p. 37, printed 28) — Adam Shah Kalhoro's tomb, a living site of veneration that has no archive row;
  good for a future row and for the Kalhora-sanctity argument.
- "The oriental system of markets and fairs on saints' days at saints' tombs was well established"
  (p. 98, folio 89) — the book's flat statement that shrine fairs were an economic institution; the
  single most transferable sentence in the book for ʿurs and mela material.
- "The day is spent in amusements and visits to the shrines." (p. 146, folio 137, quoting the
  *Tarikh-i-Tahiri*) — a seventeenth-century description of pilgrimage practice at Makali, in a
  source's own voice.
- "It was the Kalhora's practice of deferring to the priestly class that led to the extraordinary
  reverence for pirs, Sayids and holy men" (p. 163, folio 154) — the book's causal explanation of
  Sindhi pir-reverence; quotable wherever an entry needs a reason rather than a description.
- "The social religion of Sind was built up round the reverence of the murid for the murshid, and the
  sarn;tity[text-layer?] of the Pir" (pp. 166–167, folios 157–158) — the doctrinal core of the
  pir–murid relation as Sorley states it; note the damaged word sits on "sanctity".
- "MacMurdo estimated the expense of the ecclesiastical establishments at one-third of the gross
  revenue enjoyed by the state" (p. 167, folio 158) — the only quantified estimate of shrine and
  religious-establishment cost in the book; good for shrine economy, attributed as the book
  attributes it.
- "the most notorious of all being the shrine of the Kalandar Shah Lal Baz (or Udero Lal) at Sehwan"
  (p. 168, folio 159) — the sentence that names Sehwan's shrine as the most famous in Sind, and the
  sentence that carries the book's contested Lal Shahbaz = Udero Lal parenthesis (see Doubts).
- "The pir was usually connected with some shrine. He was invested with the pag (turban)." (p. 176,
  folio 167) — the book's definition of what a pir was institutionally, with its one piece of
  investiture vocabulary.
- "the poems have been recited weekly on Fridays in the mausoleum which has become a kind of holy
  shrine which still draws its crowds" (p. 185, folio 176) — the only statement of continuing practice
  at Bhit in the whole book; the load-bearing citation for `bhit-bhit-shah` as a living site.
- "In the numerous qu�tations[text-layer?] from old records … I have almost always retained the
  spelling found in the originals, even though this spelling is more often than not erroneous."
  (p. 203, folio 194) — Appendix VI, Sorley's declared editorial method; quote it wherever an entry
  reproduces one of his odd spellings, and see Doubts for what it does *not* cover.
- "much of tl;le[text-layer?] best music ' and . singing . i�[text-layer?] ·associated[text-layer?]
  with religious observance at t01pbs[text-layer?] and shrines of Muslim saints" (p. 249, folio 240)
  — the link between shrine and musical performance; four damaged words, and the footnote marker on
  this sentence is orphaned (see Doubts).
- "the famous Lal Shahbaz, or Jivelal, the ' living Pir ' whose shrine at Sehwan attracts a great
  gathering of both Mussulmans and Hindus every year in a strange mixture of worship" (p. 257,
  folio 248) — the book's account of the Sehwan ʿurs and its shared Hindu–Muslim attendance; the
  best single sentence for `lal-shahbaz-qalandar`.
- "thousands of Hindus and Muhammadans camped round the t<?mh[text-layer?], mixing freely, and
  singing the Sufi songs that ever melt the heart" (p. 299, folio 290, quoting Jethmal Parsram) — the
  fullest description of a Sindhi ʿurs in the book; the damaged word is "tomb" and sits at the centre
  of the sentence.

## Doubts and review points

Everything below is what a human must check before a fact from this book enters an archive entry.
Page references are PDF page indices, with the printed folio where the notes recorded one; nothing has
been repaired, renumbered or normalised, and no flag has been dropped.

### The `[text-layer?]` damage class — the book's standing inventory (ruling (b))

**The route is `text_layer` and the transcription is damaged throughout.** The dominant damage class
is `[text-layer?]`: **6,421 flagged tokens across the 24 chunk-notes files, roughly 268 per chunk**.
`[OCR?]` and `[illegible…]` each appear 19 times in the notes; **every chunk that addresses the
question states in as many words that no `[OCR?]` and no `[illegible]` marker was left anywhere in
its pages** (chunks 001–003, 005–010, 012, 013, 016, 019–021, 024 all say so explicitly). That is a
fact about the route, which produces no transcriber marks, **not a statement that the text is sound**:
every damaged string inventoried in the chunk notes was found by reading, not by flag, and chunk 013
adds that in a chunk four-fifths tables and figures "the absence of transcriber flags is actively
misleading". Chunk 024 goes further for the index: "a lost digit inside a page reference leaves a
perfectly plausible-looking number". **A reviewer must assume more damage than is listed.**

**This damage is NOT a total-and-mechanical mapping** — it is not a ligature substitution and cannot
be reversed token by token, because the same source letter goes to different substitutes on different
pages and the faults run in both directions (letters become digits and digits become letters). Ruling
(b)'s exemption therefore does **not** apply: every flagged token keeps its inline flag in the
archive, and the class is inventoried here once. **No flag may be dropped from any word carried out of
this book.**

The observed shapes, which are the classes a reviewer will meet:

- **Broken words mid-token.** `RisaJ.o`, `successfally` (p. 7), `fori:n` (p. 7), `rust!c` (p. 9),
  `wor�`, `satisfacforily` (p. 8), `diffa:ulties` (p. 7), `paudty` for paucity (p. 14), `b!oken`
  (p. 20), `foun�l` (p. 19), `fesult` (p. 18), `bwing` (p. 16), `insta:aee` (p. 14), `Isla.in`
  (p. 21), `l\foghul` / `l\.foghul` (pp. 17, 23), `Srnn` for SIND (p. 14), `spul-wrapt` (p. 9).
- **Stray full stops and mid-word punctuation inserted by the scan.** `in-the first half`,
  `are. known`, `possibl.e` (p. 54), `convent.ional` (p. 26), `find·the` (p. 27), `pla,ce` and `a,nd`
  (p. 55), `s.anction` (p. 61), `.42r` with an intrusive leading stop (p. 430, folio 421).
- **Spaced-out display type in running heads.** `S H A H  A B D U L  L A T I F  O F  B H I T` and
  hundreds of variants; the head is damaged on nearly every page of every chunk and **is not a title
  authority for this book**. Word spacing is broken in body type too — `Se a power`, `t rib ut e`,
  `ambit ious`, `isolat ion`, `ne ighbours`, `j u dgements` (chunk 001), and the systematic space
  after `j` (`j ourney`, `j oin`, `Raj a`, `Punj ab`, `subj ect`), which chunk 022 calls the single
  most frequent defect in its pages.
- **Three-digit numbers split by a space, the corpus-wide default.** `"41 7"` (p. 426), `"4 15"`
  (p. 424), and with them `39 6` (p. 405), `4 04` (p. 413), `40 9` (p. 418), `4 12` (p. 421), `4 16`
  (p. 425), `4 18` (p. 427), `1 87` (p. 196), `1 89` (p. 198), `1 95` (p. 204), `2 70` (p. 279),
  `2 82` (p. 291), `2 90` (p. 299), `2 97` (p. 306), `3 04` (p. 313), `3 06` (p. 315), `34 r`
  (p. 350) and the thirty-plus broken figures in the Index of Opening Lines (pp. 310–313, folios
  301–304). **27 occurrences of this shape are counted for this book; it is expected, not a surprise.**
- **`H` → `R` in the title word.** `BRIT` / `B R I T` for `BHIT` in the running head, on pp. 15, 17,
  20, 23, 25, 54, 57, 62, 67, 71, 72, 80, 83, 84, 91, 108, 134, 145, 147, 149, 153, 156, 162, 166,
  168, 175, 182, 193, 197, 215, 217, 219, 220, 232, 237, 239, 240, 244, 246, 253, 256, 259, 260, 262,
  263, 268, 270, 271, 275, 278, 280, 291, 296, 300, 302, 311, 313, 315, 323, 333, 336, 337, 338, 345,
  365, 373, 374, 376, 389, 403, 406, 413, 418, 429. **This is a letter substitution, not a variant
  spelling: a search of this book for "Bhit" will miss pages.** Chunk 021 found the same fault in the
  *body* text — `Brit[text-layer?]` for "But" (p. 354, folio 345) — which proves it is a text-layer
  behaviour and not the head's letterspacing. New head forms recorded late: `:f3 H I T` (p. 249),
  `BHTT` (p. 409), `ilH I T` (p. 347), `B H.lT` (p. 361), `B HJ. T` (p. 356).
- **Long vowels destroyed in transliterated names — one mechanical fault, hundreds of instances, and
  Appendix VI does not license it.** `ii` for a macroned vowel (`Siifi`, `Talpiir`, `Baliichi`,
  `Shikarpiir`, `Nasarpiir`, `Khairpiir`, `Niir`, `Masiimi`, `Arghiin`, `Rajpiits`, `Punhiin`,
  `Bhambh6r`, `Maiziiri`, `Fariiki`, `Daiidpotras`, `Mussalmiin`, `huziirs`, `giir`); `6` or capital
  `O` for an accented o (`Kalh6ra`, `KalhOro`, `Umark6t`, `Jat6is`, `Khah6ri`, `Girnir`); and
  irregular substitutes `fi`, `fl`, `U.`, `f`, `2` (`Shikarpfir`, `Balfichistan`, `BalU.chi`,
  `B2liichi`, `Jamf`, `MARUf`, `SUHINf`). **Chunk 013 warns explicitly that Appendix VI (p. 203,
  folio 194) covers only five consonant classes — "the various forms of the ' h ', ' s ', ' t ', ' d '
  and z sounds" — and says nothing about vowel length, so none of these forms is the author's choice
  and none may be cited as his transliteration.** Chunk 001 adds that the pronunciation key (p. 6)
  has lost its diacritics entirely and is unusable.
- **Replacement characters (`�`) and Greek/Cyrillic substitutions.** Pervasive; heaviest on
  pp. 266–275 (chunk 017 names pp. 267, 272, 273, 275 as its four worst pages) and pp. 221–222
  (chunk 014's worst). Examples: `wor�`, `D.Li�t.` (p. 2), `t'�1e` (p. 5), `Bµt` (p. 266),
  `co:µtaining` (p. 267), `l\4ystidsm` (p. 274), `l\funtakhah` (p. 268).
- **Digits and letters exchanged in both directions.** `1` → `I`, `l`, `r`, `i`, `!`, `n`, `J`;
  `0` → `o`, `Q`, `°`; `11` → `n` or `u`; `2` → `z`; `5` → `S` or `L`; `8` → `S`; `6` → `q`; and the
  reverse — `d` → `4` (`pierce4`, `Belov' 4`, `fortnig4t's`, `Arrange4�`), `o` → `6` and `d` → `4`
  together in `4o6r` for "door" (p. 339, folio 330, where the undamaged word prints four words later),
  `l` → `1` (`1ast`, `Fitzgera1d`, `Bija1`, `appea1`, `Moghu1`, `Al1`, `1ove`, `h111s`, `sc111b`),
  `5` inside a word (`fill5!d`, p. 363). **Chunk 015 states the finding plainly: "a figure in this
  book cannot be trusted even when it looks like a plain word", and the `1`-for-`l` substitution
  inside a proper name is the most dangerous class in the book because it is invisible at a glance.**
- **The fraction `½` destroyed into `!`, `l`, `i` or `j`.** `in breadth ! coveds`, `paies ! pies`,
  `41! rupees`, `2! per cent`, `l rupee to the town`, `freight to Tatta i rupee` (chunk 003);
  `14j` and `12!` yards, `! of a rupee` (chunk 004); `12!d.` (p. 106); `Rs. 3 or 3!` (p. 130);
  `4i`, `3i`, `6i`, `I 3i` (p. 199, folio 190). **Confirmed as a class in chunk 013; in chunks 012,
  016, 018, 019, 020, 021 and 024 no fraction appears at all, so the class could not be tested there
  — absence of the fault in those chunks is not evidence of soundness.**

### The printed-page offset: printed = PDF − 9, and where it does not hold

**The notes establish printed page = PDF page − 9, holding unbroken across the body from PDF p. 12**,
verified chunk by chunk (chunk 012: printed 164 at PDF p. 173 through printed 176 at PDF p. 185;
chunk 013: printed 180 at PDF p. 189 through 202 at PDF p. 211, and "the numbering does not reset at
Part II"; chunk 016: printed 234 at PDF p. 243 through 249 at PDF p. 258; chunk 017: held on all
fifteen numbered pages, "Part II does not renumber"; chunk 018: 267 at p. 276 through 282 at p. 291;
chunk 019: 283 at p. 292 through 307 at p. 316, and neither `BOOK III` nor the start of the
translation reset it; chunk 021: 339 at p. 348 through 363 at p. 372, `PART III` did not reset it;
chunk 022: held on all twenty-seven pages). **The last page on which it holds is PDF p. 441 → printed
432** (chunk 024). Chunk 024 also supplies an independent confirmation from inside the book: the
index places the influences chapter at printed 236–251 and the Baluchi section at 246–249 (p. 439),
and chunk 016 read that chapter at PDF pp. 245–258 = folios 236–249 — **the two agree exactly.**
Chunk 024 adds the caveat that this **does not verify the offset for PDF pp. 292–426**, and that any
index folio between 283 and 417 must be converted only after chunks 019–023 confirm it.

**The offset does NOT apply to the roman-numbered front matter, which is out of sequence in this
scan.** The acknowledgements page carries printed **ix** at PDF p. 5, while the Preface carries
printed **vii** at PDF p. 7 and **viii** at PDF p. 8 — **so ix precedes vii in the PDF**. Either the
scan reorders the preliminaries or pages are missing between them. **The −9 offset must not be
extended backward into the front matter** (p. 5, pp. 7–8). The citable-passage form `(p. 7, folio vii)`
is the notes' own record of a printed roman folio, not a computed one.

**Do not compute a folio the notes did not print.** Every chunk states this as a rule for its own
unnumbered pages, and chunk 010 says it most sharply of PDF p. 150: "Its folio would be 141 on the
arithmetic, but per the convention for this book no folio has been predicted for it… If a later
worker or the consolidation pass adds 'folio 141' there, that is an invention."

**Pages that print no number, cited by PDF index only, with no folio predicted:** pp. 49, 65, 67, 73,
74, 150, 172, 186, 187, 205, 206, 207, 245, 261, 304, 305, 308, 309, 314, 318, 319, 370, 411, 416,
422, 433, 434, 435, 436, 437, 438, 442. **Pages with no running head but a drop folio at the foot,
which IS used:** p. 118 (folio 109), p. 272 (263), p. 284 (`�75[text-layer?]`), p. 298 (289), p. 306
(`2 97[text-layer?]`), p. 310 (301), p. 320 (`3II[text-layer?]`), p. 342 (333), p. 368 (`35 9`),
p. 401 (392). **Chunk 023 flags an inconsistency here a human must settle: pp. 411, 416 and 422 are
cycle-opening pages that print no folio, while p. 401, an otherwise identical cycle-opening page,
prints `392` at the foot** — either the book's practice is inconsistent or the text layer dropped
three page feet and kept one.

**Printer's gathering signatures that are NOT page numbers and have not been read as any kind of
number:** a stray `2` at the foot of p. 26; `4` at p. 58; `5` at p. 74; `7` at p. 106; `8` at p. 122;
`10` at p. 154; `13` at p. 202; `16` at p. 250; `] !)[text-layer?]` beside the folio at p. 298; `17`
at p. 266; `18` at p. 282; `20` at p. 314; `21` at p. 330; `22` at p. 346; `23` at p. 362; `26` at
p. 410; `27` at p. 426. Chunks 019 and 021 record an arithmetic that would make these sixteen-page
gathering marks — **"THIS IS AN INFERENCE FROM ARITHMETIC ON THREE PRINTED STRINGS AND IS NOT
ANYTHING THE BOOK SAYS"** — and no citation anywhere depends on it. Likewise the stray `I` at p. 172,
the stray `I` under the running head at p. 281, the stray `r` under the head at p. 247, the stray `J :![text-layer?]`
at the foot of p. 186 and the stray `0` mid-sentence at p. 286 have not been read as numbers.

"Numerals are the least reliable thing in this text layer, and no date from this chunk should enter an
entry unchecked." Every item below carries its flag and its page.

  be supplied for it.**

### Flagged money, measurement and quantity figures

- **`.Rs. 31 per annum` for the artisans' poll tax (p. 157, folio 148) is "the single most
  quotable-and-wrong figure in the chunk".** Two reasons internal to the book: the general *sar
  shumari* is "one rupee per head per annum" (p. 156, folio 147) and the Larkana paper-makers' tax is
  "Rs . 8-12-0 per annum" (p. 158, folio 149, footnote 1), so Rs 31 is an order of magnitude out of
  line; and this text layer destroys `½` into `!`, `l` or `i`, so **Rs. 3½ is the reading the
  surrounding figures support.** Carried exactly as printed; a human must check it against the PDF.
- `Rs. 13-10-9 per cent` (p. 159, folio 150) — printed as an annas-and-pies amount governed by "per
  cent" and internally odd; the sentence sets it against a consignment "of a value of one hundred
  rupees".
- **The `Rezki` percentages do not cohere** (p. 158, folio 149): "an anna per rupee" (= 6.25 per
  cent) against a stated `5'[text-layer?]` per cent and 4 per cent, with `roo[text-layer?]` for 100.
  Left unreconciled, as the book leaves it.
- **`rm persons` (p. 131, folio 122) — "the worst single case and it is a total, not a detail".** The
  itemised list "r amildar, r mehta, 6 j emadars, roo sepoys, r trumpeter and r masalchi" would sum
  to 110 if every `r` is 1, "but that arithmetic is the reader's, not the book's".
- **Three money figures in one indigo sentence are unrecoverable (p. 106, folio 97):** "at 18d., and
  the Ahmedabad one at `gd.[text-layer?]` per pound, but the former was invoiced at
  `12!d.[text-layer?]` per pound at Surat `ill t66o[text-layer?]`". Only "18d." survives clean; **the
  price comparison the sentence exists to make cannot be read off the text layer.**
- `£50 per annuin` (p. 112) and `cotton yam, 4001250 bales` (p. 114, **where the separator has been
  eaten and the true figure is unrecoverable**) — the other two damaged body figures of chunk 008.
- **The Ain-i-Akbari assessment figures at p. 150 "are internally corrupt and must not be used":**
  mahals `1 8, I I, 9, 7` and 8; dams `29,999,99 1, u, 784, 586, 15, 546,808, 7,835,600` and
  5,085,048 — five divisions but the run does not resolve into five figures, and `u,` is a letter in
  a numeral position. Sorley himself says "It is, however, not easy to make sense of these figures".
- The exchange rate `i20 current to lOO silver` and `145· current to roo silver` (p. 62, folio 53)
  — "almost certainly 120:100 and 145:100, but the note keeps them as printed".
- Appendix III/IV figures with punctuation substituted inside a number: `25�000`, `22;000`,
  `4:2;000`, `18;000`, `4;696`, `6,f5 7`, `.n,892`, `22_3`, `2 5,900` (pp. 198–199); `28,ooo`,
  `I6,ooo` (p. 198); `2,5qo`, `3q,964` (p. 199); `47°`, `�.47°`, `192°8`.
- **The seven column headings of the `Marine Force for Service of Bombay (or Surat)` table are
  destroyed and the figures are therefore unlabelled** (p. 196, folio 187) — surviving fragments

### Flagged personal names

- **The Kalhora dynasty's name appears in at least nine damaged forms** — `Kalh6ro`, `Kalh6ra`,
  `Kalhora`, `Kalhorn`, `KalhOra`, `KalhOro`, `KalhOrn`, `Kalli6ro`, `Kalh5ra` — across pp. 8, 13,
  18, 19, 23, 25, 30, 35, 38–41, 46–48, 57–61, 65, 71–77, 101, 103, 104, 110, 111, 116, 118, 120,
  121, 123, 124, 126, 127, 129, 130, 132, 135, 138, 141–185, 217, 223, 226, 255, 436, 438, 439.
  **This is one mechanical fault (ō → 6 / O / rn), not nine variant forms used by the author, and
  none has been normalised. `Kalhorn` is text-layer damage, not a variant spelling. The five (later
  nine) forms must be treated as one name, and the damaged forms must not travel into an entry.**

  KalhOro[text-layer?]` (p. 104, folio 95).

- `Shaf'aat Ahmad Khan` (p. 114, footnote 1) against `Sha'aaft Ahmad Khan` (p. 115, footnote 1) —

### Flagged place names

- **`Taha` (p. 21) "is very likely the Tatta named twice elsewhere in the chunk (pp. 20, 22), but the
  chunk prints `Taha` and I have not corrected it. Where Muhammad Bin Tughlak died is therefore as
  printed, not as resolved."**
- `Duabo` (p. 19) — the chunk's spelling of the second battle of 1843, paired with Miani; not
  corrected.
- **"Alor" versus the archive's "Aror" (p. 20, footnote 1) — "a spelling question I am not
  resolving."** Chunk 024 adds "Alor, I m[text-layer?], 238" (p. 432) and notes that chunk 016's
  `included.Alar[text-layer?]` sits at the same folio 238 in the same context: **"the index prints
  `Alor`, the running text printed `Alar`[text-layer?], and a human should settle which is the book's
  form."**
- `Sewanna` (p. 56, folio 47) and `Sarkar Sovistan` (p. 64, folio 55) — **"both plausibly denote
  Sehwan… but the identification is mine and not the book's, and in any case the references are to a
  police post and a revenue division, not to a shrine."**
- `Halla chand` (p. 65), Ghulam Shah's "future residence" — **"could be Hala… but the book does not
  say so and prints it as two words."** `Chakar Hala` (p. 150, footnote 1) — **"the printed form is
  'Chakar Hala', a revenue division, not 'Hala', and the chunk makes no connection."**
- `Siccarpore` / `Sicc.a Poer` (pp. 62, 66, folios 53, 57); `Shikarpiir` / `Shikarpfir` /
  `Sliikarpiir` / `Shikarpoor` / `Shikarpore`.
- `Difi[text-layer?]` (p. 72, folio 63) — **"unplaced… Could be a corrupted toponym; the chunk does
  not say."**
- `Laribunder` (p. 77, folio 68) against `Latibunder[text-layer?]` (p. 79, folio 70) — **"the same

- `Islamgur/ Aurangabunder/` (p. 69, folio 60) written as one place with two names inside a quoted

  differently and the chunk never says whether they are the same place" — (not stated). Do not
  conflate them.**
- **"Hala Haweli" (pp. 180, 181; folios 171, 172) is "a *different*, ruined village and must not be
  conflated with Hala"** (p. 182, folio 173).
- **"Dhat" is used in two cycles and the book's identification covers only one.** Rano "lives in
  Dhat, which appears to be another name for Ludh6[text-layer?]" (p. 411), but Marui, whose home is
  Malir, says "From the clothes that in Dhat I had worn" (p. 424, folio "4 15"). **"Either Dhat is a
  wider region containing both, or the two are different places, or Sorley's equation is wrong. The
  chunk does not say — (not stated)."**
- **Seventeenth-century spellings must not be mistaken for scan damage, and vice versa.** The quoted
  factory letters print "clotheing", "wieght", "Ophium", "henns", "citty", "indicoe", "baftaes",
  "joories", "alejaes", "corge", "rebbells", "occations", "ratebooke" — **the documents' own
  orthography, not flagged.** Where the classes collide — "Nursapoore" vs "Nusarpiir", "Derbellah" vs
  "Darbelo" vs "Dorbello" vs "Derbella", "Khandierah" vs "Khandearah" vs "Kandiaro", "Bussara" vs
  "Bussora" — **"a human must decide case by case."**

### Flagged titles, technical terms and sur names

- **The Sur titles "are the archive's likely harvest from this chunk and every one of them should be
  checked against the PDF": "Sur Kedaro", "Sur Lilan Chanesar", "Sur Kapati" (p. 243), "Sur Sorath"
  (p. 250), "Sur Dahir", "Sur Rip", "Sur Asa" (p. 254). "None carries a `[text-layer?]` flag because
  none shows visible damage — but this book loses single letters silently, the titles are short, and
  'Sur Rip' and 'Sur Asa' in particular are short enough that a lost letter would leave a
  plausible-looking word."** The same warning is repeated for the path-stage names "nasut",
  "tarikat", "aruf", "hakikat" (pp. 292–293, folios 283–284) and for "Pokardas" (p. 317, folio 308).
- The index's `Surs of the Risalo` block (p. 440): `(Sasui ..\bri[text-layer?])` — **"a backslash
  inside a word, and the second element is unreadable"**; `(Maiziiri[text-layer?])`;
  `(Barvo Sindhi:[text-layer?])`; `(Sa.rang[text-layer?])`; `(Momul Ra.no[text-layer?])`;
  `(Yaman Kalya.n[text-layer?])`; `(Sur A sa[text-layer?])`; `(Suhini}[text-layer?]`. **"Of the
  twenty-one titles, only Dahir, Desi, Husaini, Kalyan, Kapati, Kedaro, Kohiari, Lilan Chanesar,
  Marui, Ramkali, Rip, Samundi and Sorath print without visible damage — and this book loses single
  letters silently, so even those should be checked."** Their page figures are as fragile:
  `(Kedaro) 1 74[text-layer?], 234`; `(Sorath) 2 19[text-layer?], 24I[text-layer?], 262, 271`.
- `Sasui .Abri[text-layer?]` (p. 279, folio 270) and "Sasui Abri" (p. 231, folio 222) — **"printed as
  two words and may be one poem or a compound title. The chunk does not say. (not stated)"**, and the

- **`Sur Barvo Sindhi` — "one title or two words? It is not resolvable from the sentence whether
  'Sindhi' belongs to the title or is a stray qualifier"** (p. 279, folio 270).
- `Khahqri` (pp. 231–232, folios 222–223) — **"the `q` is suspicious… it is one of the chunk's

- `Malakus` (p. 231, folio 222) — **"the form is unusual and may be damaged. Flagged; not repaired."**
- **`kami (amatory)` (pp. 146, 229; folios 137, 220) — the Ain-i-Akbari's word for the music of Sind.

- **"Bharava" (p. 231, folio 222) against "Raga Bhairava" (p. 230, folio 221)** — the same mode name

- **"bina" (p. 228, folio 219) against "biria, or vina" (p. 232, folio 223)** — "the same instrument

### The footnote apparatus

- **"Footnote markers are frequently fused to the preceding word or number… so at least two footnote
  calls above may be mis-attached"** (chunk 003): "in Sind I7581", "reign. 1", "employ.2". The same
  fault recurs as `Burnes3 in 1828` (p. 126), `Pelseart2` (p. 134), `here3` (p. 129), `F ariiki2`
  (p. 130), `Akbar4` (p. 130), `Surat6in r780` (p. 131), `diet.3` and `'4, 5` (p. 131), `coinage. i`
  (p. 133), `wheel, z with` (p. 139), `khasas-'r` (p. 140), `contaminated1` (p. 104), `trade1`
  (chunk 005). **"Attribution of a figure to a particular source should be checked against the PDF
  wherever a marker is involved."**
- **Footnote numbering breaks on p. 108 (folio 99):** the body carries 1, 2 and then **4**, while the
  printed footnotes are 1, 2 and 3. **"the Burton citation 'History of Sindh, pp. 240-I[text-layer?].'
  cannot be confidently attached to the rhinoceros-hide sentence."**
- **A footnote marker points to the wrong note on p. 124:** Von Orlich's quotation carries `2`, but
  footnote 2 is the Masson note on Shikarpur. **"No source is given anywhere in the chunk for the Von
  Orlich quotation, and the year '1842' is supplied only in Sorley's introductory clause."**
- **Scrambled numbering on pp. 158–159:** the Carless quotation is followed by `'4`, the
  thirteen-cesses quotation by `'3`, while the page's footnote block is numbered 1, 2, 3.
  **"the pairing of the Carless quotation to a specific source is (not stated) on this evidence."**
- **Orphaned markers with no footnote.** A bare `1` four lines above the wrong sentence at p. 175
  (folio 166), with the note "Gurbuxani : Shah jo Risa..lo[text-layer?], I, p. 50." at the foot —
  **"The marker's true anchor is the Sunni/Shia sentence earlier on the page, and the attribution
  above assumes that."** The same at p. 180 (folio 171), p. 249 (**"No footnote text prints on p. 249
  to match it… The quoted sentence is the chunk's most important single statement for the archive"**),
  and p. 280 (folio 271), where "' God who is One no rival ·hath. 1[text-layer?]" trails a marker and
  **"p. 280 carries no footnote at all… Either the marker is spurious text-layer debris or the
  footnote is lost."** Markers displaced across a line break at pp. 283 and 288.
- **Footnote numbering out of reading order on p. 256 (folio 247):** the reference marked `2` occurs
  well above the reference marked `1`. **"The attributions given above follow the marker numbers, not
  the reading order; a human should confirm them."**
- **`pp. 71-81` (p. 111) against `pp. TE-81` (p. 113) for what is evidently the same Sarkar
  reference — "the two disagree and the chunk does not reconcile them."**
- **The same page number, `p. 344`, is cited for two different Pottinger quotations** (p. 126,
  folio 117, footnote 4, the Tatta weavers; p. 127, folio 118, footnote 1, the Karachi
  revenue-farming), **"and the title itself is destroyed differently in each"** —
  `Travels in Beloochistiin and Sinde` against `Travels in Belooi"l!-istiin[text-layer?] and Sinde`.
  **"One of the two is likely wrong."**
- **`p. 42` then `ibid. , p. 3.` under one source** (p. 185, folio 176) — **"A move from p. 42 back
  to p. 3 under 'ibid.' is possible but odd; given this chunk's numeral damage, check both."**
- `Postans : op. cit., p. 284.` (p. 60, folio 51) — a short-form citation **"whose full reference is
  not in this chunk, so the parwana's terms are quoted here at one remove."**
- **"No footnote appears on any page of chunk 021" (pp. 348–372) — "twenty-five consecutive pages
  carry none. A human should confirm that the footnotes are absent from the printed pages rather than
  lost by the extractor."**
- `Elliot (I. 4[text-layer?])` (p. 270, folio 261) against `Elliot (I, 491)` (p. 174, folio 165) for
  the same claim — **"Whether `I. 4` is a different reference or a text-layer truncation of the same

  4jo4[text-layer?])` (p. 247, folio 238) — **"'4jo4' is not a page number in any reading… None of

- **Out-of-order and implausible index ranges (p. 438 and around).** `privileged classes, 144,
  I34-62[text-layer?]` — **"A range starting at 134 is 23 pages longer than any of them.
  `I34[text-layer?]` is very likely a damaged `154` or `157`. Do not cite this range."**
